# Signal MD mentor-page drafts: topics 1-500 (corrected)

Rebuilt from fresh Europe PMC evidence. Replaces `topics-0001-0250.json` and `topics-0251-0500.json`, which cited mismatched PMIDs. Every cited PMID was checked to exist with a matching title (3,549 citations, 0 mismatches). Status is `draft_pending_clinician_review` unless marked `needs_evidence`. Nothing is loaded to the database.

## Cluster `aa_amyloidosis` (topics 1) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** AA amyloidosis is a complication of sustained inflammation, so control of the underlying disease is the treatment. Autoinflammatory syndromes (FMF, CAPS, TRAPS), uncontrolled adult-onset Still's disease and injecting-related soft-tissue infection are recognised causes.

**Teaching points**
- A systematic review of causes listed 48 disease associations as strong, 19 weak, 23 unclear and 60 unlikely, and proposed a pragmatic diagnostic approach for AA amyloidosis without an obvious underlying disease. [PMID 31766892]
- In familial Mediterranean fever, IL-1 inhibitors are effective and relatively safe for colchicine non-responders, and tocilizumab might be of interest in selected patients with established AA amyloidosis resistant to colchicine and IL-1 inhibitors. [PMID 40562663, 38950176]
- In cryopyrin-associated periodic syndrome, AA amyloidosis occurred in nearly all subtypes; deaths were in patients who never received IL-1 inhibitors, and 89% of those treated had a greater than 50% fall in proteinuria. [PMID 35262642]
- TRAPS is revealed by AA amyloidosis in most cases, so clinical features of TRAPS should be screened for in AA patients, and IL-1 antagonists can preserve renal function. [PMID 33715002]
- AA amyloidosis is a rare, severe complication of uncontrolled active adult-onset Still's disease that could be prevented by early diagnosis and better disease control, including more frequent use of biologic therapy. [PMID 31488308]
- There is strong evidence linking skin and soft-tissue infections to AA amyloidosis among people who inject drugs. [PMID 29697879]

**Uncertainties**
- Optimal treatment of AA amyloidosis secondary to chronic arthritis is not covered by the retrieved excerpts.

**MCQ angles**
- Treat the underlying inflammatory disease.
- IL-1 blockade in autoinflammatory causes.

**Case hooks**
- Long-standing inflammatory arthritis with new nephrotic-range proteinuria.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Rheumatoid arthritis and juvenile arthritis as causes, diagnosis (biopsy, SAA scintigraphy) and monitoring by SAA levels are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 29697879: Drawing attention to a neglected injecting-related harm: a systematic review of AA amyloidosis among people who inject drugs (systematic review, 2018)
- PMID 31488308: AA amyloidosis secondary to adult onset Still's disease: About 19 cases (systematic review, 2020)
- PMID 31766892: Causes of AA amyloidosis: a systematic review (systematic review, 2020)
- PMID 33715002: Tumour necrosis factor receptor-1 associated periodic syndrome (TRAPS)-related AA amyloidosis: a national case series and systematic review (systematic review, 2021)
- PMID 35262642: AA amyloidosis complicating cryopyrin-associated periodic syndrome: a study of 86 cases including 23 French patients and systematic review (systematic review, 2022)
- PMID 38950176: Could tocilizumab be used in familial Mediterranean fever? A systematic review (systematic review, 2025)
- PMID 40562663: Efficacy and safety of treatments in familial Mediterranean fever and its complications: a systematic review informing the EULAR/PReS recommendations for familial Mediterranean fever (guideline, 2025)

## Cluster `abatacept_ra` (topics 2) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Abatacept (T-cell co-stimulation blockade) is one of several biologic options in RA. Evidence suggests it is a plausible choice in RA-associated interstitial lung disease, and weight or BMI may influence response to some biologics.

**Teaching points**
- The EULAR 2022 and 2025 systematic reviews of DMARD efficacy informed the update of the RA management recommendations, and strategic and head-to-head studies helped refine the established treatment algorithm. [PMID 36368906, 41997804]
- Current evidence suggests abatacept may be a plausible alternative for RA patients with interstitial lung disease, with a lower relative risk of ILD deterioration at 24 months than TNF inhibitors and csDMARDs and no unexpected adverse events. [PMID 33887489]
- Janus kinase inhibitors in RA-ILD were well tolerated and might offer comparable safety and efficacy to abatacept and rituximab. [PMID 39270812]
- Weight and BMI should be considered when planning treatment, with the effect more pronounced for TNF inhibitors than for other biologics; similar evidence exists for tocilizumab and abatacept. [PMID 37150489]
- A meta-analysis found no difference in cardiovascular outcomes between abatacept and TNF inhibitors (OR 1.08), while tocilizumab may be associated with a lower MACE risk than TNF inhibitors. [PMID 30875456]
- Early treatment of at-risk individuals may delay RA onset, though whether it prevents RA remains undetermined. [PMID 36280095]

**Uncertainties**
- Prospective trials of abatacept in RA-ILD are lacking.

**MCQ angles**
- Abatacept in RA with interstitial lung disease.
- Seropositivity and response (not in retrieved excerpts).

**Case hooks**
- Seropositive RA with basal fibrosis on CT and failure of methotrexate.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Mechanism details, seropositivity and ACPA predictors of response, dosing and infection risk are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 30875456: Comparative Risk of Cardiovascular Events With Biologic and Synthetic Disease-Modifying Antirheumatic Drugs in Patients With Rheumatoid Arthritis: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis (meta-analysis, 2020)
- PMID 33887489: Efficacy and safety of abatacept in interstitial lung disease of rheumatoid arthritis: A systematic literature review (systematic review, 2021)
- PMID 36280095: Prevention of rheumatoid arthritis: A systematic literature review of preventive strategies in at-risk individuals (systematic review, 2023)
- PMID 36368906: Efficacy of synthetic and biological DMARDs: a systematic literature review informing the 2022 update of the EULAR recommendations for the management of rheumatoid arthritis (guideline, 2023)
- PMID 37150489: Effect of body mass index on treatment response of biologic/targeted-synthetic DMARDs in patients with rheumatoid arthritis, psoriatic arthritis or axial spondyloarthritis. A systematic review (systematic review, 2023)
- PMID 39270812: Janus kinase inhibitors in rheumatoid arthritis-associated interstitial lung disease: A systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2024)
- PMID 41997804: Efficacy of synthetic and biological DMARDs: a systematic literature review informing the 2025 update of the EULAR recommendations for the management of rheumatoid arthritis (guideline, 2026)

## Cluster `aaa` (topics 3) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Retrieved AAA evidence covers detection and risk rather than the surveillance-threshold question: point-of-care ultrasound is highly accurate, smoking carries twice the relative risk in women, and advanced EVAR has better short-term but not better long-term outcomes than open repair.

**Teaching points**
- Point-of-care ultrasound by emergency physicians is highly accurate for AAA (pooled sensitivity 98.33%, specificity 99.84%), even with limited training. [PMID 39344280]
- The relative risk of AAA among current smokers is almost twice as high in women as in men, with no strong association with inflammatory markers. [PMID 40336481]
- Obesity is positively but nonlinearly correlated with AAA presence, and BMI relates to postoperative mortality in a U-shaped curve. [PMID 38320094]
- Metformin use hints at a potential role in limiting annual AAA growth, events and risk. [PMID 38239055]
- Women undergoing EVAR experience higher perioperative morbidity and less favourable long-term outcomes than men, including after rupture (8-year survival 36.7% versus 49.5%). [PMID 41923746]
- Advanced (fenestrated) EVAR had better short-term outcomes than open surgery for complex AAA but higher long-term mortality (RR 1.46). [PMID 38887046]
- ACR Appropriateness Criteria address screening for abdominal aortic aneurysm. [PMID 38823950]

**Uncertainties**
- Optimal repair choice for complex aneurysms.

**MCQ angles**
- Women have worse outcomes after EVAR.
- POCUS for AAA.

**Case hooks**
- Male smoker aged 70 with a 5.2 cm AAA on screening ultrasound.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Surveillance intervals, size thresholds for repair (5.5 cm in men), EVAR versus open indications and screening eligibility are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 38239055: Association of metformin use with abdominal aortic aneurysm: A systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2024)
- PMID 38320094: Body mass index and the risk of abdominal aortic aneurysm presence and postoperative mortality: a systematic review and dose-response meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2024)
- PMID 38823950: ACR Appropriateness Criteria® Screening for Abdominal Aortic Aneurysm (guideline, 2024)
- PMID 38887046: Outcomes of advanced EVAR versus open surgery in the management of complex abdominal aortic aneurysm repair: A systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2024)
- PMID 39344280: Enhancing clinical outcomes: Point of care ultrasound in the precision diagnosis and Management of Abdominal Aortic Aneurysms in emergency medicine: A systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2025)
- PMID 40336481: Sex-Specific Risk of Smoking for Abdominal Aortic Aneurysm and Exploration of Potential Mechanism: Meta-Analysis and Prospective Cohort Study (meta-analysis, 2025)
- PMID 41923746: Sex Differences in Outcomes After Endovascular Abdominal Aortic Aneurysm Repair: A Systematic Review and Narrative Synthesis (systematic review, 2026)

## Cluster `acs_abdominal_compartment` (topics 4) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Intra-abdominal hypertension is common in critical illness, raises acute kidney injury risk and predicts mortality, including in severe pancreatitis. Consensus guidance stresses recognition, fluid and organ support, and surgical decompression for established compartment syndrome.

**Teaching points**
- Intra-abdominal hypertension is common in critically ill patients and may be associated with higher mortality, especially at higher grades; screening and treatment effects need prospective research. [PMID 31808368]
- Intra-abdominal hypertension was associated with increased acute kidney injury risk (OR 2.57), independent of age, BMI, burns or cardiac surgery. [PMID 34053324]
- In severe acute pancreatitis, intra-abdominal hypertension is a strong predictor of mortality and respiratory failure even without abdominal compartment syndrome. [PMID 39704902]
- An emergency expert consensus stresses fluid resuscitation, organ support and preventive measures for IAH/ACS, with nutritional therapy and prevention strategies. [PMID 38845489]
- An evidence-based guideline for abdominal compartment syndrome in burns covers early detection, assessment and medical, surgical and postoperative management. [PMID 34016932]
- A simplified burn resuscitation protocol without high-dose ascorbic acid and with a lower starting fluid rate led to lower 24-hour fluid volumes without more adverse events. [PMID 38824401]
- The effect of damage-control surgery on mortality in nontraumatic abdominal emergencies remains unclear. [PMID 34882591]

**Uncertainties**
- Whether screening for IAH changes outcomes.

**MCQ angles**
- IAH raises AKI risk.
- Over-resuscitation as a trigger.

**Case hooks**
- Severe pancreatitis with rising bladder pressure and oliguria.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** WSACS definitions and grading, bladder pressure measurement technique and decompressive laparotomy indications are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 31808368: Incidence of Intra-Abdominal Hypertension and Abdominal Compartment Syndrome: A Systematic Review (systematic review, 2021)
- PMID 34016932: Abdominal compartment syndrome in burns patients: Introduction of an evidence-based management guideline and algorithm (guideline, 2021)
- PMID 34053324: Intra-abdominal hypertension and increased acute kidney injury risk: a systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2021)
- PMID 34882591: Damage-control surgery in patients with nontraumatic abdominal emergencies: A systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2022)
- PMID 38824401: Revision of an Adult Burn Center's Resuscitation Guideline Leads to Lower Resuscitation Requirements (guideline, 2024)
- PMID 38845489: [Emergency expert consensus on diagnosis and treatment of intra-abdominal hypertension and abdominal compartment syndrome in adults in China (2024)] (guideline, 2024)
- PMID 39704902: Intra-abdominal Hypertension Is a Strong Predictor of Mortality and Poor Clinical Outcome in Severe Acute Pancreatitis (meta-analysis, 2025)

## Cluster `aub` (topics 5) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Abnormal uterine bleeding assessment combines structural causes (polyps, fibroids, adenomyosis) and endometrial sampling. Inherited bleeding disorders are an important cause of heavy menstrual bleeding.

**Teaching points**
- The French national guideline gives 36 recommendations (19 strong, 17 weak) on diagnostic and therapeutic strategies for abnormal uterine bleeding. [PMID 37499278]
- Endometrial biopsy is recommended in postmenopausal women with uterine bleeding, and hysteroscopy with biopsy is useful in abnormal bleeding even without sonographic abnormality; women taking tamoxifen with endometrial thickness over 4 mm should have hysteroscopic biopsy. [PMID 37061093]
- Heavy menstrual bleeding is the commonest hospital presentation of women with inherited bleeding disorders, and UKHCDO guidance covers diagnosis and gynaecological management. [PMID 35976756]
- Adenomyosis is common in reproductive-aged women, with diagnostic and fertility-preserving management options. [PMID 37244746]
- Endometrial polyps can cause abnormal bleeding, infertility or postmenopausal bleeding, and asymptomatic polyps often need no treatment. [PMID 38325734]
- Uterine fibroids can cause bleeding and pressure symptoms, with a low threshold for investigation and a variety of treatment options. [PMID 40562356]
- Outpatient hysteroscopy guidance says conscious sedation should not be routine. [PMID 39160077]

**Uncertainties**
- Management of bleeding disorders of unknown cause varies worldwide.

**MCQ angles**
- Endometrial sampling indications.
- Structural versus non-structural causes.

**Case hooks**
- Woman with heavy periods since menarche and a family history of bleeding.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** The PALM-COEIN classification, medical therapies (tranexamic acid, LNG-IUS) and referral thresholds are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 35976756: Gynaecological management of women with inherited bleeding disorders. A UK Haemophilia Centres Doctors' Organisation Guideline (guideline, 2022)
- PMID 37061093: Endometrial biopsy: Indications, techniques and recommendations. An evidence-based guideline for clinical practice (guideline, 2023)
- PMID 37244746: Guideline No. 437: Diagnosis and Management of Adenomyosis (guideline, 2023)
- PMID 37499278: Management of women with abnormal uterine bleeding: Clinical practice guidelines of the French National College of Gynaecologists and Obstetricians (CNGOF) (guideline, 2023)
- PMID 38325734: Guideline No. 447: Diagnosis and Management of Endometrial Polyps (guideline, 2024)
- PMID 39160077: Outpatient Hysteroscopy: (Green-top Guideline no. 59) (guideline, 2024)
- PMID 40562356: Guideline No. 461: The Management of Uterine Fibroids (guideline, 2025)

## Cluster `absence_jme` (topics 6) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Retrieved evidence on generalised epilepsy is limited: AAN practice guideline updates cover newer antiepileptic drugs, and genetic work shows distinct architectures for focal and generalised epilepsy. Syndrome-specific drug choice for absence and JME is not in the excerpts.

**Teaching points**
- AAN practice guideline updates assess the efficacy and tolerability of newer antiepileptic drugs and note that no high-quality studies exist for several agents in adult new-onset epilepsy. [PMID 29898971, 30254527]
- The AAN update on treatment-resistant epilepsy informs prescribing and identifies seizure types and syndromes needing more evidence. [PMID 29898974]
- A GWAS meta-analysis of over 29,000 people with epilepsy found different genetic architectures for focal and generalised epilepsies, with synaptic processes implicated in generalised epilepsy. [PMID 37653029]
- In children with epilepsy, ADHD treatment guidance found methylphenidate effective and tolerated (Level B). [PMID 30178479]
- A Mendelian randomisation study supports a causal link between Alzheimer disease and amyloid pathology and generalised epilepsy. [PMID 37225432]

**Uncertainties**
- Drug choice specifically for absence and JME.

**MCQ angles**
- Genetic generalised epilepsy has distinct genetics.

**Case hooks**
- Teenager with morning myoclonic jerks and a tonic-clonic seizure after sleep deprivation.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Ethosuximide, valproate, levetiracetam choices, pregnancy and valproate risks, EEG patterns and lifestyle triggers are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts. Needs ILAE or NICE epilepsy guidance.

**Sources**
- PMID 29898971: Practice guideline update summary: Efficacy and tolerability of the new antiepileptic drugs I: Treatment of new-onset epilepsy: Report of the Guideline Development, Dissemination, and Implementation Subcommittee of the American Academy of Neurology and the American Epilepsy Society (guideline, 2018)
- PMID 29898974: Practice guideline update summary: Efficacy and tolerability of the new antiepileptic drugs II: Treatment-resistant epilepsy: Report of the Guideline Development, Dissemination, and Implementation Subcommittee of the American Academy of Neurology and the American Epilepsy Society (guideline, 2018)
- PMID 30178479: Systematic review of the screening, diagnosis, and management of ADHD in children with epilepsy. Consensus paper of the Task Force on Comorbidities of the ILAE Pediatric Commission (guideline, 2018)
- PMID 30254527: Practice guideline update summary: Efficacy and tolerability of the new antiepileptic drugs I: Treatment of new-onset epilepsy: Report of the American Epilepsy Society and the Guideline Development, Dissemination, and Implementation Subcommittee of the American Academy of Neurology (guideline, 2018)
- PMID 37225432: Alzheimer Disease and Epilepsy: A Mendelian Randomization Study (meta-analysis, 2023)
- PMID 37653029: GWAS meta-analysis of over 29,000 people with epilepsy identifies 26 risk loci and subtype-specific genetic architecture (meta-analysis, 2023)

## Cluster `acalculous_chole` (topics 7) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Acute acalculous cholecystitis is rare and appears in critical illness and infections. Ultrasound is a good first test, and cholecystectomy is the standard treatment while percutaneous cholecystostomy may be definitive in selected high-risk patients.

**Teaching points**
- Ultrasound is a good imaging modality for diagnosing acute cholecystitis, and emergency-physician-performed ultrasound has similar diagnostic performance to radiologist-performed ultrasound. [PMID 38037062]
- A systematic review found patients at low surgical risk may benefit from cholecystectomy for acute acalculous cholecystitis, while percutaneous cholecystostomy may be definitive without later cholecystectomy, though study quality is low. [PMID 28776380]
- For acute cholecystitis generally, laparoscopic cholecystectomy within 3 days of diagnosis is first-line therapy in most patients. [PMID 35258527]
- Acute acalculous cholecystitis is the most frequent digestive manifestation in expanded dengue (pooled frequency 21%). [PMID 41893412]
- Acalculous cholecystitis is a rare association of COVID-19 that often presents as gangrenous disease and needs aggressive treatment. [PMID 37179790]
- Acute pancreatitis and acalculous cholecystitis are unusual but plausible complications of primary Epstein-Barr virus infection, usually mild and self-limiting. [PMID 27434148]
- ACR Appropriateness Criteria address imaging for right upper quadrant pain. [PMID 31054750]

**Uncertainties**
- Quality of evidence for cholecystostomy is low.

**MCQ angles**
- Ultrasound first for right upper quadrant pain.
- Cholecystostomy for high-risk patients.

**Case hooks**
- Critically ill ventilated patient with fever and right upper quadrant tenderness.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Risk factors (critical illness, TPN), HIDA scan sensitivity and perioperative antibiotic regimens are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 27434148: Pancreatitis and cholecystitis in primary acute symptomatic Epstein-Barr virus infection - Systematic review of the literature (systematic review, 2016)
- PMID 28776380: Is cholecystectomy the treatment of choice for acute acalculous cholecystitis? A systematic review of the literature (systematic review, 2017)
- PMID 31054750: ACR Appropriateness Criteria<sup>®</sup> Right Upper Quadrant Pain (guideline, 2019)
- PMID 35258527: Acute Cholecystitis: A Review (review, 2022)
- PMID 37179790: Acute acalculous cholecystitis as a rare gastroenterological association of COVID-19: a case series and systematic review (systematic review, 2023)
- PMID 38037062: Diagnostic performance of ultrasound in acute cholecystitis: a systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2023)
- PMID 41893412: Expanded Dengue and the Digestive System: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis (meta-analysis, 2026)

## Cluster `accel_athero` (topics 8) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Chronic inflammatory disease accelerates cardiovascular risk, so control of inflammation, risk stratification and lipid monitoring are central. JAK inhibitors can worsen lipids, and SLE has distinct risk factors for venous and arterial thrombosis.

**Teaching points**
- An Austrian consensus proposes a structured, disease-specific approach to cardiovascular risk management in RA, PsA and spondyloarthritis, combining inflammation control, risk stratification and tailored diagnostics. [PMID 41925750]
- Oral JAK inhibitors are associated with dyslipidaemia, so regular cardiovascular risk assessment and routine lipid monitoring may be essential during treatment. [PMID 41160040]
- A meta-analysis identified separate risk factors for venous and arterial thrombosis in SLE, supporting thrombotic-type-specific risk assessment. [PMID 41468843]
- Statin therapy may reduce disease activity and inflammation in SLE, with consistent benefit in younger patients. [PMID 41663155]
- The 2024 Mexican SLE guideline update added vaccines, pregnancy and cardiovascular risk using the GRADE system. [PMID 39505612]

**Uncertainties**
- Best cardiovascular risk calculator for inflammatory disease.

**MCQ angles**
- JAK inhibitors and lipid changes.
- SLE thrombosis risk factors.

**Case hooks**
- Woman with long-standing SLE and rising LDL cholesterol.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** The mechanism of accelerated atherosclerosis, EULAR risk multiplier and treatment targets are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 39505612: Clinical Practice Mexican Guidelines for the Treatment of Systemic Lupus Erythematosus: 2024 Update (guideline, 2024)
- PMID 41160040: Risk of Dyslipidemia Associated With Oral Janus Kinase Inhibitors: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis of Randomized Placebo-Controlled Trials (meta-analysis, 2026)
- PMID 41468843: Factors associated with venous and arterial thrombosis in patients with Systemic Lupus Erythematosus: A systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2026)
- PMID 41663155: Statin therapy in systemic lupus erythematosus: a meta-analysis of disease activity and inflammatory biomarkers (meta-analysis, 2026)
- PMID 41925750: [Consensus statement of the Austrian Society for Rheumatology and Rehabilitation on the management of increased cardiovascular risk in rheumatoid arthritis, psoriatic arthritis and spondyloarthritis] (guideline, 2026)

## Cluster `acei_arb` (topics 9) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** ACE inhibitors and ARBs are core guideline-directed therapy, but prescribing is suboptimal and falls over time in diabetes with CKD. In advanced CKD, starting an ACEi or ARB protects against kidney failure requiring replacement therapy but not against death.

**Teaching points**
- Initiation of ACEi or ARB therapy in advanced CKD protects against kidney failure with replacement therapy but not against death. [PMID 38950402]
- Prescribing of guideline-directed therapies in patients with diabetes and CKD was suboptimal and waned quickly, with insurance type and health system associated with prescribing. [PMID 37395334]
- Early adoption of guideline-recommended therapy after acute heart failure decompensation is limited, and lower 30-day death or readmission was seen only with ACEi/ARB/ARNi use. [PMID 39922597]
- In patients aged 80 or older with acute myocardial infarction after PCI, guideline-directed medical therapy was associated with lower cardiovascular death but not lower all-cause mortality. [PMID 38233147]
- ACS patients without standard modifiable risk factors had higher in-hospital mortality and cardiogenic shock yet received less guideline-directed therapy at discharge. [PMID 36179904]
- Cardiac resynchronisation therapy was associated with enabling use and dosing of heart failure therapy and with reduced loop diuretic need in HFrEF. [PMID 40470816]

**Uncertainties**
- Mortality benefit in advanced CKD.

**MCQ angles**
- ACEi/ARB in advanced CKD and kidney failure risk.
- Under-prescription of guideline-directed therapy.

**Case hooks**
- Diabetic patient with eGFR 22 and albuminuria not on an ACE inhibitor.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Indications by condition, hyperkalaemia and creatinine monitoring, pregnancy contraindication and angioedema are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 36179904: Higher mortality in acute coronary syndrome patients without standard modifiable risk factors: Results from a global meta-analysis of 1,285,722 patients (meta-analysis, 2023)
- PMID 37395334: Prescription of guideline-directed medical therapies in patients with diabetes and chronic kidney disease from the CURE-CKD Registry, 2019-2020 (guideline, 2023)
- PMID 38233147: Guideline-Directed Medical Therapy for Elderly Patients With Acute Myocardial Infarction Who Undergo Percutaneous Coronary Intervention　- Insights From a Retrospective Observational Study (guideline, 2024)
- PMID 38950402: Angiotensin-Converting Enzyme Inhibitors or Angiotensin-Receptor Blockers for Advanced Chronic Kidney Disease : A Systematic Review and Retrospective Individual Participant-Level Meta-analysis of Clinical Trials (meta-analysis, 2024)
- PMID 39922597: Temporal trends in guideline-recommended medical therapy after an acute heart failure decompensation event: an observational analysis from Generator Heart Failure DataMart (guideline, 2025)
- PMID 40470816: Cardiac resynchronization therapy for enabling guideline-directed medical therapy optimization in heart failure (guideline, 2025)

## Cluster `achalasia` (topics 10) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Achalasia treatment options are pneumatic dilation, Heller myotomy and POEM. POEM has durable efficacy (87% long-term) with stable post-procedure reflux, and subtype 3 favours POEM; guidelines from ASGE, UEG/ESNM and SAGES cover choice and follow-up.

**Teaching points**
- A meta-analysis found long-term clinical efficacy of POEM persisted in 87% of patients, with a pooled severe adverse event rate of 1.5% and minimal risk of Barrett's oesophagus and peptic strictures. [PMID 35798336]
- The 2024 SAGES update gives a conditional recommendation for either POEM or Heller with fundoplication in subtypes 1 and 2, and prefers POEM for subtype 3; POEM should be used with appropriate PPI use. [PMID 40399617]
- The ASGE guideline gives evidence-based recommendations comparing the effectiveness, adverse effects and cost of achalasia therapies. [PMID 31839408]
- European UEG/ESNM guidelines cover diagnosis, medical, endoscopic and surgical therapy, treatment failure, follow-up and oesophageal cancer risk in adult achalasia. [PMID 32213062]
- ESGE advises G-POEM in carefully selected patients in expert centres only, preferably within clinical trials, because data are limited. [PMID 32375192]
- A POEM centre should maintain a prospective registry of procedures, techniques and adverse events. [PMID 40203869]
- The global incidence and prevalence of achalasia have increased notably, with large worldwide variation. [PMID 38430514]

**Uncertainties**
- Long-term comparison of POEM with Heller myotomy.

**MCQ angles**
- Manometric subtype 3 favours POEM.
- Post-POEM reflux requires PPI surveillance.

**Case hooks**
- Young adult with dysphagia to solids and liquids and a bird-beak oesophagus.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Chicago classification, botulinum toxin and pneumatic dilation efficacy and cancer surveillance are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 31839408: ASGE guideline on the management of achalasia (guideline, 2020)
- PMID 32213062: European guidelines on achalasia: United European Gastroenterology and European Society of Neurogastroenterology and Motility recommendations (guideline, 2020)
- PMID 32375192: Endoscopic management of gastrointestinal motility disorders - part 1: European Society of Gastrointestinal Endoscopy (ESGE) Guideline (guideline, 2020)
- PMID 35798336: Long-term outcomes of peroral endoscopic myotomy for achalasia: a systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2023)
- PMID 38430514: Global trends in incidence and prevalence of achalasia, 1925-2021: A systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2024)
- PMID 40203869: Curriculum for training in peroral endoscopic myotomy (POEM) in Europe (Part I): European Society of Gastrointestinal Endoscopy (ESGE) Position Statement (guideline, 2025)
- PMID 40399617: 2024 Update to SAGES guidelines for the use of peroral endoscopic myotomy (POEM) in the treatment of achalasia (guideline, 2025)

## Cluster `acls` (topics 11, 308) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** The 2025 ERC and AHA resuscitation guidelines are the current references for adult ALS, post-resuscitation care and special circumstances. Post-arrest care covers temperature control, seizures, prognostication and organ donation.

**Teaching points**
- The ERC 2025 adult ALS guidelines emphasise early and effective ALS interventions to improve survival from in- or out-of-hospital cardiac arrest. [PMID 41117572]
- The ERC 2025 special circumstances guidelines describe modifications to basic and advanced life support for prevention and treatment of cardiac arrest in specific situations. [PMID 41117569]
- ERC/ESICM 2025 post-resuscitation care covers haemodynamic monitoring, seizure control, temperature control, intensive care management, prognostication, rehabilitation and organ donation. [PMID 41123621, 41117575]
- The AHA 2025 post-cardiac arrest care guideline includes treatment of myoclonus, survivorship for patients, caregivers and rescuers, and the role of organ donation. [PMID 41122894]
- The ILCOR 2025 consensus on science for advanced life support provides treatment recommendations and lists priority knowledge gaps. [PMID 41122842]
- ERC 2025 adult basic life support embeds technology use into the relevant sections. [PMID 41117574]

**Uncertainties**
- Evidence gaps are listed in the ILCOR consensus.

**MCQ angles**
- Post-arrest care bundle elements.
- Special circumstances modifications.

**Case hooks**
- Witnessed VF arrest in hospital.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** The shockable and non-shockable algorithms, drug doses, compression rate and depth, and reversible causes are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 41117569: European Resuscitation Council Guidelines 2025 Special Circumstances in Resuscitation (guideline, 2025)
- PMID 41117572: European Resuscitation Council Guidelines 2025 Adult Advanced Life Support (guideline, 2025)
- PMID 41117574: European Resuscitation Council Guidelines 2025 Adult Basic Life Support (guideline, 2025)

## Cluster `acromegaly` (topics 12, 13) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Acromegaly diagnosis and remission are defined by GH and IGF-I criteria in expert consensus, with surgery first and somatostatin analogues as adjuvant therapy. Controlled disease normalises mortality, and pasireotide-induced hyperglycaemia needs dedicated management.

**Teaching points**
- A consensus sets criteria for acromegaly diagnosis and remission, stressing new understandings of GH and IGF-I disorder and the importance of expert management. [PMID 37923946]
- A 2025 consensus update on therapeutic outcomes provides an evidence-based management algorithm that considers adenoma mass, surgical outcomes and factors determining pharmacological choice. [PMID 40804505]
- Mortality in acromegaly is normalised with biochemical control and decreased in the last decade with more frequent adjuvant somatostatin analogue use. [PMID 29764907]
- A systematic review found acromegaly leads to common comorbidities including diabetes, thyroid cancer and menstrual disorders, with recognition of a combination of features key to diagnosis. [PMID 37210433]
- Stereotactic radiosurgery offers effective tumour control in most secretory pituitary adenomas but a lower rate of endocrine remission. [PMID 34479203]
- An expert consensus advises managing pasireotide-induced hyperglycaemia with attention to acromegaly control, diabetes education and lifestyle, and consulting diabetologists in uncertainty. [PMID 38405148]
- Predictive markers for postsurgical medical management of acromegaly were reviewed by consensus, including tumour granulation pattern and somatostatin receptor expression. [PMID 30657362]

**Uncertainties**
- Selection of medical therapy after surgery.

**MCQ angles**
- Biochemical control normalises mortality.
- Pasireotide hyperglycaemia.

**Case hooks**
- Patient with enlarging hands, sleep apnoea and raised IGF-1.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** The OGTT GH suppression threshold, IGF-1 interpretation, surgery remission rates and somatostatin analogue dosing are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 29764907: Mortality in acromegaly decreased in the last decade: a systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2018)
- PMID 30657362: PREDICTIVE MARKERS FOR POSTSURGICAL MEDICAL MANAGEMENT OF ACROMEGALY: A SYSTEMATIC REVIEW AND CONSENSUS TREATMENT GUIDELINE (guideline, 2019)
- PMID 34479203: Stereotactic radiosurgery for secretory pituitary adenomas: systematic review and International Stereotactic Radiosurgery Society practice recommendations (guideline, 2022)
- PMID 37210433: Prevalence of clinical signs, symptoms and comorbidities at diagnosis of acromegaly: a systematic review in accordance with PRISMA guidelines (guideline, 2023)
- PMID 37923946: Consensus on criteria for acromegaly diagnosis and remission (guideline, 2024)
- PMID 38405148: Management of pasireotide-induced hyperglycemia in patients with acromegaly: An experts' consensus statement (guideline, 2024)
- PMID 40804505: Consensus on acromegaly therapeutic outcomes: an update (guideline, 2025)

## Cluster `acs` (topics 14, 34, 35, 36) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** The 2025 ACC/AHA ACS guideline and the Australian guideline set current care; P2Y12 monotherapy may prove better than aspirin for long-term prevention and CYP2C19 genotyping may guide P2Y12 choice. Secondary prevention uses stricter LDL targets and PCSK9 inhibitors.

**Teaching points**
- The Australian ACS guideline supports selective intravascular imaging in NSTEACS, updated P2Y12 timing, stricter LDL-C targets and PCSK9 inhibitor use in secondary prevention. [PMID 41693087]
- Commentary on the 2025 ACS guideline urges that CYP2C19-guided P2Y12 inhibitor selection be incorporated, especially for patients at high bleeding risk. [PMID 42089171]
- New data from HOST-EXAM and SMART CHOICE 3 suggest P2Y12 monotherapy may be better than aspirin for long-term prevention of major adverse cardiovascular events. [PMID 42832065]
- A UK guideline addresses CYP2C19 genotype testing for clopidogrel. [PMID 41338248]
- Mental disorders, particularly PTSD and sleep disorders, are significant risk factors for ACS. [PMID 41533387]
- Where HIT patients need cardiac surgery with negative functional assays, limited intraoperative heparin re-exposure carries a low risk of HIT recurrence with postoperative monitoring. [PMID 42248416]

**Uncertainties**
- Optimal long-term antiplatelet monotherapy.

**MCQ angles**
- CYP2C19 and clopidogrel.
- P2Y12 monotherapy after PCI.

**Case hooks**
- NSTEMI patient at high bleeding risk starting dual antiplatelet therapy.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Diagnosis (troponin pathways), risk scores, timing of angiography, DAPT duration and anticoagulant choices are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**

- PMID 41533387: Mental Disorders as a Risk Factor of Acute Coronary Syndrome: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis (meta-analysis, 2026)

## Cluster `active_tb` (topics 15) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Xpert MTB/RIF Ultra is the key rapid molecular test for pulmonary TB and rifampicin resistance. Survivors have lasting lung function loss and risk of chronic pulmonary aspergillosis, and anti-TB hepatotoxicity relates to slow-acetylator NAT2 genotype.

**Teaching points**
- In people with a history of tuberculosis, Xpert Ultra summary sensitivity and specificity were 84.8% and 86.2%, and Xpert Ultra effectively diagnosed pulmonary TB and rifampicin resistance. [PMID 40728034, 39754613]
- People who recover from pulmonary TB have significantly decreased lung function, with FEV1 more affected than FVC and a mixed obstructive and restrictive pattern with predominant airflow obstruction. [PMID 40412395]
- The high burden of chronic pulmonary aspergillosis among TB survivors, particularly those with persistent symptoms, supports routine CPA screening in TB programmes. [PMID 40117217]
- Chronic pulmonary aspergillosis is associated with substantial mortality, predicted by age, subtype and comorbidity. [PMID 39617023]
- Slow-acetylator NAT2 genotype predicts increased risk of anti-tuberculosis drug-induced hepatotoxicity, allowing dose adjustment or alternative therapy. [PMID 39639188]
- Pulmonary TB is associated with increased lung cancer risk, supporting targeted screening, early detection and smoking cessation. [PMID 39733773]
- A review of extra-pulmonary TB prevalence in Africa calls for coordinated efforts targeting both extrapulmonary and pulmonary disease. [PMID 38263374]

**Uncertainties**
- Treatment regimens are not covered by the retrieved excerpts.

**MCQ angles**
- Xpert Ultra detects rifampicin resistance.
- Post-TB lung disease and aspergillosis.

**Case hooks**
- Patient with cough, night sweats and a positive Xpert trace result.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** The standard four-drug regimen, duration, monitoring, contact tracing and drug-resistant TB regimens are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 38263374: Prevalence of extra-pulmonary tuberculosis in Africa: A systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2024)
- PMID 39617023: Mortality in chronic pulmonary aspergillosis: a systematic review and individual patient data meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2025)
- PMID 39639188: Pharmacogenomic insights into tuberculosis treatment shows the NAT2 genetic variants linked to hepatotoxicity risk: a systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2024)
- PMID 39733773: Pulmonary Tuberculosis and Risk of Lung Cancer: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis (meta-analysis, 2025)
- PMID 39754613: Diagnostic accuracy of Xpert MTB/RIF Ultra for detecting pulmonary tuberculosis and rifampicin resistance: a systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2025)
- PMID 40117217: A Systematic Review of Chronic Pulmonary Aspergillosis Among Patients Treated for Pulmonary Tuberculosis (meta-analysis, 2025)

## Cluster `acute_abdomen` (topics 16, 17) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Emergency laparotomy benefits from ERAS-style perioperative pathways, frailty assessment and a laparoscopic-first approach in stable patients. Sarcopenia predicts mortality, and prophylactic negative pressure wound therapy lowers surgical site infection.

**Teaching points**
- ERAS Society consensus guidelines for emergency laparotomy gather evidence on perioperative care components, much of it extrapolated from elective or general emergency surgery. [PMID 37277507, 37277506]
- The WSES Cesena consensus suggests laparoscopy as the first approach for stable patients needing emergency abdominal surgery for general surgery emergencies and abdominal trauma. [PMID 38066631]
- A WSES position paper addresses assessing and managing frailty in emergency laparotomy. [PMID 37355698]
- Sarcopenia can identify preoperative high-risk patients, since it predicts postoperative mortality after emergency laparotomy. [PMID 35752855]
- Prophylactic negative pressure wound therapy reduces overall surgical site infection for closed laparotomy wounds, especially in high-risk patients. [PMID 32588378]
- The OBA guideline outlines focused emergency operative management of acute abdomen after bariatric surgery, requiring close collaboration across surgery, radiology, endoscopy and anaesthesia. [PMID 36167572]
- A Chinese expert consensus covers diagnosis and treatment of small bowel obstruction. [PMID 37217345]

**Uncertainties**
- Evidence base for many ERAS components in emergency laparotomy is extrapolated.

**MCQ angles**
- Laparoscopic-first for stable patients.
- Frailty and sarcopenia as risk markers.

**Case hooks**
- Older frail patient with peritonitis needing emergency laparotomy.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Differential diagnosis by quadrant, resuscitation, imaging choice and antibiotic regimens are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 32588378: Prophylactic negative pressure wound therapy for closed laparotomy wounds: a systematic review and meta-analysis of randomised controlled trials (meta-analysis, 2021)
- PMID 35752855: Effect of sarcopenia in predicting postoperative mortality in emergency laparotomy: a systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2022)
- PMID 36167572: Operative management of acute abdomen after bariatric surgery in the emergency setting: the OBA guidelines (guideline, 2022)

## Cluster `pain_opioid` (topics 18, 126) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Opioid-sparing multimodal analgesia is the aim: regional blocks (TAP) reduce opioid use, buprenorphine may be a first-line postoperative opioid, and tramadol's harms likely outweigh limited chronic pain benefits.

**Teaching points**
- Buprenorphine supports use as a first-line opioid analgesic for acute postoperative pain needing opioids, with a favourable safety profile and longer duration of action, potentially reducing opioid-related harm. [PMID 39753290]
- The TAP block is effective for pain management and opioid sparing after abdominal surgery. [PMID 41339171]
- The 2026 ASA practice guideline on perioperative pain using local and regional anaesthesia is limited by low methodological quality, inconsistent outcome measures and small samples. [PMID 41363869]
- Tramadol versus placebo for chronic pain gives only small improvements while likely increasing serious adverse events, so potential harms likely outweigh limited benefits. [PMID 41057269]
- A German guideline on spinal cord stimulation for chronic neuropathic pain preferentially recommends dorsal root ganglion stimulation over spinal cord stimulation for CRPS. [PMID 42126579]
- European interdisciplinary guidelines address pain management in acute pancreatitis, including epidural analgesia, acupuncture, paediatric use and pregnancy. [PMID 42299777]

**Uncertainties**
- Quality of regional anaesthesia trials is limited.

**MCQ angles**
- Opioid-sparing multimodal analgesia.
- Tramadol risk-benefit.

**Case hooks**
- Post-laparotomy patient requiring analgesia with minimal opioid exposure.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Pain assessment tools, opioid conversion, safety measures (naloxone, prescribing limits), neuropathic pain drugs and chronic pain biopsychosocial care are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 39753290: Buprenorphine versus full agonist opioids for acute postoperative pain management: a systematic review and meta-analysis of randomized controlled trials (meta-analysis, 2026)
- PMID 41057269: Tramadol versus placebo for chronic pain: a systematic review with meta-analysis and trial sequential analysis (meta-analysis, 2026)
- PMID 41339171: Transversus abdominis plane block for postoperative pain management and opioid sparing: a systematic review and meta-analysis of randomised controlled trials (meta-analysis, 2026)
- PMID 41363869: 2026 American Society of Anesthesiologists Practice Guideline on Perioperative Pain Management Using Local and Regional Analgesia for Cardiothoracic Surgeries, Mastectomy, and Abdominal Surgeries (guideline, 2026)

## Cluster `pericarditis` (topics 19, 67) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** The first combined ESC guidelines on myocarditis and pericarditis highlight interleukin-1 antagonists for recurrent pericarditis. A network meta-analysis found colchicine and anti-IL-1 agents both reduce recurrence, with anti-IL-1 agents causing more non-serious adverse events.

**Teaching points**
- The first combined European guidelines on myocarditis and pericarditis bring interleukin-1 antagonists to the fore for pericarditis and consider genetic factors in recurrent forms. [PMID 41511532]
- Colchicine and anti-interleukin-1 agents are both effective in reducing the risk of recurrent pericarditis; anti-IL-1 agents cause more frequent non-serious adverse events, with serious adverse event evidence inconclusive. [PMID 40551317]
- An international position statement on pericardial diseases emphasises multimodality cardiac imaging and new therapeutics. [PMID 39111992]
- For pericardial complications after open heart surgery, evidence for some preventive and treatment interventions is relatively weak and large trials are needed. [PMID 39848652]
- Canadian imaging referral guidelines cover pericardial syndromes among cardiovascular indications. [PMID 38733286]
- In frail older patients, optimal pleuro-myopericarditis treatment may differ from guideline-based strategies when patient-specific risks outweigh benefits. [PMID 42232314]

**Uncertainties**
- Evidence in frail older patients.

**MCQ angles**
- IL-1 antagonists for recurrence.
- Colchicine in recurrent pericarditis.

**Case hooks**
- Recurrent pleuritic chest pain after a first pericarditis episode.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Diagnostic criteria, NSAID dosing and colchicine duration for a first episode are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**

## Cluster `aortic_dissection` (topics 20, 21, 22, 172) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Retrieved evidence centres on outcomes rather than the diagnostic pathway: type A dissection carries organ-specific malperfusion risk that predicts in-hospital death, and for type B the question is whether and when to add TEVAR to medical therapy. Guideline-level recommendations were not in the retrieved set.

**Teaching points**
- In acute type A aortic dissection, in-hospital survival varies with the type of malperfusion; mesenteric, coronary and cerebral malperfusion carried the highest in-hospital mortality, so organ-specific malperfusion should inform perioperative risk and surgical planning. [PMID 39792052]
- Women with type A dissection more often had pericardial effusion or tamponade, while in-hospital surgical mortality did not differ by sex (RR 1.02), suggesting a personalised approach. [PMID 36629802]
- A meta-analysis found TEVAR was associated with lower mortality than best medical therapy in type B dissection, especially in patients aged 65 or older, at the cost of longer hospital and ICU stays, with randomised trials still needed. [PMID 39261808]
- For uncomplicated type B dissection, the role of TEVAR remains debated: it improves aortic remodelling but there is no definitive survival evidence, and trials and guidelines that include high-risk features are needed. [PMID 40582458]
- Meta-analyses of TEVAR timing found the subacute phase (14-90 days from onset) associated with better long-term outcomes, including lower aortic-related death and reintervention. [PMID 38142754, 38590280]
- Retrograde type A dissection after TEVAR is rare but carries high mortality (about 42%), especially in the first month and with proximal bare-stent grafts. [PMID 37058052]
- The GERAADA score showed better calibration than EuroSCORE II for 30-day mortality after surgery for acute type A dissection, though discrimination of both was moderate. [PMID 40221854]
- AI tools can feasibly detect aortic dissection on CT but do not yet demonstrate clinical applicability, and most studies were low quality. [PMID 40503985]

**Uncertainties**
- Whether pre-emptive TEVAR improves survival in uncomplicated type B dissection.

**MCQ angles**
- Malperfusion type and operative mortality in type A dissection.
- Timing of TEVAR in type B dissection.

**Case hooks**
- Type A dissection with mesenteric malperfusion on CT angiography.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Presentation, the diagnostic pathway (CT angiography, D-dimer), blood-pressure and heart-rate targets with beta-blockers, indications for emergency surgery and classification systems are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts. Needs the ESC 2024 or ACC/AHA 2022 aortic guideline before approval.

**Sources**
- PMID 36629802: Sex differences in type A acute aortic dissection: a systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2023)
- PMID 37058052: A systematic review and meta-analysis of retrograde type A aortic dissection after thoracic endovascular aortic repair in patients with type B aortic dissection (meta-analysis, 2023)
- PMID 38142754: Thoracic endovascular aortic repair for hyperacute, acute, subacute and chronic type B aortic dissection: Meta-analysis of reconstructed time-to-event data (meta-analysis, 2024)
- PMID 38590280: Optimal Timing of Pre-emptive Thoracic Endovascular Aortic Repair in Uncomplicated Type B Aortic Dissection: A Network Meta-Analysis (meta-analysis, 2026)
- PMID 39261808: Efficacy of thoracic endovascular aortic repair versus medical therapy for treatment of type B aortic dissection (meta-analysis, 2024)
- PMID 39792052: Organ-specific malperfusion in acute type A aortic dissection: epidemiological meta-analysis of incidence rates (meta-analysis, 2024)
- PMID 40221854: The German Registry of Acute Aortic Dissection Type A score for 30-day mortality prediction in Type A Acute Aortic Dissection surgery: a systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2025)
- PMID 40503985: Role of Artificial Intelligence in Detecting and Classifying Aortic Dissection: Where Are We? A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis (meta-analysis, 2025)
- PMID 40582458: TEVAR Versus Optimal Medical Therapy for Uncomplicated Type B Aortic Dissection: A Systematic Review &amp; Meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2025)

## Cluster `asthma_exac` (topics 23, 24, 74, 196) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Acute asthma care centres on bronchodilators and systemic corticosteroids; high-dose inhaled corticosteroids may add benefit, and IV magnesium helps children. Prevention depends on adherence, eosinophilic phenotype and inhaler technique.

**Teaching points**
- A meta-analysis found moderate evidence that high-dose inhaled corticosteroids added to systemic corticosteroids reduce hospital admission (OR 0.73) in ED treatment of moderate-to-severe exacerbations. [PMID 31521830]
- In children, intravenous magnesium sulfate improved pulmonary function and reduced hospitalisation and further treatment, whereas nebulised magnesium had no significant effect. [PMID 29851914]
- The Italian paediatric acute-asthma guideline advises against epinephrine, allows IV magnesium sulfate in severe attacks or FEV1 below 60% unresponsive to inhaled therapy, allows heliox in life-threatening attacks, and does not recommend leukotriene receptor antagonists. [PMID 29625590]
- Adherence of 80% or more lowered the odds of severe exacerbation (OR 0.53), and discontinuing therapy raised it. [PMID 32192946]
- Blood eosinophils of 200 cells/microlitre or more are associated with exacerbation (a modifiable factor in asthma management). [PMID 33135257]
- Inhaler technique education reduced exacerbation rates in older adults with asthma or COPD (pooled risk ratio 0.71). [PMID 30291745]
- Oral timolol and infusion propranolol were associated with higher asthma attack risk and should be avoided in patients at risk of asthma. [PMID 33432057]
- GORD and asthma exacerbation are only weakly associated. [PMID 34448255]

**Uncertainties**
- Optimal role of inhaled steroids in the emergency department.

**MCQ angles**
- IV magnesium in children.
- Beta-blockers that provoke asthma.

**Case hooks**
- Child with severe attack unresponsive to initial inhaled therapy.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Severity classification, oxygen targets, nebulised salbutamol and ipratropium dosing, oral steroid doses, and escalation to ventilation are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts. Needs the BTS/SIGN or GINA acute asthma sections.

**Sources**
- PMID 29625590: Guideline on management of the acute asthma attack in children by Italian Society of Pediatrics (guideline, 2018)
- PMID 29851914: Intravenous and Nebulized Magnesium Sulfate for Treating Acute Asthma in Children: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis (meta-analysis, 2018)
- PMID 30291745: Inhaler Technique Education and Exacerbation Risk in Older Adults with Asthma or Chronic Obstructive Pulmonary Disease: A Meta-Analysis (meta-analysis, 2019)
- PMID 31521830: Inhaled Corticosteroids in Acute Asthma: A Systemic Review and Meta-Analysis (meta-analysis, 2020)
- PMID 32192946: Association between adherence and severe asthma exacerbation: A systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2020)
- PMID 33135257: Blood eosinophil count as predictor of asthma exacerbation. A meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2021)
- PMID 33432057: Do beta-adrenergic blocking agents increase asthma exacerbation? A network meta-analysis of randomized controlled trials (meta-analysis, 2021)
- PMID 34448255: Gastroesophageal reflux disease and asthma exacerbation: A systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2022)

## Cluster `meningitis_bact` (topics 25, 26, 27, 225) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Bacterial meningitis is time-critical: antibiotics later than two hours after presentation roughly double mortality. Case fatality has fallen over eighty years but remains high; molecular and host-marker tests may help when cultures are negative.

**Teaching points**
- In community-acquired bacterial meningitis, odds of mortality increase markedly when antibiotics are given later than two hours after presentation, and delay raised the odds of neurological impairment (aOR 1.79). [PMID 35027606]
- Case fatality of community-acquired bacterial meningitis has declined over the last century but a high burden remains. [PMID 39093565]
- Aetiology of bacterial meningitis differs by age group and region. [PMID 29889859]
- Metagenomic next-generation sequencing has moderate diagnostic accuracy for bacterial meningitis/encephalitis and may help when standard diagnosis fails. [PMID 35474146]
- 16S/18S rRNA gene PCR with Sanger sequencing can diagnose non-growing pathogens and may change antibiotic treatment when cultures stay negative. [PMID 37367430]
- Heparin-binding protein has high diagnostic accuracy for bacterial infections including meningitis (sensitivity and specificity 87% at a cut-off of 32.868 ng/ml). [PMID 38297213]
- Febrile neonates have about twice the rate of bacteraemia and meningitis as febrile infants in the second month of life. [PMID 30901044]

**Uncertainties**
- Role of adjunctive dexamethasone is not covered by the retrieved excerpts.

**MCQ angles**
- Time to antibiotics matters.
- Aetiology by age.

**Case hooks**
- Febrile adult with neck stiffness and altered consciousness.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Empiric regimens by age, dexamethasone timing and benefit by organism, CT before lumbar puncture, CSF interpretation and prophylaxis of contacts are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 29889859: Global etiology of bacterial meningitis: A systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2018)
- PMID 30901044: Prevalence of Bacteremia and Bacterial Meningitis in Febrile Neonates and Infants in the Second Month of Life: A Systematic Review and Meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2019)
- PMID 35027606: Longer than 2 hours to antibiotics is associated with doubling of mortality in a multinational community-acquired bacterial meningitis cohort (meta-analysis, 2022)
- PMID 35474146: Diagnostic accuracy of the metagenomic next-generation sequencing (mNGS) for detection of bacterial meningoencephalitis: a systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2022)
- PMID 37367430: Direct 16S/18S rRNA Gene PCR Followed by Sanger Sequencing as a Clinical Diagnostic Tool for Detection of Bacterial and Fungal Infections: a Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis (meta-analysis, 2023)
- PMID 38297213: The diagnostic utility of heparin-binding protein among patients with bacterial infections: a systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2024)
- PMID 39093565: Global Case Fatality of Bacterial Meningitis During an 80-Year Period: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis (meta-analysis, 2024)

## Cluster `agitation` (topics 28, 93) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Non-pharmacological measures are first-line for agitation in the ICU. In dementia, medications are reserved for refractory cases, and routine long-term use is not supported. Melatonin may improve sleep and reduce delirium.

**Teaching points**
- A 2026 clinical practice guideline recommends nonpharmacological prevention and management of patient agitation in the ICU, to be integrated into standard care. [PMID 41297363]
- Pharmacological management of agitation in dementia should be reserved for refractory cases, and long-term or routine use is not supported by current evidence. [PMID 41527161]
- Melatonin in the ICU may improve sleep and reduce delirium without more adverse effects, though certainty is limited by bias and inconsistency. [PMID 40662882]
- ICU delirium and pain prevalence have decreased since pain, agitation and delirium guidelines but remain common, up to one-half of patients. [PMID 41117943]
- Ethnic disparities exist in rapid tranquillisation use in adult mental health inpatient settings (relative risk 1.32). [PMID 39805639]
- A Canadian guideline covers assessment and management of behaviour change after moderate-to-severe traumatic brain injury. [PMID 39256159]

**Uncertainties**
- Responsive patient subgroups for medication.

**MCQ angles**
- Non-drug measures first.
- Antipsychotics in dementia are limited to refractory cases.

**Case hooks**
- Agitated delirious ICU patient pulling at lines.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Rapid tranquillisation drug choice and doses, de-escalation technique, monitoring and legal framework are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 39256159: Updated Canadian Clinical Practice Guideline for the Rehabilitation of Adults With Moderate to Severe Traumatic Brain Injury: Behavioral Recommendations (guideline, 2024)

## Cluster `cholecystitis` (topics 29, 30) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** For acute cholecystitis in high-risk patients, drainage options (percutaneous cholecystostomy or EUS-guided drainage) are bridges, with delayed laparoscopic cholecystectomy after recovery. Tokyo and IDSA guidance cover diagnosis and antibiotics.

**Teaching points**
- The Italian consensus suggests delayed laparoscopic cholecystectomy in patients with percutaneous gallbladder drainage after recovery, at least 6 weeks after drainage. [PMID 38153659]
- An international Delphi on percutaneous cholecystostomy chose the transhepatic route and laparoscopic cholecystectomy afterwards (93.1%), with only six statements reaching consensus. [PMID 39396036]
- A multidisciplinary consensus guides management of high-surgical-risk patients with acute cholecystitis after percutaneous cholecystostomy, including tube removal when biliary tree patency is shown. [PMID 40072363]
- AGA and ASGE guidance address EUS-guided gallbladder drainage in acute cholecystitis for patients who are not candidates for cholecystectomy. [PMID 36967319, 39078360]
- The 2024 IDSA guideline update addresses complicated intra-abdominal infection using GRADE methodology. [PMID 38963820]
- ACR Appropriateness Criteria address imaging of right upper quadrant pain. [PMID 37236744]

**Uncertainties**
- EUS-guided versus percutaneous drainage.

**MCQ angles**
- Drainage then delayed cholecystectomy for unfit patients.

**Case hooks**
- Frail patient with gangrenous cholecystitis not fit for surgery.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Tokyo Guidelines severity grades, ascending cholangitis management and ERCP timing, and antibiotic regimens are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 36967319: AGA Clinical Practice Update on Role of EUS-Guided Gallbladder Drainage in Acute Cholecystitis: Commentary (guideline, 2023)
- PMID 37236744: ACR Appropriateness Criteria® Right Upper Quadrant Pain: 2022 Update (guideline, 2023)
- PMID 38153659: Acute cholecystitis management in high-risk, critically ill, and unfit-for-surgery patients: the Italian Society of Emergency Surgery and Trauma (SICUT) guidelines (guideline, 2024)
- PMID 38963820: 2024 Clinical Practice Guideline Update by the Infectious Diseases Society of America on Complicated Intraabdominal Infections: Diagnostic Imaging of Suspected Acute Cholecystitis and Acute Cholangitis in Adults, Children, and Pregnant People (guideline, 2024)
- PMID 39078360: American Society for Gastrointestinal Endoscopy guideline on the role of therapeutic EUS in the management of biliary tract disorders: summary and recommendations (guideline, 2024)
- PMID 39396036: International Delphi consensus on the management of percutaneous choleystostomy in acute cholecystitis (E-AHPBA, ANS, WSES societies) (guideline, 2024)

## Cluster `delirium` (topics 31, 32, 33) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Delirium is common in critical illness and AKI and is linked to worse outcomes. Guidelines address pain, sedation, sleep and immobility; ketamine and antipsychotics have limited guidance, and music and some drugs may reduce delirium.

**Teaching points**
- SCCM 2026 guidelines on caring for older adults in the ICU include recommendations on antipsychotic medication for delirium treatment. [PMID 41860322]
- A Dutch guideline offers 22 recommendations on pain, analgesia/sedation, delirium, immobility and sleep in ICU patients. [PMID 42090788]
- A rapid practice guideline addresses ketamine analgo-sedation for ventilated adults, noting large trials are needed. [PMID 39207913]
- Delirium is common in critically ill patients with AKI and is associated with worse outcomes and longer hospital stay (WMD 4.78 days). [PMID 41405369]
- Pre-recorded music interventions reduce delirium development in mostly elderly patients (CI 0.37-0.73). [PMID 41173741]
- Drug interventions to prevent postoperative delirium in older adults, including olanzapine and others, showed potential benefit but evidence was moderate to very low quality. [PMID 41690769]
- ICU delirium and pain prevalence have fallen since pain, agitation and delirium guidelines yet remain common. [PMID 41117943]

**Uncertainties**
- Evidence quality for pharmacological prevention is low.

**MCQ angles**
- Non-drug measures and sleep.
- AKI and delirium.

**Case hooks**
- Elderly post-operative patient with fluctuating attention.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** CAM-ICU or 4AT screening, causes checklist, haloperidol dosing and capacity assessment are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 39207913: Ketamine Analgo-sedation for Mechanically Ventilated Critically Ill Adults: A Rapid Practice Guideline from the Saudi Critical Care Society and the Scandinavian Society of Anesthesiology and Intensive Care Medicine (guideline, 2025)
- PMID 41117943: Prevalence and incidence of ICU delirium and pain: a systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2025)
- PMID 41173741: Pre-Recorded Music Interventions on Delirium in Hospitalized Adults: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis (meta-analysis, 2026)
- PMID 41405369: Association Between Acute Kidney Injury, Delirium, and Outcomes in Patients With Critical Illness: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis (meta-analysis, 2026)
- PMID 41690769: Effectiveness of drug interventions to prevent delirium after surgery for older adults: systematic review and network meta-analysis of randomised controlled trials (meta-analysis, 2026)
- PMID 41860322: Society of Critical Care Medicine Guidelines on Caring for Older Adults in the ICU (guideline, 2026)
- PMID 42090788: The Dutch Clinical Practice Guideline: Pain, analgesia/sedation, delirium, immobility and sleep disturbances; PADIS (guideline, 2026)

## Cluster `adhf` (topics 37, 38, 42) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Diuretic response in acute heart failure can be guided by urinary sodium, and acetazolamide is an effective add-on increasing decongestion without mortality change. Lung ultrasound aids diagnosis, and deprescribing diuretics is feasible in selected patients.

**Teaching points**
- Urinary sodium analysis may allow fast titration of diuretics in acute decompensated heart failure, with a point-of-care sensor enabling immediate readings. [PMID 40017142]
- Acetazolamide as add-on therapy increased diuresis, natriuresis and clinical decongestion (RR 1.35) but was not associated with mortality differences. [PMID 38985385, 38416359]
- Prehospital lung ultrasound has similar diagnostic characteristics for ADHF as in the ED, and a positive scan may justify early treatment. [PMID 38522242]
- Diuretic discontinuation could be safe and feasible in carefully selected patients, though high-quality evidence is lacking. [PMID 39117602]
- A systematic review of guidelines on diuretic appropriateness found few offered actionable deprescribing support, calling for implementation-ready guidance. [PMID 41825469]
- A European consensus provides a framework for guideline-directed therapy in patients with heart failure and obesity. [PMID 41771102]

**Uncertainties**
- Optimal diuretic strategy beyond urinary sodium guidance.

**MCQ angles**
- Acetazolamide add-on.
- Urinary sodium to titrate loop diuretics.

**Case hooks**
- Congested patient with a poor response to initial IV furosemide.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Initial diuretic dosing, haemodynamic profiles, vasodilator and inotrope use, discharge criteria and GDMT sequencing are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 38416359: Acetazolamide as an Adjunctive Diuretic Therapy for Patients with Acute Decompensated Heart Failure: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis (meta-analysis, 2024)
- PMID 38522242: Diagnostic accuracy of prehospital lung ultrasound for acute decompensated heart failure: A systematic review and Meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2024)
- PMID 38985385: Acetazolamide therapy in patients with acute heart failure: a systematic review and meta-analysis of randomized controlled trials (meta-analysis, 2024)
- PMID 39117602: The effects of diuretic deprescribing in adult patients: A systematic review to inform an evidence-based diuretic deprescribing guideline (guideline, 2025)
- PMID 40017142: Urinary sodium analysis: The key to effective diuretic titration? European Journal of Heart Failure expert consensus document (guideline, 2025)
- PMID 41771102: Implementation of guideline-directed medical therapy in patients with heart failure and obesity: European Journal of Heart Failure expert consensus document (guideline, 2026)
- PMID 41825469: Guidelines and tools to assess appropriateness of diuretic prescribing and aid deprescribing: a systematic review (guideline, 2026)

## Cluster `adem` (topics 39) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** ADEM in adults has worse outcomes than in children, with residual deficits in about half of cases. COVID-19-associated ADEM resembles classic ADEM; MOGAD is a related antibody-mediated disease with its own French and European guidance.

**Teaching points**
- Adults with ADEM had worse outcomes than children, with a mortality rate of 7.8% and a 47.5% risk of residual deficits. [PMID 35757742]
- SARS-CoV-2-associated ADEM, though rare, shares most features of classic ADEM, and ADEM and acute haemorrhagic leukoencephalitis after COVID-19 had high haemorrhage rates and poor outcomes. [PMID 34459986, 34452974]
- The EU paediatric MOG consortium proposed a clinical classification of paediatric MOGAD phenotypes. [PMID 33162302]
- French guidelines for MOG antibody-associated disease stress multidisciplinary management. [PMID 40494723]
- Neuromyelitis optica spectrum disorder consensus recommendations cover diagnosis, treatment, paediatric patients and pregnancy. [PMID 41591715]
- Brain MRI in neurologically symptomatic COVID-19 patients commonly showed infarction, olfactory bulb abnormalities, white matter abnormalities and microbleeds. [PMID 37535100]

**Uncertainties**
- Acute treatment evidence is not covered by the retrieved excerpts.

**MCQ angles**
- Post-infectious demyelination.
- MOG antibodies in ADEM.

**Case hooks**
- Child with encephalopathy and multifocal MRI lesions after a viral illness.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Diagnostic criteria, corticosteroid and IVIG treatment, and distinction from first MS attack are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 33162302: E.U. paediatric MOG consortium consensus: Part 1 - Classification of clinical phenotypes of paediatric myelin oligodendrocyte glycoprotein antibody-associated disorders (guideline, 2020)
- PMID 34452974: Acute Disseminated Encephalomyelitis and Acute Hemorrhagic Leukoencephalitis Following COVID-19: Systematic Review and Meta-synthesis (systematic review, 2021)
- PMID 34459986: SARS-CoV-2-associated acute disseminated encephalomyelitis: a systematic review of the literature (systematic review, 2022)
- PMID 35757742: Clinical Presentation and Outcomes of Acute Disseminated Encephalomyelitis in Adults Worldwide: Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis (meta-analysis, 2022)

- PMID 40494723: French guidelines for the diagnosis and management of MOG antibody-associated disease (guideline, 2025)
- PMID 41591715: Consensus Recommendations for the Diagnosis and Treatment of Neuromyelitis Optica Spectrum Disorders (NMOSD): The MENACTRIMS Guidelines (guideline, 2026)

## Cluster `acute_gout` (topics 40, 41) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Acute gout is diagnosed by synovial fluid analysis when testing is needed. ACP and APLAR guidelines cover treatment, urate-lowering therapy after discussion of benefits and harms, and long-term low-dose colchicine appears safe in patients without advanced renal or liver disease.

**Teaching points**
- ACP recommends synovial fluid analysis when clinical judgment indicates diagnostic testing is necessary in possible acute gout (weak recommendation, low-quality evidence). [PMID 27802479]
- ACP recommends discussing benefits, harms, costs and preferences before starting urate-lowering therapy with prophylaxis in patients with recurrent attacks. [PMID 27802508]
- Urate-lowering therapy decreases serum urate levels and reduces risk for acute gout attacks. [PMID 27802478]
- A consensus statement finds long-term low-dose colchicine safe in patients without advanced renal or liver disease, with no increased cancer, sepsis, cytopenia or myotoxicity risk from its biological effects. [PMID 34416165]
- The 2021 APLAR guideline includes recommendations for gout with moderate to severe renal impairment and non-pharmacologic interventions. [PMID 34931463]
- Updated Chinese guidelines prefer citrate over sodium bicarbonate for urine alkalinisation. [PMID 40692263]
- G-CAN consensus statements define labels for eight gout disease states and the elements of gout pathophysiology. [PMID 31501138, 29799677]

**Uncertainties**
- Management when colchicine is contraindicated is not covered.

**MCQ angles**
- Synovial fluid analysis for diagnosis.
- Prophylaxis while starting urate-lowering therapy.

**Case hooks**
- Hospitalised patient with acute podagra and renal impairment.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Flare treatment choices (NSAIDs, steroids, IL-1), renal dosing of colchicine, and treat-to-target urate goals are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 27802478: Management of Gout: A Systematic Review in Support of an American College of Physicians Clinical Practice Guideline (guideline, 2017)
- PMID 27802479: Diagnosis of Acute Gout: A Clinical Practice Guideline From the American College of Physicians (guideline, 2017)
- PMID 27802508: Management of Acute and Recurrent Gout: A Clinical Practice Guideline From the American College of Physicians (guideline, 2017)
- PMID 29799677: Gout, Hyperuricemia, and Crystal-Associated Disease Network Consensus Statement Regarding Labels and Definitions for Disease Elements in Gout (guideline, 2019)

## Cluster `stroke_reperf` (topics 43, 44, 75, 115) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** The 2026 AHA/ASA acute stroke guideline updates reperfusion care. Tenecteplase 0.25 mg/kg is an effective alternative to alteplase, intra-arterial thrombolysis after successful thrombectomy may help selected patients, and early DOAC initiation after stroke with AF appears safe.

**Teaching points**
- The 2026 AHA/ASA guideline modifies the approach to thrombolysis contraindications and highlights remaining knowledge gaps. [PMID 41582814]
- Tenecteplase was superior for excellent functional outcomes and not inferior for major neurological improvement or death versus alteplase, with 0.25 mg/kg the optimal dose. [PMID 40775658]
- Tenecteplase 0.25 mg/kg gives better efficacy, while alteplase remains safer for symptomatic intracranial haemorrhage, so thrombolytic selection should be individualised. [PMID 40827614]
- A network meta-analysis found alteplase 0.6 mg/kg safer, and tenecteplase 0.25 mg/kg and alteplase 0.6 mg/kg preferable beyond 4.5 hours with tissue window assessment. [PMID 41026449]
- Adjunctive intra-arterial thrombolysis after successful thrombectomy appears to benefit selected patients, such as those with higher NIHSS or cardioembolic aetiology. [PMID 40506219, 40622116]
- Early DOAC initiation after acute ischaemic stroke with non-valvular AF appears safe and potentially more effective than delayed treatment. [PMID 41591595]
- Fibrinogen depletion was seen only after alteplase and tended to accompany more major haemorrhagic complications. [PMID 40518695]

**Uncertainties**
- Optimal selection for adjunctive intra-arterial thrombolysis.

**MCQ angles**
- Tenecteplase versus alteplase dose.
- Timing of anticoagulation after stroke with AF.

**Case hooks**
- Large vessel occlusion presenting at 5 hours with salvageable tissue on imaging.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Time windows, imaging selection criteria, blood pressure targets and thrombectomy trial results (DAWN, DEFUSE-3) are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 40506219: Adjunctive intra-arterial thrombolysis after successful endovascular thrombectomy for large vessel occlusion: Meta-analysis of outcomes, dosage, and patient selection (meta-analysis, 2026)
- PMID 40518695: Fibrinogen time course in acute ischemic stroke patients treated with intravenous thrombolysis with alteplase or tenecteplase (meta-analysis, 2025)
- PMID 40622116: The Added Benefit of Intra-Arterial Thrombolysis After Successful Recanalization by Endovascular Treatment: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis of Randomized-Controlled Clinical Trials (meta-analysis, 2025)
- PMID 40775658: Tenecteplase versus alteplase in patients with acute ischemic stroke: an updated systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2025)
- PMID 40827614: Comparative Efficacy and Safety of Different Tenecteplase Doses With Alteplase in Acute Ischemic Stroke: A Systematic Review With Pairwise and Network Meta-Analysis to Determine the Optimal Dose (meta-analysis, 2025)
- PMID 41026449: Safety Outcomes Following Intravenous Thrombolysis in Acute Ischemic Stroke: A Systematic Review and Network Meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2026)
- PMID 41582814: 2026 Guideline for the Early Management of Patients With Acute Ischemic Stroke: A Guideline From the American Heart Association/American Stroke Association (guideline, 2026)
- PMID 41591595: Timing of anticoagulation therapy in patients with acute ischemic stroke and atrial fibrillation: a GRADE-based expert opinion recommendation (guideline, 2026)
