## Cluster `mech_valve` (topics 148) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Warfarin remains the anticoagulant for mechanical valves; routine aspirin use beyond guideline indications adds bleeding. DOAC use in mechanical valves is under review, and low-dose vitamin K for supratherapeutic INR has not been shown to cause thromboembolism.

**Teaching points**
- A significant proportion of patients on warfarin for mechanical valves take guideline-discordant aspirin, and aspirin deprescribing in selected patients may safely reduce bleeding events. [PMID 38280559]
- A systematic review and meta-analysis of vitamin K in mechanical valve patients with supratherapeutic INR addressed the thromboembolism risk of low-dose vitamin K, and more well-designed studies are needed. [PMID 36086772]
- A review compares direct-acting oral anticoagulants versus warfarin in mechanical valve patients, with findings needing exploration in specific subgroups. [PMID 40525009]
- Portable coagulometers with internet connectivity offer a new model of warfarin management after mechanical valve replacement, improving follow-up and compliance. [PMID 36311931]
- In pregnancy with a mechanical valve on non-warfarin anticoagulation, valve thrombosis may present as acute heart failure, and surgery should be prompt if needed. [PMID 33678660]
- In mechanical aortic valve patients with warfarin hypersensitivity due to an F9 variant, factor Xa or thrombin inhibitors deserve further investigation. [PMID 40015335]

**Uncertainties**
- DOACs in mechanical valves remain unproven.

**MCQ angles**
- Avoid unnecessary aspirin with warfarin.
- Mechanical valve thrombosis in pregnancy.

**Case hooks**
- Mechanical mitral valve patient with INR of 8 and no bleeding.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** INR targets by valve position, bridging protocols and DOAC contraindication evidence (RE-ALIGN, PROACT Xa) are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 33678660: Mechanical heart valve thrombosis during pregnancy under non<b>-</b>warfarin anticoagulation therapy: Two<b>-</b>case report and literature review (review, 2021)
- PMID 36086772: Safety of Vitamin K in mechanical heart valve patients with supratherapeutic INR: A systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2022)
- PMID 36311931: Combining portable coagulometers with the Internet: A new model of warfarin anticoagulation in patients following mechanical heart valve replacement (review, 2022)
- PMID 38280559: Prevalence of Guideline-Discordant Aspirin Use and Associated Adverse Events in Patients on Warfarin for Mechanical Valve Replacement (guideline, 2024)
- PMID 40015335: Use of Oral Anti-Xa Inhibitor in Prosthetic Mechanical Aortic Valve with Warfarin Hypersensitivity Due to the FIX p.(Ala37Thr) Propeptide Variant: Case Report and Literature Review (review, 2025)
- PMID 40525009: Efficacy and Safety of Direct-Acting Oral Anticoagulants Versus Warfarin in Patients With Mechanical Valve Replacement (review, 2025)

## Cluster `cancer_anticoag` (topics 150, 298, 340) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** For cancer-associated VTE, LMWH or DOACs (apixaban, rivaroxaban) are used, with choice by bleeding risk, drug interactions and preference; anticoagulation continues beyond six months in active cancer. Thromboprophylaxis can be given in selected ambulatory patients.

**Teaching points**
- The BSH guideline recommends continuing anticoagulation beyond 6 months for cancer-associated VTE with active cancer, with drug choice guided by interactions and patient preference. [PMID 38664942]
- The 2022 international guideline suggests LMWH or DOACs (rivaroxaban or apixaban) for ambulatory patients with locally advanced or metastatic pancreatic cancer on anticancer therapy and low bleeding risk. [PMID 35772465]
- A meta-analysis found DOACs versus LMWH for cancer thrombosis associated with significantly increased major bleeding and a trend to more clinically relevant non-major bleeding, with small absolute differences (2-3%). [PMID 29506866]
- The Canadian treatment algorithm tailors anticoagulation to cancer type, bleeding risk and drug interactions, and adapts therapy as cancer status changes. [PMID 34940092, 30464682]
- Guideline updates added DOACs as an option for VTE management in cancer patients. [PMID 32736784]
- An implementation model delivered VTE risk assessment and prophylaxis to prevent cancer-associated thrombosis in outpatients. [PMID 32267798]
- Multidisciplinary awareness programmes are needed to improve guideline adherence in cancer-associated VTE. [PMID 27566698]

**Uncertainties**
- Choice of DOAC versus LMWH in GI and genitourinary cancers.

**MCQ angles**
- Duration beyond 6 months in active cancer.
- DOAC bleeding risk.

**Case hooks**
- Pancreatic cancer patient with a new PE.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Catheter-related thrombosis management, dosing and thrombocytopenia adjustments are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 27566698: Factors influencing adherence to clinical guidelines in the management of cancer-associated thrombosis (guideline, 2016)
- PMID 29506866: Direct oral anticoagulant (DOAC) versus low-molecular-weight heparin (LMWH) for treatment of cancer associated thrombosis (CAT): A systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2019)
- PMID 30464682: Treatment algorithm in cancer-associated thrombosis: Canadian expert consensus (guideline, 2018)
- PMID 32267798: Successful Model for Guideline Implementation to Prevent Cancer-Associated Thrombosis: Venous Thromboembolism Prevention in the Ambulatory Cancer Clinic (guideline, 2020)
- PMID 32736784: Clinical practice guidelines for the treatment and prevention of cancer-associated thrombosis (guideline, 2020)
- PMID 34940092: Treatment Algorithm in Cancer-Associated Thrombosis: Updated Canadian Expert Consensus (guideline, 2021)
- PMID 35772465: 2022 international clinical practice guidelines for the treatment and prophylaxis of venous thromboembolism in patients with cancer, including patients with COVID-19 (guideline, 2022)
- PMID 38664942: Cancer-associated venous thrombosis in adults (second edition): A British Society for Haematology Guideline (guideline, 2024)

## Cluster `anticoag_pregnancy` (topics 151) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Pregnancy VTE guidance varies across international guidelines and evidence specific to pregnancy is limited. D-dimer can safely rule out VTE at low or intermediate pretest probability, and ASH advises antepartum prophylaxis for prior unprovoked or hormone-associated VTE.

**Teaching points**
- ASH 2018 gives a conditional recommendation for antepartum anticoagulant prophylaxis with a history of unprovoked or hormone-associated VTE and against it for prior VTE with a resolved non-hormonal provoking factor. [PMID 30482767]
- A meta-analysis suggests D-dimer allows safe exclusion of VTE in pregnant women with suspected VTE and low/intermediate or unlikely pretest probability. [PMID 34161671]
- Critical appraisal of international guidelines found inconsistencies in prevention and treatment recommendations for pregnancy-associated VTE and a need for high-quality pregnancy-specific evidence. [PMID 31419948]
- In women with persistent antiphospholipid antibodies, evidence about aspirin or heparin for pregnancy outcomes is uncertain. [PMID 32358837]
- VTE incidence during pregnancy and postpartum is relatively low, but the clinical burden is high. [PMID 26489486]
- A position paper establishes a more uniform strategy for diagnosing pregnancy-associated VTE. [PMID 27058795]
- A position paper provides recommendations for pregnancy-associated superficial venous thrombosis. [PMID 35187959]

**Uncertainties**
- Role of low-dose aspirin in antiphospholipid pregnancy.

**MCQ angles**
- D-dimer in pregnancy.
- Prophylaxis for prior hormone-associated VTE.

**Case hooks**
- Pregnant woman with previous unprovoked DVT.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** LMWH dosing in pregnancy, peripartum timing, warfarin avoidance and mechanical valve management are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 26489486: A systematic review and meta-analysis of the epidemiology and burden of venous thromboembolism among pregnant women (meta-analysis, 2016)
- PMID 27058795: Diagnosis of pregnancy-associated venous thromboembolism - position paper of the Working Group in Women's Health of the Society of Thrombosis and Haemostasis (GTH) (guideline, 2016)
- PMID 30482767: American Society of Hematology 2018 guidelines for management of venous thromboembolism: venous thromboembolism in the context of pregnancy (guideline, 2018)
- PMID 31419948: Critical appraisal of international guidelines for the prevention and treatment of pregnancy-associated venous thromboembolism: a systematic review (guideline, 2019)
- PMID 32358837: Aspirin or heparin or both for improving pregnancy outcomes in women with persistent antiphospholipid antibodies and recurrent pregnancy loss (meta-analysis, 2020)
- PMID 34161671: D-dimer to rule out venous thromboembolism during pregnancy: A systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2021)
- PMID 35187959: Position Paper on the Management of Pregnancy-Associated Superficial Venous Thrombosis. Balkan Working Group for Prevention and Treatment of Venous Thromboembolism (guideline, 2022)

## Cluster `anticoag_reversal` (topics 152, 153, 154) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** DOAC reversal depends on the clinical scenario: clinical guidelines and emergency consensus recommend specific antidotes in life-threatening bleeding, and no recommendation can be given for recombinant factor VIIa as a nonspecific agent. Periprocedural resumption evidence is insufficient to favour same-day over later restart.

**Teaching points**
- A clinical guideline on reversal of DOACs in life-threatening bleeding gives no recommendation for recombinant factor VIIa as a nonspecific haemostatic agent due to scarce data. [PMID 38567679]
- A European emergency consensus describes specific antidotes for major bleeding in anticoagulated patients, with strategies for traumatic, intracranial and gastrointestinal bleeding. [PMID 37427548]
- The ESO guideline on reversal of oral anticoagulants in intracerebral haemorrhage aims to normalise coagulation, with no direct data on functional outcome or mortality. [PMID 31903428]
- The ACG-CAG guideline could not recommend same-day versus 1-7 day resumption of anticoagulants or P2Y12 inhibitors after endoscopic procedures because of insufficient evidence. [PMID 35297395, 35368325]
- Recommendations for management of cardiac surgery patients on DOACs note that emergency cases need particular attention. [PMID 37812245]
- ICSH recommendations address laboratory measurement of DOACs based on publications, expert opinion and good laboratory practice. [PMID 33742436]

**Uncertainties**
- Functional outcome benefit of reversal.

**MCQ angles**
- Specific antidotes for life-threatening bleeds.
- Restart timing after procedures.

**Case hooks**
- Patient on apixaban with intracerebral haemorrhage.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Idarucizumab and andexanet doses, prothrombin complex concentrate use for warfarin, vitamin K and bridging rules are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 31903428: European Stroke Organisation Guideline on Reversal of Oral Anticoagulants in Acute Intracerebral Haemorrhage (guideline, 2019)
- PMID 33742436: 2021 Update of the International Council for Standardization in Haematology Recommendations for Laboratory Measurement of Direct Oral Anticoagulants (guideline, 2021)
- PMID 35297395: American College of Gastroenterology-Canadian Association of Gastroenterology Clinical Practice Guideline: Management of Anticoagulants and Antiplatelets During Acute Gastrointestinal Bleeding and the Periendoscopic Period (guideline, 2022)
- PMID 35368325: American College of Gastroenterology-Canadian Association of Gastroenterology Clinical Practice Guideline: Management of Anticoagulants and Antiplatelets During Acute Gastrointestinal Bleeding and the Periendoscopic Period (guideline, 2022)
- PMID 37427548: Management of major bleeding for anticoagulated patients in the Emergency Department: an European experts consensus statement (guideline, 2023)
- PMID 37812245: Optimal management of cardiac surgery patients using direct oral anticoagulants: recommendations for clinical practice (guideline, 2023)
- PMID 38567679: Clinical guideline on reversal of direct oral anticoagulants in patients with life threatening bleeding (guideline, 2024)

## Cluster `antidepressant_steps` (topics 155) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** For treatment-resistant depression, ketamine/esketamine and neuromodulation (deep TMS) have supportive meta-analytic evidence, although ketamine did not reduce suicidal ideation in one analysis and DBS shows no sham-controlled benefit. Augmentation and switching strategies compare by relative effectiveness.

**Teaching points**
- A meta-analysis of relative effectiveness in treatment-resistant depression identified treatments with clear benefit, including ketamine and neurostimulation, with others (minocycline, aripiprazole) promising for some outcomes. [PMID 39739012]
- The number needed to treat for ketamine in adults with treatment-resistant depression was below 10 across observation intervals. [PMID 38636712]
- Deep TMS is an effective and safe strategy for treatment-resistant depression with similar adverse event rates to sham. [PMID 38574492]
- Ketamine and esketamine failed to reduce suicidal ideation in patients with treatment-resistant depression in one meta-analysis. [PMID 38117332]
- Deep brain stimulation for treatment-resistant depression has limited sham-controlled data and does not show significant improvement over placebo. [PMID 39197490]
- Esketamine carries abuse potential and unknown long-term effects, with signals regarding deaths and suicidality in testing. [PMID 39876682]
- Psilocybin shows a time-dependent therapeutic effect in major depressive disorder in a dose-response network meta-analysis. [PMID 39754904]
- Psychedelic-induced psychosis evidence is of low quality, and a conservative approach is recommended until more data are available. [PMID 39592825]

**Uncertainties**
- Long-term safety of esketamine.

**MCQ angles**
- Neuromodulation options for TRD.
- Ketamine effect size and suicidality.

**Case hooks**
- Patient who failed two antidepressant trials considering esketamine.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** STAR*D sequential steps (switch vs augment with lithium, bupropion, buspirone, CBT) and first-line SSRI choice are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 38117332: A systematic review and meta-analysis of the efficacy of ketamine and esketamine on suicidal ideation in treatment-resistant depression (meta-analysis, 2024)
- PMID 38574492: Deep transcranial magnetic stimulation for treatment-resistant depression: A systematic review and meta-analysis of randomized controlled studies (meta-analysis, 2024)
- PMID 38636712: Number needed to treat (NNT) for ketamine and esketamine in adults with treatment-resistant depression: A systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2024)
- PMID 39197490: Efficacy of Deep Brain Stimulation for Treatment-Resistant Depression: Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis (meta-analysis, 2024)
- PMID 39592825: Reconsidering evidence for psychedelic-induced psychosis: an overview of reviews, a systematic review, and meta-analysis of human studies (meta-analysis, 2025)
- PMID 39739012: Relative effectiveness of antidepressant treatments in treatment-resistant depression: a systematic review and network meta-analysis of randomized controlled trials (meta-analysis, 2025)
- PMID 39754904: Efficacy and safety of psilocybin in the treatment of Major Depressive Disorder (MDD): A dose-response network meta-analysis of randomized placebo-controlled clinical trials (meta-analysis, 2025)
- PMID 39876682: Esketamine Treatment for Depression in Adults: A PRISMA Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis (meta-analysis, 2025)

## Cluster `aed_choice` (topics 156) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Antiseizure medication choice is shaped by pregnancy risk, drug interactions and adverse effects. Folic acid of at least 0.4 mg daily is advised for people with epilepsy of child-bearing potential on ASMs, carbamazepine should not be co-prescribed with clozapine, and cannabidiol can cause liver injury.

**Teaching points**
- Clinicians should prescribe at least 0.4 mg of folic acid daily before and during pregnancy to people with epilepsy of child-bearing potential treated with an antiseizure medication, to reduce neural tube defects and possibly improve neurodevelopmental outcomes. [PMID 38748979]
- Women with epilepsy have worse perinatal outcomes and should receive pregnancy counselling from an epilepsy specialist who can optimise their antiseizure regimen. [PMID 36912826]
- Carbamazepine should not be co-prescribed with clozapine because of agranulocytosis risk and induction of clozapine metabolism. [PMID 38613254]
- Cannabidiol-associated liver enzyme elevations and drug-induced liver injury are common adverse events, so liver function should be monitored. [PMID 36912195]
- After paediatric epilepsy surgery with seizure freedom, ASM cessation allows optimisation of intellectual and developmental trajectories. [PMID 38031640]
- ILAE-linked guidance on neonatal seizures recommends a standardised pathway for managing neonatal seizures in each neonatal unit. [PMID 37655702]
- A national Delphi consensus in Egypt adapts epilepsy management to resource-limited settings. [PMID 38790148]

**Uncertainties**
- Syndrome-specific first-line choices are not in the retrieved excerpts.

**MCQ angles**
- Folic acid dose with ASMs.
- Carbamazepine-clozapine interaction.

**Case hooks**
- Young woman with epilepsy planning pregnancy on valproate.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** First-line drug by seizure type, valproate teratogenicity, enzyme-inducer interactions, withdrawal and driving rules are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 36912195: Cannabidiol-associated hepatotoxicity: A systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2023)
- PMID 36912826: Comparison of Perinatal Outcomes for Women With and Without Epilepsy: A Systematic Review and Meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2023)
- PMID 37655702: Treatment of seizures in the neonate: Guidelines and consensus-based recommendations-Special report from the ILAE Task Force on Neonatal Seizures (guideline, 2023)
- PMID 38031640: Long-term intellectual and developmental outcomes after pediatric epilepsy surgery: A systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2024)
- PMID 38613254: Optimizing co-prescription of clozapine and antiseizure medications: a systematic review and expert recommendations for clinical practice (guideline, 2024)
- PMID 38748979: Teratogenesis, Perinatal, and Neurodevelopmental Outcomes After In Utero Exposure to Antiseizure Medication: Practice Guideline From the AAN, AES, and SMFM (guideline, 2024)
- PMID 38790148: Consensus Guideline on the Management of Epilepsy in Egypt: A National Delphi Consensus Study (guideline, 2024)

## Cluster `antifungal` (topics 157, 158) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Isavuconazole and voriconazole are first-line for invasive pulmonary aspergillosis in ICU, with liposomal amphotericin B as an alternative. Global guidelines (ECMM) update candidiasis management, and antifungal prophylaxis is advised in severe aplastic anaemia with neutropenia.

**Teaching points**
- For invasive aspergillosis in ICU, isavuconazole and voriconazole are recommended first line, with liposomal amphotericin B as an alternative. [PMID 40465080]
- A fungal infection study group provides guidelines on epidemiology, diagnosis, definitions and therapy of invasive pulmonary aspergillosis in ICU. [PMID 41311305]
- The ECMM global guideline updates recommendations for managing Candida infections, addressing emerging pathogens and new treatment options. [PMID 39956121]
- Spanish guidelines address treatment of invasive pulmonary aspergillosis and preventive and empirical therapy for invasive candidiasis, based largely on low-quality evidence. [PMID 39556361]
- The British Society for Medical Mycology gives best-practice diagnostic recommendations for serious fungal disease with a focus on fungal stewardship. [PMID 41232547]
- The 2026 ASH guideline recommends antibiotic and antifungal prophylaxis for patients with severe aplastic anaemia who are neutropenic. [PMID 41920091]
- Trichophyton indotineae infections have a median time to complete clinical cure of 11.5 weeks with 19.7% recurrence, in the context of antifungal resistance. [PMID 40186426]
- A consensus guideline on tinea capitis addresses measures to interrupt chains of infection, including treating asymptomatic carriers. [PMID 42444404]

**Uncertainties**
- Evidence for several ICU antifungal strategies is low quality.

**MCQ angles**
- First-line therapy for invasive aspergillosis.
- Antifungal resistance.

**Case hooks**
- Ventilated patient with severe influenza and new cavitating pulmonary lesions.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Mechanisms of azoles, echinocandins and polyenes, candidaemia first-line agent, drug interactions and TDM are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 39556361: Treatment of Invasive Pulmonary Aspergillosis and Preventive and Empirical Therapy for Invasive Candidiasis in Adult Pulmonary and Critical Care Patients: An Official American Thoracic Society Clinical Practice Guideline (guideline, 2025)
- PMID 39956121: Global guideline for the diagnosis and management of candidiasis: an initiative of the ECMM in cooperation with ISHAM and ASM (guideline, 2025)
- PMID 40186426: Therapeutic Outcomes in Patients With Trichophyton indotineae: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis of Individual Patient Data (meta-analysis, 2025)
- PMID 40465080: Diagnosis and treatment of invasive pulmonary aspergillosis in critically ill intensive care patients: executive summary of the German national guideline (AWMF 113-005) (guideline, 2025)
- PMID 41232547: British Society for Medical Mycology best practice recommendations for the diagnosis of serious fungal diseases: 2025 update (guideline, 2026)
- PMID 41311305: Management of Invasive Pulmonary Aspergillosis in Intensive Care Units: Guidelines From the Fungal Infection Network of Switzerland (FUNGINOS) (guideline, 2025)
- PMID 41920091: American Society of Hematology 2026 guidelines for the diagnosis and management of severe and very severe acquired aplastic anemia (guideline, 2026)
- PMID 42444404: Consensus-based Guideline on tinea capitis (guideline, 2026)

## Cluster `antigbm` (topics 159) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Anti-GBM disease is rare with high renal risk. KDIGO 2021 covers glomerular disease management, double-positive ANCA/anti-GBM cases are difficult to treat, and kidney transplant should wait for 6 months of anti-GBM antibody negativity.

**Teaching points**
- The KDIGO 2021 glomerular diseases guideline grades evidence and recommendations including for anti-GBM disease. [PMID 34556300]
- Kidney transplantation in anti-GBM disease should be performed 6 months after absence of anti-GBM antibodies. [PMID 34826843]
- Anti-GBM antibodies show high sensitivity and specificity for diagnosing anti-GBM disease, with cohort studies needed to compare immunoassays. [PMID 34515033]
- ANCA and anti-GBM double-positive patients are difficult to treat, supporting simultaneous antibody testing and systematic renal biopsy in rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis. [PMID 34242834]
- A meta-analysis of anti-GBM disease provides epidemiology, clinical features, risk factors and outcomes, with caution due to observational design. [PMID 38493958]
- About one in four children with crescentic glomerulonephritis progress to kidney failure. [PMID 39278987]
- A core curriculum on therapeutic plasma exchange discusses its use from a nephrologist's perspective given limited supporting evidence. [PMID 36775691]
- An international consensus recommends indirect immunofluorescence for ANCA testing beyond systemic vasculitis. [PMID 32663621]

**Uncertainties**
- Evidence for plasma exchange rests on limited data.

**MCQ angles**
- Wait 6 months of antibody negativity before transplant.
- Double-positive disease.

**Case hooks**
- Young smoker with haemoptysis and rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Plasma exchange, cyclophosphamide and steroid regimens and alveolar haemorrhage management are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**

## Cluster `anti_il6r` (topics 160) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** IL-6 receptor blockade is effective in difficult-to-treat RA (tocilizumab 8 mg/4 weeks may be first choice), giant cell arteritis and refractory uveitis, and in hospitalised moderate-to-severe COVID-19. Large-vessel vasculitis guidelines are updated, and upadacitinib is emerging in GCA.

**Teaching points**
- Tocilizumab and rituximab have better efficacy and safety in difficult-to-treat RA, with tocilizumab 8 mg every 4 weeks possibly first choice for remission. [PMID 39198829]
- Tocilizumab is a therapeutic option for refractory non-infectious uveitis after failure of conventional immunosuppressants and anti-TNF agents. [PMID 41424786]
- A systematic review informing EULAR PMR/GCA recommendations found multiple trials supporting IL-6, IL-17 and JAK inhibition and B-cell depletion in GCA and PMR, though robust Takayasu trials are lacking. [PMID 42856880]
- French recommendations standardise use of immunosuppressants and biologics in giant cell arteritis. [PMID 39500637]
- Turkish recommendations note upadacitinib seems promising in GCA but needs more real-life safety data in the elderly with high cardiovascular risk. [PMID 41930660]
- A systematic review supports the 2026 JCS guideline on large-vessel vasculitis, with comparable adverse event rates between treatment groups. [PMID 41692437]
- Tocilizumab and sarilumab are effective in moderate-to-severe COVID-19 without burden of serious adverse events. [PMID 35343397]
- Several pharmacological interventions are efficacious and generally safe for managing fatigue in inflammatory rheumatic diseases. [PMID 38056919]

**Uncertainties**
- Takayasu arteritis evidence.

**MCQ angles**
- Tocilizumab in GCA and RA.
- Anti-IL-6 in refractory uveitis.

**Case hooks**
- GCA patient relapsing on tapering prednisolone.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Infection, lipid and liver monitoring, neutropenia and gastrointestinal perforation risk are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 35343397: Clinical efficacy and safety of interleukin-6 receptor antagonists (tocilizumab and sarilumab) in patients with COVID-19: a systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2022)
- PMID 38056919: Efficacy of pharmacological interventions: a systematic review informing the 2023 EULAR recommendations for the management of fatigue in people with inflammatory rheumatic and musculoskeletal diseases (guideline, 2023)
- PMID 39198829: Efficacy and safety of current therapies for difficult-to-treat rheumatoid arthritis: a systematic review and network meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2024)
- PMID 39500637: Use of immunosuppressants and biologics in giant cell arteritis: Recommendations of the French Study Group for Large Vessel Vasculitis (GEFA) (guideline, 2025)
- PMID 41424786: Effectiveness and safety of tocilizumab in refractory noninfectious uveitis: a systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2025)
- PMID 41692437: Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis for JCS 2026 Guideline on Management of Large-Vessel Vasculitis (guideline, 2026)
- PMID 41930660: Turkish Society for Rheumatology recommendations for the diagnosis, follow-up and management of giant cell arteritis (guideline, 2026)
- PMID 42856880: A systematic literature review to inform the 2025 EULAR recommendations for the management of polymyalgia rheumatica and primary large vessel vasculitis: management of disease including relapse and complications (guideline, 2026)

## Cluster `amr` (topics 161, 162) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** A 2025 practice guideline gives pathogen-specific recommendations for multidrug-resistant Gram-negative infections, and Dutch guidance covers MDRO control in hospitals. Carbapenem-resistant hypervirulent Klebsiella carries a mortality threat, and COVID-19 altered MDR prevalence differently across regions.

**Teaching points**
- A guideline for antimicrobial treatment of multidrug-resistant Gram-negative infections organises therapeutic recommendations by pathogen and infection site, including bloodstream, intra-abdominal, urinary and respiratory infections. [PMID 41130038]
- The Dutch guideline for preventing and controlling MDROs in hospitals gives practical recommendations to mitigate transmission and sustain low resistance levels. [PMID 41204306]
- The mortality risk of carbapenem-resistant hypervirulent Klebsiella pneumoniae was compared with classic strains, supporting identification strategies and targeted infection control. [PMID 41283021]
- COVID-19 affected the prevalence of multidrug-resistant bacteria differently by region and healthcare facility. [PMID 41045404]
- The AUA/CUA/SUFU guideline update on recurrent uncomplicated UTI in women reduces reliance on antimicrobial detection and emphasises clinician judgement on individual risk and benefit. [PMID 40905426]
- The 2026 EAU guidelines summarise classification, diagnosis and treatment of urological infections. [PMID 42457453]

**Uncertainties**
- Local resistance patterns determine treatment.

**MCQ angles**
- Pathogen-specific MDR Gram-negative therapy.
- Infection control to limit MDRO spread.

**Case hooks**
- ICU patient colonised with a carbapenem-resistant Enterobacterales.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Resistance mechanisms (ESBL, AmpC, carbapenemases), drug choices by enzyme class, and burden statistics are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 40905426: Updates to Recurrent Uncomplicated Urinary Tract Infections in Women: AUA/CUA/SUFU Guideline (2025) (guideline, 2026)
- PMID 41045404: Impact of COVID-19 on the prevalence of multi-drug-resistant bacteria: a literature review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2025)

## Cluster `aps` (topics 165, 166, 167) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** In antiphospholipid syndrome, DOACs carry increased arterial thrombosis risk versus VKAs, so warfarin remains standard. Triple positivity and lupus anticoagulant predict adverse pregnancy outcomes, and aPS/PT antibodies are common in APS.

**Teaching points**
- Patients with antiphospholipid syndrome randomised to DOACs compared with VKAs have increased risk of arterial thrombosis, with no significant difference in subsequent VTE or major bleeding. [PMID 36328154]
- Triple positivity, lupus anticoagulant positivity and laboratory category I are the most important predictors of adverse pregnancy outcomes in APS. [PMID 34280554]
- In SLE, predictors of adverse pregnancy outcomes include disease activity at conception and secondary antiphospholipid syndrome. [PMID 39153486]
- Phosphatidylserine/prothrombin antibodies are frequently positive in APS, especially in lupus anticoagulant positive patients, but their classification role needs study. [PMID 35526513]
- For recurrent VTE on anticoagulation, evidence is minimal and expert-based, with options to switch or intensify anticoagulant therapy. [PMID 38688270]
- The RCOG guideline on recurrent miscarriage includes supportive care and treatments such as progesterone for bleeding in early pregnancy. [PMID 37334488]
- The Canadian recurrent pregnancy loss guideline stresses trauma-informed care and shared decision-making. [PMID 41176277]

**Uncertainties**
- Optimal anticoagulation after recurrent VTE in APS.

**MCQ angles**
- Avoid DOACs in triple-positive APS.
- Pregnancy risk predictors.

**Case hooks**
- Young woman with stroke, livedo and lupus anticoagulant.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Classification criteria (Sapporo/Sydney, ACR-EULAR 2023), INR targets, LMWH plus aspirin in pregnancy and catastrophic APS are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 34280554: Pregnancy outcome predictors in antiphospholipid syndrome: A systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2021)
- PMID 35526513: Prevalence of aPhosphatidylserine/prothrombin antibodies and association with antiphospholipid antibody profiles in patients with antiphospholipid syndrome: A systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2022)
- PMID 36328154: Direct Oral Anticoagulants vs Vitamin K Antagonists in Patients With Antiphospholipid Syndromes: Meta-Analysis of Randomized Trials (meta-analysis, 2023)
- PMID 37334488: Recurrent MiscarriageGreen-top Guideline No. 17 (guideline, 2023)
- PMID 38688270: Recurrent Venous Thromboembolism in Patients on Anticoagulation: An Update Based on the Revised AWMF S2k Guideline (guideline, 2024)
- PMID 39153486: Pregnancy outcome predictors in systemic lupus erythematosus: a systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2024)
- PMID 41176277: Guideline No. 464: Recurrent Pregnancy Loss (guideline, 2025)

## Cluster `antipsychotic_metabolic` (topics 168) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Metformin is recommended for preventing and treating antipsychotic-induced weight gain, with consensus guidelines for co-commencement. GLP-1 agonists are promising, and second-generation antipsychotics differ in weight-gain propensity and dose-response.

**Teaching points**
- A consensus-validated guideline recommends metformin for preventing antipsychotic-induced weight gain, including co-commencement. [PMID 39657713]
- Metformin is effective for treating antipsychotic-induced weight gain in schizophrenia or schizoaffective disorder, but not for fasting glucose in one analysis. [PMID 27716110]
- The BAP guideline covers management of weight gain, metabolic disturbance and cardiovascular risk associated with psychotropic drugs. [PMID 27147592]
- Second-generation antipsychotics differ in propensity to produce weight gain and in the shapes of their dose-response curves. [PMID 35137229]
- GLP-1 agonists such as semaglutide and liraglutide are promising for antipsychotic-induced weight gain, with acceptable adverse effects and no worsening of psychopathology. [PMID 39048532]
- Antipsychotic-related weight gain is polygenic and associated with variants in genes coding for pharmacodynamic targets. [PMID 27217270]
- A meta-analysis described metabolic risk changes with second-generation antipsychotics from start of treatment to end of follow-up. [PMID 33686614]
- UK recommendations support optimised use of metformin when managing antipsychotic-induced weight gain. [PMID 34588212]

**Uncertainties**
- Long-term outcomes of GLP-1 agonists in psychosis.

**MCQ angles**
- Metformin for antipsychotic weight gain.
- Choice of antipsychotic by metabolic risk.

**Case hooks**
- Patient gaining weight rapidly after starting olanzapine.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Monitoring schedule (weight, waist, glucose, lipids), thresholds for switching and cardiovascular risk calculation are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 27147592: BAP guidelines on the management of weight gain, metabolic disturbances and cardiovascular risk associated with psychosis and antipsychotic drug treatment (guideline, 2016)
- PMID 27217270: Pharmacogenetic Associations of Antipsychotic Drug-Related Weight Gain: A Systematic Review and Meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2016)
- PMID 27716110: Metformin in prevention and treatment of antipsychotic induced weight gain: a systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2016)
- PMID 33686614: Second-Generation Antipsychotic Drugs for Patients with Schizophrenia: Systematic Literature Review and Meta-analysis of Metabolic and Cardiovascular Side Effects (meta-analysis, 2021)
- PMID 34588212: Metformin in the management of antipsychotic-induced weight gain in adults with psychosis: development of the first evidence-based guideline using GRADE methodology (guideline, 2022)
- PMID 35137229: Antipsychotic-Induced Weight Gain: Dose-Response Meta-Analysis of Randomized Controlled Trials (meta-analysis, 2022)
- PMID 39048532: Glucagon-like peptide agonists for weight management in antipsychotic-induced weight gain: A systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2024)
- PMID 39657713: Metformin for the Prevention of Antipsychotic-Induced Weight Gain: Guideline Development and Consensus Validation (guideline, 2025)

## Cluster `tavr_antithrombotic` (topics 169, 173, 174) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Mid-term outcomes of TAVR and SAVR in low-risk severe aortic stenosis are similar, though pacemaker implantation is more common with TAVR. Cerebral embolic protection is not supported routinely, and urgent TAVI lowers mortality compared with balloon valvuloplasty.

**Teaching points**
- Mid-term mortality and disabling stroke rates in severe aortic stenosis were similar with transcatheter and surgical replacement in low-risk patients. [PMID 41196804]
- Current evidence does not support routine use of cerebral embolic protection during TAVR given lack of significant benefit in key outcomes. [PMID 41729166, 40910674]
- Urgent TAVI reduced in-hospital and 30-day mortality compared with balloon aortic valvuloplasty, though larger studies are needed. [PMID 40988363]
- Sutureless AVR had lower 30-day and one-year mortality and fewer valvular leaks than TAVR in a meta-analysis, with similar in-hospital and two-year mortality. [PMID 41453511]
- Unplanned conversion to cardiac surgery during TAVI is not increased in urgent versus elective cases, though AKI and longer stay suggest patient factors contribute to adverse outcomes. [PMID 41214437]
- SGLT2 inhibitor benefit after TAVI rests on limited observational evidence and needs randomised trials. [PMID 42056230]
- An Italian interdisciplinary consensus addresses anaesthesia for transfemoral TAVI, with individualised decision-making. [PMID 41483996]

**Uncertainties**
- Optimal antithrombotic therapy after TAVR is not covered by the retrieved excerpts.

**MCQ angles**
- Cerebral embolic protection is not routine.
- Urgent TAVI versus valvuloplasty.

**Case hooks**
- Low-risk 72-year-old with severe aortic stenosis choosing TAVR or SAVR.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Antithrombotic regimen after TAVR (single antiplatelet versus anticoagulant), grading severity, low-flow low-gradient aortic stenosis and indications for intervention are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 40910674: Efficacy of Cerebral Embolic Protection Device in Transcatheter Aortic Valve Replacement: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis (meta-analysis, 2025)
- PMID 40988363: Direct Comparison of Urgent Transcatheter Aortic Valve Implantation and Balloon Aortic Valvuloplasty: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis (meta-analysis, 2025)
- PMID 41196804: Mid-term Outcomes of Transcatheter Aortic Valve Replacement vs. Surgical Aortic Valve Replacement in Low-to-Moderate Risk Patients with Severe Aortic Stenosis: A Systematic Review and Meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2026)
- PMID 41214437: Conversion to Cardiac Surgery During Elective and Urgent Transcatheter Aortic Valve Implantation: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis (meta-analysis, 2026)
- PMID 41453511: Sutureless versus transcatheter aortic valve replacement in patients with aortic stenosis: An updated meta-analysis of short- and mid-term outcomes with trial sequential analysis (meta-analysis, 2026)
- PMID 41483996: Management of anaesthesia for transfemoral transcatheter aortic valve implantation: an Italian interdisciplinary consensus statement (guideline, 2026)
- PMID 41729166: The efficacy and safety of cerebral embolic protection devices in patients undergoing transcatheter aortic valve replacement: a systematic review and meta-analysis with trial sequential analysis of randomized controlled trials (meta-analysis, 2026)
- PMID 42056230: Therapeutic impact of SGLT2 inhibitors following transcatheter aortic valve implantation: a grade-assessed systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2026)

## Cluster `anxiety` (topics 170, 171) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Retrieved evidence suggests brain stimulation (low-frequency rTMS most promising) is effective for anxiety disorders, OCD and PTSD, and cannabidiol may help social anxiety in small samples. Pharmacogenetic guidance exists for tricyclics, and childhood trauma predicts later anxiety disorders.

**Teaching points**
- Brain stimulation techniques are effective for anxiety disorders, OCD and PTSD, with low-frequency rTMS the most promising option. [PMID 39477076]
- Cannabidiol shows therapeutic potential in anxiety disorders, but findings should be interpreted cautiously given the limited clinical sample. [PMID 38924898]
- The Dutch pharmacogenetics guideline considers CYP2D6 genotyping for five TCAs and CYP2C19 for clomipramine in anxiety disorders or OCD, with genotyping considered individually. [PMID 41526670]
- Childhood traumas including physical, sexual and emotional abuse and bullying are consistently associated with later anxiety disorders, highlighting early intervention. [PMID 39824317]
- An evidence mapping appraises guidelines for psychosocial interventions in adult anxiety disorders. [PMID 41190305]
- A genome-wide study of anxiety disorders in over 122,000 cases identified risk loci and highlighted GABAergic signalling. [PMID 41634414]
- PTSD and sleep disorders are significant risk factors for acute coronary syndrome. [PMID 41533387]

**Uncertainties**
- First-line drug and psychological therapy ranking is not in the retrieved excerpts.

**MCQ angles**
- Brain stimulation options.
- Trauma history and anxiety.

**Case hooks**
- Patient with recurrent panic attacks after childhood trauma.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** First-line SSRI/SNRI and CBT recommendations, benzodiazepine caution and panic disorder specifics are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**

- PMID 39477076: Efficacy and acceptability of brain stimulation for anxiety disorders, OCD, and PTSD: A systematic review and network meta-analysis of randomized controlled trials (meta-analysis, 2025)
- PMID 39824317: Meta-analysis of prospective longitudinal cohort studies on the impact of childhood traumas on anxiety disorders (meta-analysis, 2025)
- PMID 41190305: Psychosocial interventions for anxiety disorders in adults: evidence mapping and guideline appraisal (guideline, 2025)
- PMID 41526670: Dutch Pharmacogenetics Working Group (DPWG) guideline for the gene-drug interaction between CYP2D6 and CYP2C19 and tricyclic antidepressants (guideline, 2026)

## Cluster `aplastic` (topics 178, 179) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** BSH 2024 guidelines and a Delphi consensus guide adult and severe aplastic anaemia; transplant strategy depends on donor, conditioning and GVHD prophylaxis, and ASH 2026 adds eltrombopag to immunosuppression and anti-infective prophylaxis in neutropenia. Paediatric work-up is standardised.

**Teaching points**
- The BSH guideline on adult aplastic anaemia recommends multidisciplinary discussion and involvement of expert centres to improve outcomes, with special attention to the elderly. [PMID 38247114]
- A modified Delphi panel gave consensus recommendations for management of severe aplastic anaemia, to be validated with clinical data. [PMID 38669341]
- ASH 2026 recommends adding eltrombopag to immunosuppressive regimens and using antibiotic and antifungal prophylaxis in neutropenic patients. [PMID 41920091]
- Evidence-based guidance on allogeneic HCT for severe aplastic anaemia highlights donor choice, conditioning, GVHD prophylaxis and post-transplant immunosuppression as areas for research. [PMID 39307421]
- The Chinese Society of Hematology consensus covers indications, conditioning and donor selection for HSCT, including ATG, G-CSF and post-transplant cyclophosphamide in haploidentical HSCT. [PMID 34526099, 29495966]
- A North American consensus standardises diagnostic work-up for severe aplastic anaemia in children. [PMID 34342889]

**Uncertainties**
- Optimal first-line choice of immunosuppression versus transplant by age.

**MCQ angles**
- Eltrombopag with immunosuppression.
- Prophylaxis in neutropenia.

**Case hooks**
- Young adult with pancytopenia and a hypocellular marrow.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Severity criteria, ATG plus ciclosporin regimens, matched sibling donor first-line transplant age thresholds and iron chelation are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**

## Cluster `appendicitis` (topics 180) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** The SAGES 2024 and EAES guidelines address appendicitis management. The AIR score outperforms Alvarado for diagnosis, non-operative management is an option in selected children without appendicolith, and imaging helps distinguish complicated disease.

**Teaching points**
- The SAGES guideline provides recommendations on controversies in appendicitis diagnosis and treatment, with future research opportunities. [PMID 38740595]
- The AIR score has better diagnostic capacity than the Alvarado score and is a safe basis for risk-stratified management. [PMID 39923099]
- Non-operative management of uncomplicated appendicitis in children has success rates similar to operative management in those without appendicolith. [PMID 40133910]
- A 2026 meta-analysis provides data to inform shared decision-making about non-operative management in paediatric uncomplicated appendicitis. [PMID 41046476]
- Imaging with ultrasound and CT shows inconclusive results for differentiating simple and complicated appendicitis in children. [PMID 39520568]
- The EAES rapid guideline recommends appendectomy for complicated appendicitis or appendicolith and laparoscopic or open appendectomy beyond the 20th week of pregnancy based on surgeon preference. [PMID 36307599]
- The 2024 IDSA update addresses complicated intra-abdominal infection. [PMID 38963819]

**Uncertainties**
- Imaging accuracy in children.

**MCQ angles**
- AIR versus Alvarado score.
- Antibiotics-first in uncomplicated paediatric disease.

**Case hooks**
- Child with right iliac fossa pain and no appendicolith on ultrasound.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Antibiotic regimens and duration, timing of appendicectomy, perforated disease and pregnancy imaging are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 36307599: EAES rapid guideline: systematic review, meta-analysis, GRADE assessment, and evidence-informed European recommendations on appendicitis in pregnancy (guideline, 2022)
- PMID 38740595: SAGES guideline for the diagnosis and treatment of appendicitis (guideline, 2024)
- PMID 38963819: 2024 Clinical Practice Guideline Update by the Infectious Diseases Society of America on Complicated Intra-abdominal Infections: Diagnostic Imaging of Suspected Acute Appendicitis in Adults, Children, and Pregnant People (guideline, 2024)

## Cluster `ards` (topics 181, 464) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** The 2026 guideline on ventilation and ECMO in ARDS advises early spontaneous breathing, protective ventilation and individual PEEP, with neuromuscular blockade and corticosteroids not routine in moderate to severe disease. SCCM guidance addresses neuromuscular blockade, and mesenchymal cell therapy remains unproven.

**Teaching points**
- A 2026 guideline on mechanical ventilation and ECMO in ARDS advises early spontaneous breathing, protective respiratory parameters and individual PEEP, and says muscle relaxation or corticosteroids should not be routine for moderate to severe ARDS. [PMID 41495012]
- SCCM guidelines on neuromuscular blockade in adults with ARDS recognise that institutional and patient-specific considerations must guide decisions. [PMID 41773929]
- The 2025 ESICM guideline on fluid therapy made four conditional recommendations and four ungraded best-practice statements for ARDS-related questions, with no recommendation on two. [PMID 40163133]
- Mesenchymal stem cells and extracellular vesicles are well tolerated in ARDS, but efficacy needs larger trials. [PMID 41023747]
- Consensus recommendations address perioperative mechanical ventilation strategies, with protective ventilation expected to improve respiratory outcomes. [PMID 42612906]
- A consensus immune dysregulation framework for sepsis and critical illness quantifies cellular compartment dysregulation with prognostic value. [PMID 41028543]
- Machine learning prediction of ARDS needs better integration into clinical practice. [PMID 40359510]

**Uncertainties**
- Corticosteroid benefit in ARDS is not clearly summarised in the retrieved excerpts.

**MCQ angles**
- Protective ventilation and individual PEEP.
- Neuromuscular blockade is not routine.

**Case hooks**
- Ventilated patient with PaO2/FiO2 of 90 and bilateral infiltrates.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Berlin criteria, severity classes, prone positioning, ECMO indications and the DEXA-ARDS trial are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 40163133: European Society of Intensive Care Medicine (ESICM) 2025 clinical practice guideline on fluid therapy in adult critically ill patients: part 2-the volume of resuscitation fluids (guideline, 2025)
- PMID 40359510: Predictive Modeling of Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome Using Machine Learning: Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis (meta-analysis, 2025)

## Cluster `abg` (topics 182, 183) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Blood gas interpretation uses the anion gap to unravel mixed disorders; arterial and venous samples serve different purposes. Bicarbonate therapy shows limited benefit in severe metabolic acidosis except with AKI, and altitude affects expected bicarbonate values.

**Teaching points**
- A practical guide describes gap calculations to address multiple concurrent metabolic disturbances and clarify mixed acid-base conditions, and discusses arterial versus venous samples. [PMID 40336441]
- Bicarbonate therapy gives limited benefit in severe metabolic acidosis (pH below 7.1 and bicarbonate below 6 mEq/L) but improves survival in patients with accompanying acute kidney injury. [PMID 31183278]
- Altitude must be considered when calculating bicarbonate concentrations in cities above 1500 m to avoid overdiagnosing acid-base disorders. [PMID 27120676]
- Aminoglycosides or colistin can trigger proximal or loop of Henle/distal tubular dysfunction with electrolyte and acid-base disorders. [PMID 33535401]
- pH homeostasis interacts with inflammation and metabolism pathways that drive CKD progression. [PMID 35513635]
- Guideline recommendations on regional citrate anticoagulation for CRRT were formed by expert consensus. [PMID 37248514]

**Uncertainties**
- Bicarbonate therapy indications.

**MCQ angles**
- Anion gap in mixed disorders.
- Bicarbonate in acidosis with AKI.

**Case hooks**
- ICU patient with pH 7.18, low bicarbonate and AKI.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Stepwise interpretation (pH, pCO2, bicarbonate, compensation formulae, A-a gradient) is not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 27120676: Bicarbonate Values for Healthy Residents Living in Cities Above 1500 Meters of Altitude: A Theoretical Model and Systematic Review (systematic review, 2016)
- PMID 31183278: Bicarbonate Therapy for Critically Ill Patients with Metabolic Acidosis: A Systematic Review (systematic review, 2019)
- PMID 33535401: Electrolyte and Acid-Base Disorders Triggered by Aminoglycoside or Colistin Therapy: A Systematic Review (systematic review, 2021)
- PMID 35513635: Kidney metabolism and acid-base control: back to the basics (review, 2022)
- PMID 37248514: Management of regional citrate anticoagulation for continuous renal replacement therapy: guideline recommendations from Chinese emergency medical doctor consensus (guideline, 2023)
- PMID 40336441: Mastering blood gas interpretation: A practical guide for primary care providers (guideline, 2025)

## Cluster `arterial_ulcer` (topics 184) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Arterial leg ulcers reflect advanced peripheral artery disease. The 2024 multisociety PAD guideline and the 2025 ACC statement on PAD in diabetes frame care, while underprescription of recommended medication and patient nonadherence are common.

**Teaching points**
- The 2024 multisociety lower extremity PAD guideline updates recommendations with new evidence and adds recommendations for comprehensive PAD care. [PMID 38752899, 38743805]
- The 2025 ACC scientific statement on PAD in adults with diabetes outlines future research on earlier diagnosis, screening and trial endpoints. [PMID 41405527]
- The SVS guideline on intermittent claudication stresses patient-centred assessment of risk, benefit, impairment and durability. [PMID 40316185]
- Clinician underprescription of, and patient nonadherence to, guideline-recommended medications are documented in PAD. [PMID 40791889]
- Alcohol intake of 2 drinks per week or less was associated with reduced PAD risk, with risk rising at 10 or more drinks per week. [PMID 38626304]
- A Saudi guideline offers an evidence-based framework for cardiovascular disease prevention emphasising lifestyle and optimised pharmacology. [PMID 41628971]

**Uncertainties**
- Revascularisation strategy for chronic limb-threatening ischaemia is not in the retrieved excerpts.

**MCQ angles**
- Medical therapy underuse in PAD.
- PAD in diabetes.

**Case hooks**
- Diabetic smoker with a painful ulcer on the toe and absent pulses.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Ulcer features that distinguish arterial from venous ulcers, ABI interpretation, limb salvage and wound care are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**

## Cluster `asbestosis` (topics 185) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Occupational asbestos disease guidance (AWMF S2k) covers diagnosis, rehabilitation and recognition of new associations; people with asbestosis or silicosis have higher lung cancer risk and need monitoring. Retrieved evidence on asbestosis management itself is limited.

**Teaching points**
- The updated AWMF S2k guideline on occupational asbestos-related diseases covers outpatient rehabilitation and training therapy for the first time and addresses ovarian cancer as a new occupational disease after asbestos exposure. [PMID 33728629]
- Lung cancer risk is higher in exposed individuals with asbestosis or silicosis than in exposed workers without fibrosis, so they should be monitored for lung cancer. [PMID 38355151]
- MicroRNAs have potential as early biomarkers of asbestos-related lung cancer and mesothelioma, though large validation studies are needed. [PMID 38402124, 27259231]
- Reliable assessment of mesothelioma mortality in major asbestos-user countries is a concern and supports the call for an international ban on asbestos. [PMID 36819965]
- YKL-40 may be a useful biomarker for diagnosis and prognosis of interstitial lung disease. [PMID 33907118]

**Uncertainties**
- Treatment evidence for asbestosis is not covered by the retrieved excerpts.

**MCQ angles**
- Lung cancer surveillance in asbestosis and silicosis.
- Mesothelioma and asbestos exposure.

**Case hooks**
- Retired insulation worker with basal crackles and pleural plaques.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Diagnostic criteria (exposure history, HRCT features), latency, compensation context and management (smoking cessation, vaccination, oxygen) are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 27259231: Diagnostic value of microRNAs in asbestos exposure and malignant mesothelioma: systematic review and qualitative meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2016)

## Cluster `ascites` (topics 186, 399) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Ascites guidelines manage cirrhotic ascites with attention to albumin use, infection and hepatorenal syndrome; terlipressin plus albumin treats HRS. TIPS prevents further decompensation and improves survival in selected cirrhosis with portal hypertension.

**Teaching points**
- Guidelines on the management of ascites in cirrhosis use GRADE methodology and are intended to inform practising clinicians. [PMID 33067334]
- For hepatorenal syndrome, the Chinese ascites guideline recommends vasopressor terlipressin with albumin, and local microbiology guides anti-infection treatment. [PMID 30656520]
- TIPS reduces further decompensation and increases survival compared with standard care in cirrhosis with portal hypertension-related complications. [PMID 37141993]
- The AGA update on AKI in cirrhosis advises that TIPS should not be used as specific treatment of HRS and addresses renal replacement therapy in selected circumstances. [PMID 36075500]
- Few evidence-based indications support routine albumin use, and guidelines give actionable recommendations on appropriate use. [PMID 38447639, 38607390]
- A comparison found good concordance between European, American and British guidelines on ascites, SBP and HRS, recommending vasoactive drugs. [PMID 26671516]

**Uncertainties**
- Indications for routine albumin.

**MCQ angles**
- Terlipressin plus albumin for HRS.
- TIPS for refractory complications.

**Case hooks**
- Cirrhotic with tense ascites and fever.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Diagnostic paracentesis (SAAG, neutrophil count), sodium restriction, diuretic regimens and SBP prophylaxis are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 26671516: The clinical management of abdominal ascites, spontaneous bacterial peritonitis and hepatorenal syndrome: a review of current guidelines and recommendations (guideline, 2016)
- PMID 30656520: Chinese guidelines on the management of ascites and its related complications in cirrhosis (guideline, 2019)
- PMID 33067334: Guidelines on the management of ascites in cirrhosis (guideline, 2021)
- PMID 36075500: AGA Clinical Practice Update on the Evaluation and Management of Acute Kidney Injury in Patients With Cirrhosis: Expert Review (guideline, 2022)

- PMID 38607390: Albumin: a comprehensive review and practical guideline for clinical use (guideline, 2024)

## Cluster `aspiration_pneumonia` (topics 187) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Poor oral hygiene and dysphagia drive aspiration pneumonia risk, and professional oral care lowers risk in older residents of care homes. Parkinson disease has 10% hospital mortality with aspiration pneumonia, and GLP-1 agonists increase retained gastric contents without more aspiration pneumonia.

**Teaching points**
- Pathogenic oral microorganisms are associated with aspiration pneumonia in older residents of care homes, and professional oral hygiene care helps reduce risk. [PMID 32677660]
- Oropharyngeal dysphagia is increasingly prevalent across populations, so causes should be monitored at all levels. [PMID 35410274]
- Post-extubation dysphagia is common among intubated critical care patients, and consensus is needed on assessment timing and tools. [PMID 39217392]
- In Parkinson disease, aspiration pneumonia has hospital mortality of 10.0%, and multidisciplinary holistic care is needed. [PMID 39236309]
- GLP-1 receptor agonist use was associated with increased residual gastric contents and premature endoscopy termination, with no significant difference in aspiration pneumonia. [PMID 39694296]
- The JRS pneumonia guideline and a Chinese elderly pneumonia consensus address management, advance care planning and molecular diagnostics in older patients. [PMID 40614556, 39757093]

**Uncertainties**
- Optimal antibiotic therapy is not in the retrieved excerpts.

**MCQ angles**
- Oral hygiene reduces risk.
- Dysphagia after extubation.

**Case hooks**
- Older nursing-home resident with cough after meals and right lower lobe infiltrate.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Antibiotic selection (anaerobic cover), swallowing assessment and prevention bundles are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 32677660: Poor oral hygiene, oral microorganisms and aspiration pneumonia risk in older people in residential aged care: a systematic review (meta-analysis, 2021)
- PMID 35410274: The global prevalence of oropharyngeal dysphagia in different populations: a systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2022)
- PMID 39217392: Incidence of post-extubation dysphagia among critical care patients undergoing orotracheal intubation: a systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2024)
- PMID 39236309: Risk of aspiration pneumonia and hospital mortality in Parkinson disease: A systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2024)
- PMID 39694296: Glucagon-like peptide-1 receptor agonist use and the risk of residual gastric contents and aspiration in patients undergoing GI endoscopy: a systematic review and a meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2025)
- PMID 39757093: [Chinese expert consensus on the diagnosis and treatment of pneumonia in the elderly (2024 Edition)] (guideline, 2025)

## Cluster `aspirin_primary` (topics 188, 190, 191, 192) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Aspirin for primary prevention reduces nonfatal ischaemic events but increases major bleeding without lowering all-cause mortality, so routine use needs reconsideration. USPSTF found insufficient evidence for initiation at 70 or older, and ACC/AHA limits it to high-risk, low-bleeding-risk adults aged 40-70.

**Teaching points**
- Aspirin for primary prevention reduces nonfatal ischaemic events but significantly increases nonfatal bleeding events. [PMID 31196447]
- Aspirin primary prevention was not associated with reduced all-cause mortality but increased major bleeding, so routine use needs reconsideration. [PMID 30561620]
- Aspirin in people without cardiovascular disease was associated with lower cardiovascular events and increased major bleeding. [PMID 30667501]
- USPSTF found current evidence insufficient to assess the balance of benefits and harms of initiating aspirin for primary prevention in adults aged 70 or older (I statement). [PMID 27064677]
- ACC/AHA guidelines recommend aspirin for primary prevention in patients 40-70 years at high ASCVD risk and low bleeding risk who cannot control modifiable risk factors. [PMID 32924644]
- Aspirin use for primary prevention may be overutilised, with about half of patients discordant with recent guideline recommendations. [PMID 39374577]
- Non-smokers, statin-treated patients and males had the greatest MACE risk reduction with aspirin across subgroups. [PMID 31679516]
- Heterogeneity between guidelines suggests targeting aspirin to patients with subclinical atherosclerosis and mitigated bleeding risk. [PMID 35757317]

**Uncertainties**
- Net benefit in older adults.

**MCQ angles**
- Net benefit versus bleeding.
- Guideline-discordant aspirin use.

**Case hooks**
- Healthy 72-year-old asking whether to start a daily aspirin.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** ASPREE, ARRIVE and ASCEND trial results and age/risk thresholds are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 27064677: Aspirin Use for the Primary Prevention of Cardiovascular Disease and Colorectal Cancer: U.S. Preventive Services Task Force Recommendation Statement (guideline, 2016)
- PMID 30561620: Efficacy and safety of aspirin for primary prevention of cardiovascular events: a meta-analysis and trial sequential analysis of randomized controlled trials (meta-analysis, 2019)
- PMID 30667501: Association of Aspirin Use for Primary Prevention With Cardiovascular Events and Bleeding Events: A Systematic Review and Meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2019)
- PMID 31196447: Aspirin for Primary Prevention of Cardiovascular Events (meta-analysis, 2019)

- PMID 32924644: Aspirin for primary prevention of cardiovascular disease: a review of recent literature and updated guideline recommendations (guideline, 2021)
- PMID 35757317: Implications of the heterogeneity between guideline recommendations for the use of low dose aspirin in primary prevention of cardiovascular disease (guideline, 2022)
- PMID 39374577: Guideline concordance of aspirin use for primary prevention in adult outpatients (guideline, 2024)

## Cluster `aerd` (topics 189) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** NSAID-exacerbated respiratory disease is managed with aspirin desensitisation, which can improve quality of life, and with biologics for associated nasal polyps; dupilumab is the most effective biologic for restoring smell. Urinary LTE4 is an investigational biomarker.

**Teaching points**
- Aspirin desensitisation (oral or intranasal) in NSAID-exacerbated respiratory disease may improve health-related quality of life measured by SNOT scores. [PMID 39775459]
- The EAACI position paper defines N-ERD diagnosis and management and notes sub-endotypes may guide biological therapy. [PMID 30216468]
- Dupilumab appears more efficacious for improving sense of smell than omalizumab, mepolizumab and benralizumab in chronic rhinosinusitis with nasal polyps. [PMID 37669083]
- Biologics targeting type 2 inflammation offer treatment options for uncontrolled nasal polyps as an alternative to surgery or after persistent symptoms despite surgery. [PMID 38827258]
- The chronic rhinosinusitis practice parameter addresses aspirin treatment after desensitisation and the risks of desensitisation. [PMID 37667905]
- Urinary leukotriene E4 has been studied as an N-ERD biomarker, but varied measurement limits clinical utility. [PMID 36374376]
- The adult sinusitis guideline update recommends amoxicillin with or without clavulanate as first-line for acute bacterial rhinosinusitis. [PMID 40741969]

**Uncertainties**
- Standardised biomarker use.

**MCQ angles**
- Aspirin desensitisation benefits.
- Dupilumab for smell.

**Case hooks**
- Asthmatic with nasal polyps and wheeze after ibuprofen.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Samter's triad pathophysiology, challenge protocols, leukotriene modifiers and long-term maintenance dosing are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 30216468: Diagnosis and management of NSAID-Exacerbated Respiratory Disease (N-ERD)-a EAACI position paper (guideline, 2019)
- PMID 36374376: Urinary Leukotriene E4 as a Biomarker in NSAID-Exacerbated Respiratory Disease (N-ERD): a Systematic Review and Meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2022)
- PMID 37667905: The chronic rhinosinusitis practice parameter (guideline, 2023)
- PMID 37669083: Improvement in Smell Using Monoclonal Antibodies Among Patients With Chronic Rhinosinusitis With Nasal Polyps: A Systematic Review (meta-analysis, 2023)

## Cluster `asthma_maint` (topics 193, 197, 198, 199) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Modern asthma care favours inhaled corticosteroid-containing regimens, with as-needed ICS-formoterol or maintenance-and-reliever therapy rather than SABA alone. Severe asthma needs phenotyping and consideration of biologics, macrolides or triple inhaler therapy.

**Teaching points**
- The 2025 VA/DoD guideline strongly recommends inhaled corticosteroids and suggests ICS combined with a rapid-onset long-acting beta-agonist as both reliever and controller, with step-up by increasing ICS or adding a long-acting anticholinergic. [PMID 41698207]
- In poorly controlled asthma at GINA step 3 or 4, budesonide-formoterol maintenance and reliever therapy lengthened time to first severe exacerbation compared with stepping up or continuing maintenance ICS-LABA with SABA reliever. [PMID 35230437]
- Triple versus dual inhaler therapy in moderate to severe asthma was associated with fewer severe exacerbations and modest control gains, without differences in quality of life or mortality. [PMID 34009257]
- The ERS/ATS severe asthma guideline suggests a trial of chronic macrolide therapy for persistent symptoms at GINA step 5 and anti-IL-4/13 for severe eosinophilic or corticosteroid-dependent asthma. [PMID 31558662]
- Licensed asthma biologics reduce exacerbations and hospitalisations, improve lung function, control and quality of life, and limit systemic corticosteroid use, with greater effect in T2 inflammation. [PMID 38657997]
- The ACCP 2026 guideline gives seven recommendations on choosing a biologic, based on quality of life, lung function, exacerbation frequency, oral steroid use, endotype, biomarkers and comorbidities, while noting no comparative effectiveness trials. [PMID 41005695]
- Clinical remission is achievable for a minority on biologics, with lower rates in those with worse FEV1, longer disease, maintenance oral corticosteroids, depression or obesity. [PMID 39549709]
- A Spanish study found most guideline quality indicators of asthma care are not followed in practice. [PMID 39309478]

**Uncertainties**
- Head-to-head comparative efficacy of biologics.
- Definition of clinical remission.

**MCQ angles**
- SMART/MART regimen.
- Choosing a biologic by endotype and biomarkers.

**Case hooks**
- Adult on SABA alone with frequent night-time symptoms.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Diagnostic criteria (spirometry, FeNO), GINA step table, inhaler technique, exacerbation management and acute severity classification are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 31558662: Management of severe asthma: a European Respiratory Society/American Thoracic Society guideline (guideline, 2020)
- PMID 34009257: Triple vs Dual Inhaler Therapy and Asthma Outcomes in Moderate to Severe Asthma: A Systematic Review and Meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2021)
- PMID 35230437: Evaluation of Budesonide-Formoterol for Maintenance and Reliever Therapy Among Patients With Poorly Controlled Asthma: A Systematic Review and Meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2022)
- PMID 38657997: Biologic agents licensed for severe asthma: a systematic review and meta-analysis of randomised controlled trials (meta-analysis, 2024)
- PMID 39309478: Level of Compliance with Spanish Guideline Recommendations in the Management of Asthma (guideline, 2024)
- PMID 39549709: Clinical remission attainment, definitions, and correlates among patients with severe asthma treated with biologics: a systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2025)
- PMID 41005695: Biologic Management in Severe Asthma for Adults: An American College of Chest Physicians Clinical Practice Guideline (guideline, 2026)
- PMID 41698207: A Synopsis of the 2025 U.S. Department of Veterans Affairs and U.S. Department of Defense Clinical Practice Guideline for the Primary Care Management of Asthma (guideline, 2026)

## Cluster `asthma_biologics` (topics 194, 195, 252, 255) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Licensed asthma biologics reduce exacerbations and hospitalisations, improve lung function, control and quality of life, and spare oral steroids, especially in T2 inflammation. Choice rests on endotype, biomarkers and comorbidities because head-to-head trials are lacking; clinical remission is achievable for a minority.

**Teaching points**
- Licensed asthma biologics reduce exacerbations and hospitalisations, improve lung function, asthma control and quality of life, and limit systemic corticosteroid use, with greater effect in T2 inflammation. [PMID 38657997]
- The ACCP 2026 guideline gives seven recommendations on choosing a biologic based on quality of life, lung function, exacerbation frequency, oral steroid use, endotype, biomarkers and comorbidities, but no comparative effectiveness trials exist. [PMID 41005695]
- The EAACI systematic review found high certainty that benralizumab, dupilumab, mepolizumab and omalizumab reduce severe exacerbation rates, and benralizumab, dupilumab and mepolizumab reduce oral corticosteroid use. [PMID 32034960]
- The ERS/ATS guideline suggests anti-IL-4/13 for severe eosinophilic asthma and for corticosteroid-dependent asthma regardless of eosinophils. [PMID 31558662]
- Head-to-head comparisons show small effect size differences with wide confidence interval overlap, and direct pragmatic trials assessing remission are needed. [PMID 40156481]
- Clinical remission is achievable for a minority treated with biologics, with lower rates in worse FEV1, longer disease, maintenance oral steroids, depression and obesity. [PMID 39549709]
- Real-world anti-IL5 biologics improve outcomes with effect sizes similar to RCTs, though data are largely retrospective. [PMID 35174566]

**Uncertainties**
- Comparative efficacy of biologics.

**MCQ angles**
- Choosing a biologic by phenotype.
- Remission as a goal.

**Case hooks**
- Eosinophilic asthma with frequent exacerbations despite high-dose ICS-LABA.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Eligibility thresholds (eosinophils, IgE), dosing intervals, switching rules and stopping criteria are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 31558662: Management of severe asthma: a European Respiratory Society/American Thoracic Society guideline (guideline, 2020)
- PMID 32034960: Efficacy and safety of treatment with biologicals (benralizumab, dupilumab, mepolizumab, omalizumab and reslizumab) for severe eosinophilic asthma. A systematic review for the EAACI Guidelines - recommendations on the use of biologicals in severe asthma (guideline, 2020)
- PMID 35174566: Real-world efficacy of treatment with benralizumab, dupilumab, mepolizumab and reslizumab for severe asthma: A systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2022)
- PMID 38657997: Biologic agents licensed for severe asthma: a systematic review and meta-analysis of randomised controlled trials (meta-analysis, 2024)
- PMID 39549709: Clinical remission attainment, definitions, and correlates among patients with severe asthma treated with biologics: a systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2025)
- PMID 40156481: Head-To-Head Comparison of Biologic Efficacy in Asthma: What Have We Learned? (meta-analysis, 2025)
- PMID 41005695: Biologic Management in Severe Asthma for Adults: An American College of Chest Physicians Clinical Practice Guideline (guideline, 2026)

## Cluster `asthma_pregnancy` (topics 200) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Dutch and German guidelines address asthma during pregnancy, and an international consensus supports using asthma biologics in pregnancy and breastfeeding in line with prescribing criteria for non-pregnant people. Registries are needed to inform guidelines.

**Teaching points**
- The Dutch multidisciplinary guideline on asthma and pregnancy covers treatment and monitoring of asthma during pregnancy, developed with all relevant providers and patient representatives. [PMID 38513758]
- An international consensus supports asthma biologics being continued or initiated in pregnancy in line with prescribing criteria for non-pregnant people, and initiated or continued during breastfeeding. [PMID 39216499]
- The German asthma guideline for respiratory specialists addresses asthma treatment during pregnancy among other management challenges. [PMID 37406667]
- Guidance on administering biologics for severe asthma and allergic conditions addresses use in different health circumstances. [PMID 36124286]
- Prenatal exposure to air pollutants is associated with allergic diseases in children. [PMID 38639856]
- AHA special circumstances guidance requires modified resuscitation in conditions such as pregnancy. [PMID 41122889]

**Uncertainties**
- Registry data are essential to inform guidelines.

**MCQ angles**
- Continue asthma controllers in pregnancy.
- Biologics in pregnancy and breastfeeding.

**Case hooks**
- Pregnant woman with moderate asthma considering stopping her inhaler.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Medication safety by drug class, exacerbation management in pregnancy and monitoring are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**

## Cluster `renal_artery_stenosis` (topics 201) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** ACR Appropriateness Criteria (2017, updated 2026) guide imaging for renovascular hypertension. Endovascular treatment gives some blood pressure improvement over medical therapy in an updated meta-analysis, and RAS is common at coronary angiography, with CKD, PAD, older age and severe CAD the strongest predictors.

**Teaching points**
- ACR Appropriateness Criteria for renovascular hypertension guide imaging and treatment selection, updated in 2026. [PMID 29101991, 41817473]
- An updated meta-analysis compared endovascular treatment with medical therapy in hypertensive patients with renal artery stenosis and found improvement with balloon angioplasty with or without stenting, calling for studies in more severe stenosis. [PMID 31376542]
- RAS is common in patients undergoing coronary angiography, with CKD, PAD, older age and severe CAD the strongest predictors. [PMID 39329877]
- In transplant renal artery stenosis, PTA with stenting appears more effective than PTA alone in stabilising renal function and reducing restenosis. [PMID 34399594]
- Catheter-based renal denervation safely reduces ambulatory and office blood pressure versus sham. [PMID 39355923]

**Uncertainties**
- Benefit of revascularisation in severe stenosis.

**MCQ angles**
- Predictors of RAS.
- Stenting in transplant RAS.

**Case hooks**
- Resistant hypertension with flash pulmonary oedema.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** ACE inhibitor effects, CORAL trial results and imaging test accuracy (duplex, CTA, MRA) are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
