## Cluster `atopic_dermatitis` (topics 202) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Paediatric atopic dermatitis guidelines advise emollients, topical therapy, phototherapy and systemic agents and strongly recommend against systemic corticosteroids. Real-world JAK inhibitor outcomes match trials, and dupilumab should prompt reassessment when cutaneous lymphoma is suspected.

**Teaching points**
- The 2026 paediatric atopic dermatitis guideline conditionally recommends against topical antimicrobials and PUVA phototherapy and strongly recommends against systemic corticosteroids. [PMID 41949508]
- Real-world effectiveness and safety of JAK inhibitors in atopic dermatitis are comparable to clinical trials. [PMID 40751489]
- Dupilumab may unmask or exacerbate cutaneous T-cell lymphoma, particularly mycosis fungoides and Sezary syndrome, so consensus recommendations guide patient selection and reassessment. [PMID 41821352, 41967816]
- The European living guideline update on atopic eczema includes JAK inhibitors (abrocitinib, baricitinib, upadacitinib) and considerations for paediatric, pregnant and breastfeeding patients. [PMID 40317496]
- The ACG evidence-to-recommendation framework for mild and moderate atopic dermatitis weighs benefits, certainty, patient preferences and feasibility. [PMID 41937409]
- Increased pollutants and temperature extremes are associated with a higher population burden of atopic dermatitis. [PMID 40560568]
- Dupilumab in pregnancy showed no significant increase in miscarriage or congenital malformations, though evidence is limited. [PMID 39936607]

**Uncertainties**
- Long-term safety of JAK inhibitors.

**MCQ angles**
- Avoid systemic steroids in children.
- Dupilumab and CTCL.

**Case hooks**
- Child with chronic eczema failing topical steroids.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Diagnostic criteria, emollient and topical steroid potency ladders, wet wraps and biologic eligibility are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 39936607: Safety of Dupilumab Therapy for Atopic Dermatitis during Pregnancy: A Systematic Review and Meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2025)
- PMID 40317496: European Guideline (EuroGuiDerm) on atopic eczema: Living update (guideline, 2025)
- PMID 40560568: Climate Conditions, Weather Changes, and Air Pollutants and Atopic Dermatitis: A Meta-Analysis (meta-analysis, 2025)
- PMID 40751489: Real-World Effectiveness and Safety of JAK Inhibitors in Atopic Dermatitis: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis (meta-analysis, 2025)
- PMID 41821352: Cutaneous T-cell lymphomas and dupilumab for atopic dermatitis: A systematic review and expert consensus (guideline, 2026)
- PMID 41937409: Evidence-to-Recommendation Framework for 2026 ACE Clinical Guideline on mild and moderate atopic dermatitis (eczema) - a journey from flare to care (guideline, 2026)

## Cluster `af_rhythm` (topics 203, 207, 209) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Catheter ablation reduces mortality, stroke and heart failure hospitalisation compared with medical therapy in AF, including in AF with heart failure and in HFpEF. A 4-week blanking period may be optimal, and uninterrupted NOACs cut major bleeding periprocedurally.

**Teaching points**
- In AF with heart failure, catheter ablation reduces mortality and heart failure hospitalisation compared with medical therapy (RR 0.60 for hospitalisation). [PMID 36305545]
- Catheter ablation of AF significantly reduces death, stroke and hospitalisation compared with medical therapy alone. [PMID 32115777]
- In HFpEF with AF, catheter ablation showed cardiovascular morbidity and mortality benefits compared with standard medical therapy, and lower AF recurrence. [PMID 39278992]
- A 4-week blanking period may be the optimal cutoff after AF ablation, supporting revision of the current 3-month period. [PMID 35917218]
- Uninterrupted periprocedural NOACs are associated with low stroke or TIA and significantly less major bleeding than uninterrupted VKAs during AF ablation. [PMID 28917562]
- Most oesophageal injuries after AF ablation resolved conservatively, while a small percentage of type 2b lesions progressed to perforation or fistula. [PMID 30273767]
- A review compares guidelines and consensus documents on AF catheter ablation. [PMID 31729548]

**Uncertainties**
- Early rhythm control beyond ablation is not in the retrieved excerpts.

**MCQ angles**
- Ablation in AF with heart failure.
- Uninterrupted anticoagulation during ablation.

**Case hooks**
- AF patient with HFrEF considering ablation versus drugs.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Rate versus rhythm control trial results (AFFIRM, EAST-AFNET 4), antiarrhythmic drug choice and cardioversion are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 28917562: An updated meta-analysis of novel oral anticoagulants versus vitamin K antagonists for uninterrupted anticoagulation in atrial fibrillation catheter ablation (meta-analysis, 2018)
- PMID 30273767: Temporal relationships between esophageal injury type and progression in patients undergoing atrial fibrillation catheter ablation (systematic review, 2019)
- PMID 31729548: Atrial fibrillation catheter ablation: an updated review of current guidelines and expert consensus documents (guideline, 2019)
- PMID 32115777: Impact of atrial fibrillation catheter ablation on mortality, stroke, and heart failure hospitalizations: A meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2020)
- PMID 35917218: Evidence-based insights on ideal blanking period duration following atrial fibrillation catheter ablation (meta-analysis, 2022)
- PMID 36305545: Catheter ablation improves cardiovascular outcomes in patients with atrial fibrillation and heart failure: a meta-analysis of randomized controlled trials (meta-analysis, 2023)
- PMID 39278992: Heart failure with preserved ejection fraction and atrial fibrillation: catheter ablation vs. standard medical therapy - a systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2025)

## Cluster `atypical_pneumonia` (topics 210) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Retrieved evidence concerns Mycoplasma pneumoniae in children: macrolide resistance is rising and linked to severe and refractory disease, clarithromycin or azithromycin remain first-line with doxycycline or levofloxacin reserved for resistance, and risk factors predict complications.

**Teaching points**
- The Spanish paediatric infectious diseases position statement says clarithromycin is the treatment of choice for Mycoplasma pneumoniae with azithromycin as an alternative, reserving doxycycline and levofloxacin for resistance or CNS infection. [PMID 38987075]
- Macrolide-resistant Mycoplasma pneumoniae pneumonia is associated with more severe, refractory and complicated cases, possibly from prolonged colonisation due to ineffective treatment. [PMID 40106136]
- A global molecular epidemiology study links Mycoplasma pneumoniae to macrolide resistance and calls for ongoing surveillance. [PMID 41544187]
- A Chinese evidence-based guideline covers diagnosis and treatment of Mycoplasma pneumoniae pneumonia in children. [PMID 40241891]
- Risk factors for complications and refractory Mycoplasma pneumonia in hospitalised children were identified, with predictive models proposed for early prediction. [PMID 39696115, 41723336]
- Azithromycin sequential therapy plus inhaled terbutaline was safe and beneficial for children with Mycoplasma pneumonia, though high-quality evidence is lacking. [PMID 38944667]
- Adenovirus co-infection is associated with clinical severity in paediatric pneumonia. [PMID 40297972]

**Uncertainties**
- Adult atypical pneumonia and Legionella are not covered by the retrieved excerpts.

**MCQ angles**
- Macrolide resistance in Mycoplasma.
- Alternatives to macrolides in children.

**Case hooks**
- School-age child with persistent cough and fever not responding to amoxicillin.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Chlamydophila and Legionella features, atypical coverage in empiric CAP therapy and adult dosing are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 38944667: Azithromycin sequential therapy plus inhaled terbutaline for Mycoplasma Pneumoniae pneumonia in children: a systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2024)
- PMID 38987075: Position statement of the Spanish Society of Paediatric Infectious diseases on the diagnosis and treatment of Mycoplasma pneumoniae infection (guideline, 2024)
- PMID 39696115: Risk factors for complications of Mycoplasma pneumoniae pneumonia in hospitalized children in China: a systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2024)
- PMID 40106136: Clinical characteristics and associated factors of macrolide-resistant mycoplasma pneumoniae pneumonia in children: a systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2025)
- PMID 40241891: Evidence-based guideline for the diagnosis and treatment of &lt;i&gt;Mycoplasma pneumoniae&lt;/i&gt; pneumonia in children (2023) (guideline, 2025)
- PMID 40297972: Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis of the Association Between Clinical Severity and Co-Infection of Human Adenovirus With Other Respiratory Pathogens in Children (meta-analysis, 2025)
- PMID 41544187: Global molecular epidemiology of &lt;i&gt;Mycoplasma pneumoniae&lt;/i&gt; and its association with macrolide resistance and disease severity: a systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2026)
- PMID 41723336: Prevalence, risk factors, and early prediction of refractory pneumonia caused by Mycoplasma pneumoniae in children: a systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2026)

## Cluster `autoantibody_panel` (topics 211) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Autoantibody testing is interpreted within classification frameworks: the 2019 EULAR/ACR SLE criteria use ANA as an entry criterion. Platelet autoantibody testing in ITP is specific but insensitive, and RF/ACPA positivity associates with RA-ILD.

**Teaching points**
- The 2019 EULAR/ACR SLE classification criteria use ANA as an entry criterion with hierarchically clustered, weighted criteria and excellent sensitivity and specificity. [PMID 31383717]
- Autoantibody testing in immune thrombocytopenia has high specificity but low sensitivity, so a positive test helps rule in ITP but a negative test does not rule it out. [PMID 30801909]
- Definitions of anti-synthetase syndrome vary widely and need consensus classification criteria. [PMID 35225224]
- RF and ACPA positivity and titres are associated with interstitial lung disease in rheumatoid arthritis, along with male sex, older age, smoking and raised ESR and CRP. [PMID 37352174]
- Sjogren's syndrome is common in primary biliary cholangitis and influences the clinical phenotype. [PMID 35970319]
- Preliminary international consensus criteria identify homogeneous disorders within juvenile idiopathic arthritis. [PMID 30275259]

**Uncertainties**
- Interpretation of low-titre ANA is not covered by the retrieved excerpts.

**MCQ angles**
- ANA as an SLE entry criterion.
- Sensitivity versus specificity of autoantibody tests.

**Case hooks**
- Young woman with arthralgia and a positive ANA of 1:160.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** ENA patterns, anti-dsDNA and complement monitoring, ANCA interpretation and pre-test probability are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 30275259: Toward New Classification Criteria for Juvenile Idiopathic Arthritis: First Steps, Pediatric Rheumatology International Trials Organization International Consensus (guideline, 2019)
- PMID 30801909: The sensitivity and specificity of platelet autoantibody testing in immune thrombocytopenia: a systematic review and meta-analysis of a diagnostic test (meta-analysis, 2019)
- PMID 31383717: 2019 European League Against Rheumatism/American College of Rheumatology classification criteria for systemic lupus erythematosus (guideline, 2019)
- PMID 35225224: Defining anti-synthetase syndrome: a systematic literature review (meta-analysis, 2022)
- PMID 35970319: Prevalence and impact of Sjögren's syndrome in primary biliary cholangitis: a systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2022)
- PMID 37352174: Factors associated with interstitial lung disease in patients with rheumatoid arthritis: A systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2023)

## Cluster `autoimmune_enceph` (topics 212) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Canadian and Brazilian consensus guidelines give pragmatic acute approaches to adult autoimmune encephalitis. FDG-PET adds diagnostic value, rituximab is an effective second-line agent, and pregnancy poses higher miscarriage risk.

**Teaching points**
- Canadian consensus guidelines provide a pragmatic approach to diagnosing and treating autoimmune encephalitis in adults in the acute setting. [PMID 38312020]
- Brazilian consensus recommendations give tangible evidence for effective management of autoimmune encephalitis using mRS and CASE scales. [PMID 39089672]
- Brain 18F-FDG PET adds value for diagnosing autoimmune encephalitis, with metabolic patterns corresponding to antibody subtypes. [PMID 33677643]
- Rituximab is an effective second-line agent for autoimmune encephalitis with acceptable toxicity, improving mRS by 2.67 points. [PMID 32484900]
- Autoimmune encephalitis in pregnancy is challenging, with higher miscarriage rates and possible long-term maternal deficits. [PMID 37562964]
- International consensus definitions exist for infection-triggered encephalopathy syndromes. [PMID 39143740]
- Catatonia guidelines and a paediatric catatonia clinical pathway offer practical management guidance. [PMID 37236789, 41865798]

**Uncertainties**
- First-line immunotherapy sequencing is not in the retrieved excerpts.

**MCQ angles**
- Rituximab as second-line therapy.
- FDG-PET patterns.

**Case hooks**
- Young woman with psychosis, seizures and orofacial dyskinesias.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Antibody subtypes (NMDAR, LGI1, CASPR2), tumour screening, first-line steroids/IVIG/plasma exchange and diagnostic criteria are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 32484900: Efficacy and safety of rituximab in autoimmune encephalitis: A meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2020)
- PMID 33677643: Brain &lt;sup&gt;18&lt;/sup&gt;F-FDG PET for the diagnosis of autoimmune encephalitis: a systematic review and a meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2021)

## Cluster `autoimmune_gastritis` (topics 213) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Autoimmune gastritis is an atrophic gastritis linked to pernicious anaemia, B12 deficiency and other autoimmune diseases, and it carries gastric cancer risk. AGA and ACG guidance covers endoscopic features, surveillance of gastric premalignant conditions, and NICE guidance covers B12 deficiency.

**Teaching points**
- The AGA update on atrophic gastritis describes endoscopic features such as pale mucosa, visible vasculature, loss of gastric folds and, with intestinal metaplasia, light blue crests and white opaque fields. [PMID 34454714]
- The ACG guideline on gastric premalignant conditions highlights alignment of best practice, training for improved detection and evaluation of effects on gastric cancer incidence and mortality in the US. [PMID 40072510]
- NICE guidance on vitamin B12 deficiency in people over 16 includes diagnosis and management related to autoimmune gastritis. [PMID 38713783]
- A range of autoimmune diseases is associated with gastric cancer, possibly reflecting unreported links with autoimmune gastritis. [PMID 31048663]
- Antibody-mediated autoimmune disease is prevalent in type 1 diabetes, especially hypothyroidism and coeliac disease. [PMID 30508413]
- Polyautoimmunity is prevalent in autoimmune thyroid disease, and clustering of diseases may define phenotypes. [PMID 32738825]
- Patients with chronic spontaneous urticaria have increased risk of autoimmune diseases and should be screened for signs and symptoms. [PMID 29037900]

**Uncertainties**
- Optimal surveillance intervals are not covered by the retrieved excerpts.

**MCQ angles**
- Associated autoimmune thyroid disease and type 1 diabetes.
- Gastric cancer risk in atrophic gastritis.

**Case hooks**
- Middle-aged woman with macrocytic anaemia, neuropathy and hypothyroidism.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Intrinsic factor and parietal cell antibodies, B12 replacement regimen and carcinoid risk are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 29037900: Autoimmune comorbidity in chronic spontaneous urticaria: A systematic review (systematic review, 2017)
- PMID 30508413: Associated auto-immune disease in type 1 diabetes patients: a systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2019)
- PMID 31048663: Autoimmune Diseases and Gastric Cancer Risk: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis (meta-analysis, 2019)
- PMID 32738825: Prevalence of latent and overt polyautoimmunity in autoimmune thyroid disease: A systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2020)
- PMID 34454714: AGA Clinical Practice Update on the Diagnosis and Management of Atrophic Gastritis: Expert Review (guideline, 2021)
- PMID 38713783: Vitamin B12 deficiency in over 16s: diagnosis and management (guideline, 2024)
- PMID 40072510: ACG Clinical Guideline: Diagnosis and Management of Gastric Premalignant Conditions (guideline, 2025)

## Cluster `aiha` (topics 214, 215) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Warm AIHA is treated first with glucocorticoids, and rituximab is an established add-on with durable remission in many studies, although randomised evidence is sparse and graded very uncertain by Cochrane. Secondary causes, especially SLE and antiphospholipid antibodies, need excluding.

**Teaching points**
- A Cochrane review found very uncertain evidence on adding rituximab to glucocorticoids for partial haematological response at 12 months (one study, GRADE very low certainty). [PMID 33786812]
- A systematic review and meta-analysis of rituximab long-term outcomes reported prolonged remission benefits with a good safety profile, while calling for detailed randomised trials. [PMID 40510077]
- A Japanese guideline-based review notes cold agglutinin disease treatment with a complement-targeting agent is effective against haemolysis but not against agglutination symptoms, and elderly patients with AIHA should be assessed for malignancy. [PMID 39358288]
- Nearly half of warm AIHA cases are secondary (mean 49%), most often to systemic lupus erythematosus. [PMID 33802848]
- In SLE, antiphospholipid antibodies are associated with haemolytic anaemia (pooled OR 2.83), highest for lupus anticoagulant and anti-beta2-glycoprotein I antibodies. [PMID 34371159]
- Therapeutic plasma exchange improved short-term haematological parameters in acute AIHA onset, with unclear bias risk and no separate cold or warm data. [PMID 32384203]
- Mixed AIHA is refractory, often needing multiple lines including rituximab and cytotoxic agents. [PMID 40392014]
- AIHA can develop or worsen after mRNA COVID-19 vaccination in rare cases. [PMID 36208056]

**Uncertainties**
- Optimal timing of rituximab (first-line versus steroid-refractory).

**MCQ angles**
- Steroids first, then rituximab in refractory or relapsed disease.
- Underlying SLE or lymphoproliferative disease screening.

**Case hooks**
- Older patient with anaemia, high LDH and positive direct antiglobulin test.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Diagnostic workup (DAT, haptoglobin), steroid dosing and taper, transfusion rules, splenectomy and second-line options are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts. The phase III trial of steroid monotherapy versus steroid plus rituximab (the topic's original citation) was not part of this evidence set.

**Sources**
- PMID 32384203: Efficacy of therapeutic plasma exchange for treatment of autoimmune hemolytic anemia: A systematic review and meta-analysis of randomized controlled trials (meta-analysis, 2020)
- PMID 33786812: Disease-modifying treatments for primary autoimmune haemolytic anaemia (meta-analysis, 2021)
- PMID 33802848: Epidemiology of Secondary Warm Autoimmune Haemolytic Anaemia-A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis (meta-analysis, 2021)
- PMID 34371159: Antiphospholipid antibodies and the risk of autoimmune hemolytic anemia in patients with systemic lupus erythematosus: A systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2022)
- PMID 36208056: Development and exacerbation of autoimmune hemolytic anemia following COVID-19 vaccination: A systematic review (systematic review, 2023)
- PMID 39358288: [Current recommendations for the diagnosis and treatment of autoimmune hemolytic anemia based on etiology and pathology] (guideline, 2024)
- PMID 40392014: Mixed Autoimmune Hemolytic Anemia: A Systematic Review of Epidemiology, Clinical Characteristics, Therapies, and Outcomes (systematic review, 2025)
- PMID 40510077: Long-Term Outcomes of Rituximab Therapy in Autoimmune Hemolytic Anemia: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis (meta-analysis, 2025)

## Cluster `aih` (topics 216) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** The BSG 2025 guideline guides diagnosis and management of AIH; histological criteria from the International AIH Pathology Group improve diagnostic accuracy, and agreed response criteria (complete biochemical response, insufficient response, non-response, remission, intolerance) standardise reporting. Tacrolimus and MMF are used as second line without high-quality evidence.

**Teaching points**
- The British Society of Gastroenterology guideline informs diagnosis and management of AIH and its variant syndromes, focused on adults but relevant to older children. [PMID 40169244]
- The IAIHG defined five response criteria and endpoints for AIH: complete biochemical response, insufficient response, non-response, remission and intolerance to treatment. [PMID 35066089]
- Consensus histological criteria for AIH give a uniform approach, and their multicentre validation showed significantly improved sensitivity and specificity. [PMID 35230735, 38775078]
- Tacrolimus and mycophenolate mofetil are considered effective second-line treatments for non-responders or intolerant patients, but no high-quality evidence supports this. [PMID 33132643]
- In idiosyncratic DILI, systemic corticosteroids are controversial but may be given when DILI cannot be distinguished from AIH or presents with prominent autoimmune features. [PMID 33929376]
- Paediatric AIH guidelines from the Indian society standardise care using GRADE and structured consensus. [PMID 41264209]
- Autoimmune hepatitis is rare with similar incidence worldwide, and a higher prevalence in European and American than Asian populations and in the elderly. [PMID 31146297]

**Uncertainties**
- Evidence for second-line agents is low quality.

**MCQ angles**
- Response criteria in AIH.
- DILI versus AIH overlap.

**Case hooks**
- Woman with raised transaminases, high IgG and positive ANA.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Diagnostic scoring, first-line prednisolone and azathioprine regimens, treatment duration and withdrawal are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 31146297: Systematic review and meta-analysis on the incidence and prevalence of autoimmune hepatitis in Asian, European, and American population (meta-analysis, 2019)
- PMID 33132643: Tacrolimus and mycophenolate mofetil as second-line treatment in autoimmune hepatitis: Is the evidence of sufficient quality to develop recommendations? (guideline, 2020)
- PMID 33929376: ACG Clinical Guideline: Diagnosis and Management of Idiosyncratic Drug-Induced Liver Injury (guideline, 2021)
- PMID 35066089: Systematic review of response criteria and endpoints in autoimmune hepatitis by the International Autoimmune Hepatitis Group (systematic review, 2022)
- PMID 35230735: Consensus recommendations for histological criteria of autoimmune hepatitis from the International AIH Pathology Group: Results of a workshop on AIH histology hosted by the European Reference Network on Hepatological Diseases and the European Society of Pathology: Results of a workshop on AIH histology hosted by the European Reference Network on Hepatological Diseases and the European Society of Pathology (guideline, 2022)
- PMID 38775078: IAIH-PG consensus for histological criteria of AIH: Multicentre validation with focus on chronic liver diseases in China (guideline, 2024)
- PMID 40169244: British Society of Gastroenterology guidelines for diagnosis and management of autoimmune hepatitis (guideline, 2025)
- PMID 41264209: Autoimmune hepatitis in children: Guidelines of the Indian Society of Pediatric Gastroenterology, Hepatology, and Nutrition (ISPGHAN) (guideline, 2025)

## Cluster `aip` (topics 217) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** IgG4-related disease guidelines (UEG/SGF, Japanese consensus) direct autoimmune pancreatitis diagnosis and steroid treatment; if disease activity does not change within three months the diagnosis should be reconsidered. IgG4-RD carries higher cancer risk, especially pancreatic and lymphoma.

**Teaching points**
- European IgG4-related digestive disease recommendations advise that if there is no change in disease activity within 3 months of treatment the diagnosis should be reconsidered, and immunosuppressive drugs added if relapse occurs on treatment. [PMID 32552502]
- Japanese consensus guidelines for autoimmune pancreatitis were amended in 2020 using panel scoring of clinical statements. [PMID 35192048]
- IgG4-related sclerosing cholangitis guidelines give 14 diagnosis and 4 treatment clinical questions using modified Delphi methodology. [PMID 30575336]
- Patients with IgG4-related disease have higher overall cancer risk than the general population, especially pancreatic cancer and lymphoma. [PMID 34986892]
- Paediatric recommendations from INSPPIRE standardise diagnosis and management of autoimmune pancreatitis in childhood. [PMID 29746340]
- Guidelines on primary sclerosing cholangitis include recommendations to distinguish it from IgG4-related sclerosing cholangitis. [PMID 35359068]
- Chronic pancreatitis increases pancreatic cancer risk, rising with disease duration. [PMID 35142721]

**Uncertainties**
- Cancer surveillance in AIP is not covered by the retrieved excerpts.

**MCQ angles**
- Reconsider the diagnosis if no steroid response in 3 months.
- Distinguish IgG4-SC from PSC.

**Case hooks**
- Jaundiced older man with a sausage-shaped pancreas and raised IgG4.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Type 1 versus type 2 AIP, imaging criteria (ICDC), steroid dosing and maintenance rituximab are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 29746340: Recommendations for Diagnosis and Management of Autoimmune Pancreatitis in Childhood: Consensus From INSPPIRE (guideline, 2018)
- PMID 30575336: Clinical practice guidelines for IgG4-related sclerosing cholangitis (guideline, 2019)
- PMID 32552502: European Guideline on IgG4-related digestive disease - UEG and SGF evidence-based recommendations (guideline, 2020)
- PMID 34986892: The risk of malignancy in patients with IgG4-related disease: a systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2022)
- PMID 35142721: Chronic Pancreatitis Is a Risk Factor for Pancreatic Cancer, and Incidence Increases With Duration of Disease: A Systematic Review and Meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2022)
- PMID 35192048: Amendment of the Japanese consensus guidelines for autoimmune pancreatitis, 2020 (guideline, 2022)
- PMID 35359068: [Guidelines on the diagnosis and management of primary sclerosing cholangitis (2021)] (guideline, 2022)

## Cluster `aps12` (topics 218) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** APECED (APS-1) arises from AIRE mutations and affects many organs, including the kidneys; APS-2 combines autoimmune thyroid disease and Addison's disease. Hypoparathyroidism guidance supports follow-up, and anti-type I interferon autoantibodies are a hallmark of APECED.

**Teaching points**
- A systematic review of 938 patients with APECED describes clinical, immunological and genetic features, with most conditions appearing gradually over time. [PMID 33957837]
- Renal complications in APECED are significant and should be monitored to preserve kidney function. [PMID 40000975]
- AIRE is a key regulator of immune tolerance, with new roles beyond thymic selection of regulatory T cells. [PMID 26972725, 30565240]
- Type 3 autoimmune polyglandular syndrome is associated with Hashimoto thyroid antibodies, and 21-hydroxylase antibodies in Addison's disease can develop with APS-2. [PMID 33807517]
- A review of hypoparathyroidism guidelines covers evaluation, treatment, follow-up and special populations including acute hypocalcaemia and pregnancy. [PMID 34533015]
- At least 100 million people worldwide carry autoantibodies neutralising type I interferons. [PMID 38193358]

**Uncertainties**
- Disease management specifics for APS-1 are not covered by the retrieved excerpts.

**MCQ angles**
- AIRE gene and APECED.
- APS-2 components.

**Case hooks**
- Child with chronic mucocutaneous candidiasis and hypocalcaemia.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Diagnostic triad (candidiasis, hypoparathyroidism, adrenal insufficiency), screening schedule and treatment are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

## Cluster `autoinflammatory` (topics 219, 483) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** The 2021 EULAR/ACR points to consider guide diagnosis, treatment and monitoring of CAPS, TRAPS, MKD and DIRA; IL-1 inhibition is the cornerstone. VEXAS now has first formal ACR guidance.

**Teaching points**
- The 2021 EULAR/ACR points to consider guide diagnostic evaluation, treatment and monitoring of CAPS, TRAPS, MKD and DIRA to standardise care and improve outcomes. [PMID 35623638]
- Consensus protocols for hereditary autoinflammatory syndromes (CAPS, TRAPS and others) offer a path to rapid evaluation and effective disease control, decreasing variation in care. [PMID 32066461]
- The ACR VEXAS guidance statement is the first formal international consensus on diagnosis and management of VEXAS. [PMID 40787890]
- A systematic review of VEXAS syndrome describes clinical characteristics, disease trajectories and management, mostly in middle-aged men, with treatment optimisation needed. [PMID 38129348]
- Generalised pustular psoriasis has a Delphi consensus on diagnosis, treatment goals and maintenance criteria. [PMID 41371399]

**Uncertainties**
- Optimal VEXAS treatment.

**MCQ angles**
- IL-1 blockade in CAPS and TRAPS.
- VEXAS in older men.

**Case hooks**
- Child with recurrent fever, urticarial rash and deafness.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Genotype-phenotype features, anakinra and canakinumab dosing, colchicine use and amyloid screening are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

## Cluster `autonomic_cv` (topics 220) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Orthostatic hypotension is common in Parkinson disease (33.1%) and frailty and drives falls, yet intensive blood pressure treatment still reduces cardiovascular events regardless of orthostatic hypotension. Neurogenic OH shows exaggerated cerebral blood flow falls, and supine hypertension is highly prevalent in alpha-synucleinopathies.

**Teaching points**
- In hypertension trials, intensive therapy reduced cardiovascular disease or all-cause mortality regardless of orthostatic hypotension, without evidence of different effects in those with standing hypotension. [PMID 37847274]
- The prevalence of orthostatic hypotension in Parkinson's disease is 33.1%, more in older patients with longer duration and more severe motor symptoms. [PMID 40715652]
- Supine hypertension is highly prevalent across alpha-synucleinopathies, varying by disease type and presence of OH. [PMID 40406920]
- Neurogenic orthostatic hypotension shows significant orthostatic reductions in cerebral blood flow velocity, greater in symptomatic patients. [PMID 39569517]
- Frail older adults are more likely to have autonomic dysfunction, most strongly initial orthostatic hypotension. [PMID 37028604]
- Cardiovascular disorders are associated with falls among older adults, informing the World Guidelines for falls. [PMID 37738307]
- Sleep deprivation affects heart rate variability, and migraine is associated with altered autonomic function testing. [PMID 40895095, 38600467]

**Uncertainties**
- POTS-specific management is not covered by the retrieved excerpts.

**MCQ angles**
- Orthostatic hypotension and falls.
- Supine hypertension in synucleinopathies.

**Case hooks**
- Older patient with Parkinson disease and dizziness on standing.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Diagnostic thresholds (20/10 mmHg drop), POTS criteria, non-pharmacological measures and drugs (midodrine, fludrocortisone) are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 37028604: Frail older adults are more likely to have autonomic dysfunction: A systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2023)
- PMID 37738307: Cardiovascular Disorders and Falls Among Older Adults: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis (meta-analysis, 2024)
- PMID 37847274: Orthostatic Hypotension, Hypertension Treatment, and Cardiovascular Disease: An Individual Participant Meta-Analysis (meta-analysis, 2023)
- PMID 38600467: Reconceptualizing autonomic function testing in migraine: a systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2024)
- PMID 39569517: Cerebral Blood Flow Dynamics in Neurogenic Orthostatic Hypotension: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis (meta-analysis, 2025)
- PMID 40406920: Prevalence of Supine Hypertension in Alpha-Synucleinopathies: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis (meta-analysis, 2025)
- PMID 40715652: Prevalence and impact of orthostatic hypotension in Parkinson's disease: a systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2025)
- PMID 40895095: Effects of sleep deprivation on heart rate variability: a systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2025)

## Cluster `adpkd` (topics 221) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** KDIGO 2025 provides the current ADPKD guideline, and tolvaptan selection follows an updated consensus algorithm for rapid progression. mTOR inhibitors and somatostatin analogues are not recommended, statins have consensus support, and nephrectomy is a shared decision after multidisciplinary consultation.

**Teaching points**
- The KDIGO 2025 ADPKD guideline provides evaluation, management and treatment recommendations for providers, people with ADPKD and stakeholders, including knowledge gaps and policy implications. [PMID 39848746]
- A consensus update gives an algorithm to guide patient selection for tolvaptan with practical advice for its use, incorporating new RCT and post hoc evidence. [PMID 35134221]
- An international consensus reached agreement on statins in ADPKD, while mTOR inhibitors and somatostatin analogues are not recommended, and children should be encouraged to adopt low dietary salt. [PMID 31118499]
- A consensus statement says deciding on nephrectomy and alternatives such as kidney embolisation should be shared decision-making after multidisciplinary consultation. [PMID 39848914]
- UK guidance for children at risk of ADPKD says testing method is a shared decision and blood pressure should be assessed regularly. [PMID 31039757]
- A Spanish consensus covers cranial aneurysms, CKD and kidney replacement therapy, children, and assessing rapid progression in ADPKD. [PMID 36404270]
- Reviews outline individualised care emphasising monitoring of disease progression and shared decision-making in ADPKD. [PMID 38462235, 32523956]

**Uncertainties**
- Role of mTOR inhibitors remains limited.

**MCQ angles**
- Tolvaptan selection for rapid progression.
- No role for somatostatin analogues or mTOR inhibitors.

**Case hooks**
- 40-year-old with bilateral large kidneys and a family history of ADPKD.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Mayo imaging classification, total kidney volume, blood pressure targets and tolvaptan monitoring (liver tests) are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 31039757: Clinical practice guideline monitoring children and young people with, or at risk of developing autosomal dominant polycystic kidney disease (ADPKD) (guideline, 2019)
- PMID 31118499: International consensus statement on the diagnosis and management of autosomal dominant polycystic kidney disease in children and young people (guideline, 2019)
- PMID 32523956: Executive Summary: Clinical Practice Guideline for Autosomal Dominant Polycystic Kidney Disease in China (guideline, 2020)
- PMID 35134221: An update on the use of tolvaptan for autosomal dominant polycystic kidney disease: consensus statement on behalf of the ERA Working Group on Inherited Kidney Disorders, the European Rare Kidney Disease Reference Network and Polycystic Kidney Disease International (guideline, 2022)
- PMID 36404270: Consensus document on autosomal dominant polycystic kindey disease from the Spanish Working Group on Inherited Kindey Diseases. Review 2020 (guideline, 2022)
- PMID 38462235: The screening, diagnosis, and management of patients with autosomal dominant polycystic kidney disease: A national consensus statement from Taiwan (guideline, 2024)

- PMID 39848914: Nephrectomy in autosomal dominant polycystic kidney disease: a consensus statement of the ERA Genes & Kidney Working Group (guideline, 2025)

## Cluster `avn` (topics 222) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** The 2026 international ARCO-based guideline addresses nontraumatic osteonecrosis of the femoral head, including staging and joint-preserving versus arthroplasty decisions. Evidence for non-operative and early joint-preserving interventions is limited and observational.

**Teaching points**
- An international evidence-based guideline on nontraumatic osteonecrosis of the femoral head supports reducing practice variation, improving diagnostic accuracy and optimising treatment pathways, including identifying patients who benefit from joint-preserving interventions. [PMID 41961916, 41967526]
- A systematic review and meta-analysis found evidence for non-operative modalities and early joint-preserving interventions in ONFH is limited and mostly observational. [PMID 41892822]
- The 2026 ARCO recommendations provide standardised staging and hip-preserving treatment guidance, and decisions between joint preservation and arthroplasty are individualised. [PMID 42896984]
- ACR Appropriateness Criteria address imaging of acute hip pain. [PMID 40409883]
- Antiresorptive therapy for osteoporosis does not increase dental implant failure risk, so implants may be safely placed with bisphosphonates or denosumab. [PMID 40335186]
- Myeloma bone disease guidance addresses anti-resorptive therapy and strategies to enhance osteoblastic activity. [PMID 41082262]

**Uncertainties**
- Comparative studies of joint-preserving options are lacking.

**MCQ angles**
- ARCO staging.
- Hip-preserving surgery versus arthroplasty.

**Case hooks**
- Young adult on long-term steroids with groin pain and normal radiograph.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Risk factors (steroids, alcohol), MRI diagnosis and core decompression or bisphosphonate evidence are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**

## Cluster `bariatric` (topics 226, 227, 228, 229, 230) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Metabolic bariatric surgery is the benchmark for sustained weight and metabolic outcomes versus GLP-1 receptor agonists, which are useful adjuncts after surgery. Joint British guidance addresses diabetes around surgery, and regional blocks such as ESP reduce opioid use.

**Teaching points**
- Surgery is the most effective intervention for severe obesity and remains the benchmark for sustained metabolic and weight outcomes compared with GLP-1 receptor agonists, which are valuable non-invasive or adjunctive options. [PMID 41826529]
- GLP-1 receptor agonists after bariatric surgery give significant weight loss and cardiometabolic improvements versus placebo or lifestyle. [PMID 41071360]
- A Joint British guideline recommends specialist diabetes follow-up and inpatient diabetes care for people undergoing metabolic-bariatric surgery. [PMID 41840846]
- Ring augmentation in metabolic bariatric surgery is cautiously supported for selective use, particularly in Roux-en-Y gastric bypass, with low-quality evidence. [PMID 41770436]
- Erector spinae plane block reduces opioid consumption and middle and late postoperative pain after bariatric surgery compared with TAP block. [PMID 41417185]
- VA/DoD 2025 obesity guidance addresses GLP-1 agonists, endoscopic therapies and metabolic and bariatric surgery with stigma-informed, longitudinal care. [PMID 42636450]
- AI can enhance safety and efficiency in metabolic and bariatric surgery if clinician-led and rigorously validated. [PMID 42869677]

**Uncertainties**
- Long-term evidence for ring augmentation.

**MCQ angles**
- Surgery versus GLP-1 agonists.
- Perioperative diabetes management.

**Case hooks**
- Patient with BMI 42 and type 2 diabetes choosing between surgery and drugs.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Procedure comparison (sleeve versus bypass), eligibility criteria, complications (leak, internal hernia, dumping) and micronutrient follow-up are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 41071360: GLP-1 receptor agonists as an adjunct to bariatric surgery for weight loss and metabolic outcome improvement: a systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2025)
- PMID 41417185: Erector Spinae Plane Block Versus Transversus Abdominis Plane Block for Postoperative Analgesia in Bariatric Surgery: A Systematic Review and Network meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2026)
- PMID 41770436: Ring-augmented Metabolic Bariatric Surgery Procedures GRADE-based International Federation for the Surgery of Obesity and Metabolic Disorders (IFSO) Position Statement (guideline, 2026)
- PMID 41826529: Metabolic Bariatric Surgery versus GLP-1 Receptor Agonists for Obesity Management: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis (meta-analysis, 2026)
- PMID 41840846: Diabetes management in people undergoing metabolic-bariatric surgery: A guideline from the Joint British Diabetes Societies for Inpatient Care (JBDS-IP) Group (guideline, 2026)
- PMID 42636450: Adult Overweight and Obesity Management: Updates From the 2025 U.S. Department of Veterans Affairs and U.S. Department of Defense Clinical Practice Guidelines (guideline, 2026)

## Cluster `barrett` (topics 231, 232) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** ESGE guidance sets endoscopic surveillance and management of Barrett oesophagus, with post-treatment surveillance schedules after eradication. A dedicated Barrett's service improves dysplasia detection and guideline adherence; antireflux surgery selection depends on proven reflux.

**Teaching points**
- ESGE guidance recommends post-treatment surveillance at defined intervals (for baseline low-grade dysplasia at 1, 3 and 5 years after last treatment, after which surveillance may stop). [PMID 37813356]
- A dedicated Barrett's surveillance service showed higher dysplasia detection rate and guideline adherence than standard care, pending randomised trials. [PMID 38487558]
- The prevalence of Barrett's oesophagus in individuals with reflux varies by country and is higher in men. [PMID 32732370]
- Asia-Pacific consensus indicates endoscopic therapy for confirmed dysplasia and early cancer in Barrett's oesophagus. [PMID 27261337]
- The ICARUS guidelines give key recommendations for selecting patients for antireflux surgery, mandating objective testing if endoscopy lacks unequivocal oesophagitis. [PMID 31375601]
- A PPI deprescribing guideline gives practical advice for reducing or stopping PPIs, assisting rather than dictating decisions. [PMID 28500192]
- A Kyoto consensus defines the oesophagogastric junction, Barrett's oesophagus and the junction zone to improve classification. [PMID 35725291]

**Uncertainties**
- Risk prediction for progression to adenocarcinoma.

**MCQ angles**
- Endoscopic eradication for dysplasia.
- Dedicated surveillance services.

**Case hooks**
- Long-standing reflux with a 4 cm Barrett segment and low-grade dysplasia.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Prague criteria, surveillance intervals without dysplasia, radiofrequency ablation and PPI chemoprevention are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 27261337: Asia-Pacific consensus on the management of gastro-oesophageal reflux disease: an update focusing on refractory reflux disease and Barrett's oesophagus (guideline, 2016)
- PMID 28500192: Deprescribing proton pump inhibitors: Evidence-based clinical practice guideline (guideline, 2017)
- PMID 31375601: How to select patients for antireflux surgery? The ICARUS guidelines (international consensus regarding preoperative examinations and clinical characteristics assessment to select adult patients for antireflux surgery) (guideline, 2019)
- PMID 32732370: Global prevalence of Barrett's oesophagus and oesophageal cancer in individuals with gastro-oesophageal reflux: a systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2021)
- PMID 35725291: Kyoto international consensus report on anatomy, pathophysiology and clinical significance of the gastro-oesophageal junction (guideline, 2022)
- PMID 37813356: Diagnosis and management of Barrett esophagus: European Society of Gastrointestinal Endoscopy (ESGE) Guideline (guideline, 2023)
- PMID 38487558: Dedicated service for Barrett's oesophagus surveillance endoscopy yields higher dysplasia detection and guideline adherence in a non-tertiary setting in the UK: a 5-year comparative cohort study (guideline, 2024)

## Cluster `bcc` (topics 233) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Surgical removal remains first choice for basal cell carcinoma; hedgehog inhibitors and, in second line, anti-PD1 therapy exist for advanced disease. Gorlin syndrome needs regular skin examinations and high-risk BCC needs long-term follow-up.

**Teaching points**
- The German S2k guideline says surgical removal remains first choice in most cases and anti-PD1 inhibitors are a second-line option after hedgehog inhibitors for locally advanced and metastatic BCC. [PMID 39584658]
- The European consensus guideline recommends regular skin examinations in Gorlin patients and long-term follow-up in high-risk BCC, multiple BCCs and Gorlin syndrome. [PMID 37604067]
- Hedgehog inhibitors in BCC have high discontinuation rates, so patient expectations should be managed. [PMID 36795228]
- Optical coherence tomography techniques offer the highest accuracy for noninvasive BCC subtyping, outperforming dermoscopy. [PMID 40689050]
- Oral nicotinamide has insufficient evidence to reduce keratinocyte cancers significantly. [PMID 38201930]
- Japanese guidelines address surgical resection, radiotherapy, topical treatment and systemic therapy for BCC. [PMID 38264942]
- The incidence of cancer in patients on GLP-1 receptor agonists is low in randomised trials. [PMID 38701500]

**Uncertainties**
- Role of non-surgical options in low-risk disease.

**MCQ angles**
- Surgery first for BCC.
- Hedgehog inhibitor tolerability.

**Case hooks**
- Pearly nodule on the nose of an older man.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** High-risk features, Mohs surgery criteria, topical therapies (imiquimod) and radiotherapy are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 36795228: Efficacy and Safety of Sonic Hedgehog Inhibitors in Basal Cell Carcinomas: An Updated Systematic Review and Meta-analysis (2009-2022) (meta-analysis, 2023)
- PMID 37604067: European consensus-based interdisciplinary guideline for diagnosis and treatment of basal cell carcinoma-update 2023 (guideline, 2023)
- PMID 38201930: The Role of Nicotinamide as Chemo-Preventive Agent in NMSCs: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis (meta-analysis, 2023)
- PMID 38264942: Japanese Dermatological Association guidelines: Outlines of Japanese clinical guidelines for basal cell carcinoma 2021 (guideline, 2024)
- PMID 38701500: Association of glucagon-like peptide-1 receptor agonists with risk of cancers-evidence from a drug target Mendelian randomization and clinical trials (meta-analysis, 2024)
- PMID 39584658: S2k guideline basal cell carcinoma of the skin (update 2023) (guideline, 2024)
- PMID 40689050: FDA and EMA-approved noninvasive imaging techniques for basal cell carcinoma subtyping: A systematic review (systematic review, 2025)

## Cluster `bcp` (topics 234, 292) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** EULAR 2023 recommendations cover imaging in crystal-induced arthropathies, including calcium pyrophosphate and basic calcium phosphate. Cartilage calcification in osteoarthritis is linked to calcium crystal deposition.

**Teaching points**
- The 2023 EULAR recommendations are the first covering major crystal-induced arthropathies and guide use of common imaging modalities in clinical practice. [PMID 38320811]
- A systematic review confirmed a relevant and increasing role of imaging in crystal-induced arthropathies, including a potential role of dual-energy CT. [PMID 38702175]
- An international consensus defined characteristic imaging features of CPPD on conventional radiography, CT and DECT. [PMID 35439343]
- Reviews describe the clinical manifestations and biological effects of CPP and BCP crystal deposition and current therapeutic approaches. [PMID 30190520]
- Cartilage calcification in osteoarthritis has mechanisms and clinical relevance, with therapeutic pathways proposed to decrease calcification. [PMID 36509917]
- Low serum alkaline phosphatase warrants a diagnostic approach, as many patients are normal after sequencing ALPL exons. [PMID 36348061]

**Uncertainties**
- Treatment of BCP deposition disease is not covered by the retrieved excerpts.

**MCQ angles**
- Imaging for crystal arthropathy.
- Calcific periarthritis presentation.

**Case hooks**
- Acute painful shoulder with calcific deposits on radiograph.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Presentation of acute calcific periarthritis, NSAID, steroid and needling treatments and Milwaukee shoulder are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**

## Cluster `bpsd` (topics 235) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Guidelines for BPSD vary between statements, and non-pharmacological and person-centred approaches are favoured for emotional symptoms and sleep, with atypical antipsychotics reserved for severe symptoms because of a trade-off between benefit and harm. Reminiscence therapy and home-based activity help.

**Teaching points**
- A systematic review of BPSD guidelines found variability in recommendation statements and insufficient consideration of applicability, which may hamper implementation. [PMID 38640961]
- In a guideline review, non-pharmacological treatments may suit emotional symptoms and sleep disorders, with atypical antipsychotics a better choice for severe symptoms. [PMID 35693007]
- A network meta-analysis supports a trade-off between effectiveness and safety of atypical antipsychotics in dementia-related symptoms. [PMID 30901041]
- Person-centred care interventions improve BPSD and cognitive function in persons with dementia. [PMID 33326573]
- Dyadic psychological interventions should involve both the person with dementia and the caregiver, targeting anxiety, quality of life and carer burden. [PMID 31752633]
- Reminiscence therapy has a moderate effect on depression and can reduce reliance on antipsychotics. [PMID 30712519]
- Home-based physical activity programmes delay cognitive decline and improve BPSD, activities of daily living and carer burden. [PMID 31858111]

**Uncertainties**
- Applicability of guidelines across settings.

**MCQ angles**
- Non-drug measures first for BPSD.
- Antipsychotic benefit-harm trade-off.

**Case hooks**
- Care-home resident with dementia and evening agitation.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Antipsychotic mortality warning, drug choice, review and withdrawal, and investigating triggers are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 30712519: A systematic review and meta-analysis on the effect of reminiscence therapy for people with dementia (meta-analysis, 2019)
- PMID 30901041: Assessment of Reported Comparative Effectiveness and Safety of Atypical Antipsychotics in the Treatment of Behavioral and Psychological Symptoms of Dementia: A Network Meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2019)

## Cluster `behcet` (topics 236) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Behcet disease evidence retrieved is limited: adalimumab is safe and effective first-line for Behcet uveitis, HLA-B51 carriers have more ocular disease, and colchicine for recurrent oral ulcers is controversial. Haploinsufficiency of A20 can mimic familial Behcet disease.

**Teaching points**
- Adalimumab can be used safely and efficiently as first-line therapy in Behcet uveitis, with a corticosteroid-sparing effect and few adverse events. [PMID 36625549]
- Adalimumab is among the most effective treatments for uveitis in paediatric rheumatic diseases, though combinations of safe alternatives are preferred. [PMID 32019589]
- HLA-B51 carriers are susceptible to ocular symptoms of Behcet disease, with the association strengthening towards the east along the Silk Road. [PMID 26954704]
- The role of colchicine for recurrent oral ulcers remains controversial, with an RCT finding no difference from placebo. [PMID 32281694]
- Autosomal dominant familial Behcet-like disease and A20 haploinsufficiency respond to treatments like other autoinflammatory diseases, with IL-1 blockade a choice. [PMID 29890348]
- Fibromyalgia prevalence is as high as 80% among patients with Behcet syndrome. [PMID 28447207]

**Uncertainties**
- EULAR treatment algorithm is not in the retrieved excerpts.

**MCQ angles**
- Adalimumab for Behcet uveitis.
- HLA-B51 association.

**Case hooks**
- Young man from the Mediterranean with recurrent oral ulcers and uveitis.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Diagnostic criteria, pathergy test, vascular and neurological involvement and immunosuppressive treatment are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 26954704: HLA-B51 Carriers are Susceptible to Ocular Symptoms of Behçet Disease and the Association between the Two Becomes Stronger towards the East along the Silk Road: A Literature Survey (meta-analysis, 2017)
- PMID 28447207: Prevalence of fibromyalgia in general population and patients, a systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2017)
- PMID 29890348: Autosomic dominant familial Behçet disease and haploinsufficiency A20: A review of the literature (systematic review, 2018)
- PMID 32019589: Epidemiological and advanced therapeutic approaches to treatment of uveitis in pediatric rheumatic diseases: a systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2020)
- PMID 32281694: Value of colchicine as treatment for recurrent oral ulcers: A systematic review (meta-analysis, 2020)
- PMID 36625549: Efficacy and Safety of Adalimumab in Patients with Behçet Uveitis: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis (meta-analysis, 2024)

## Cluster `belimumab` (topics 237, 238) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Belimumab plus standard therapy is more effective in lupus nephritis with favourable safety, and improves cutaneous lupus after about 20 weeks. EULAR 2025 and Brazilian consensus guide lupus nephritis, and anifrolumab features in rare SLE manifestations.

**Teaching points**
- Belimumab plus standard therapy was more effective and had favourable safety in lupus nephritis, with no excess treatment-related adverse events (RR 1.04). [PMID 37194710]
- Belimumab is effective for cutaneous lupus in SLE, with about 20 weeks needed to see a response and a sustained response through one year. [PMID 36358025]
- EULAR 2025 recommendations for SLE with kidney involvement give consensus management adjusted for severity, efficacy, safety, cost and drug availability. [PMID 41107121]
- The Brazilian lupus nephritis consensus sets response targets such as proteinuria decrease milestones at 3 and 6 months. [PMID 38890752]
- A consensus on rare SLE manifestations includes combinations of hydroxychloroquine and glucocorticoids with anifrolumab or mycophenolate. [PMID 40418946]
- New lupus nephritis guidelines are reviewed to support individualised treatment decisions. [PMID 41082628]
- Natalizumab long-term therapy carries a higher risk of progressive multifocal leukoencephalopathy. [PMID 38321317]

**Uncertainties**
- Place of anifrolumab in lupus nephritis.

**MCQ angles**
- Belimumab add-on in lupus nephritis.
- Time to cutaneous response.

**Case hooks**
- SLE patient with active nephritis despite mycophenolate.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Mechanism details (BLyS and type I interferon), indications and infection screening for belimumab and anifrolumab are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**

## Cluster `bells_palsy` (topics 239) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Guidelines for Bell's palsy differ in quality, and Japanese guidance notes that treatment benefit varies with disease severity, with several options only weakly recommended. SARS-CoV-2 infection carries more risk of Bell palsy than vaccination.

**Teaching points**
- Japanese guidelines (2023 update) weakly recommend some treatments due to insufficient evidence, and absolute risk reduction differs by disease severity, so treatment should be decided with severity in mind. [PMID 39079445]
- A systematic appraisal found Bell's palsy guidelines to have low-to-average quality, with rigour of development, stakeholder involvement and applicability most in need of improvement. [PMID 33389026]
- Reviews of acute peripheral facial palsy note changes in grading systems and treatments from surgery to rehabilitation over the last decade. [PMID 32743989]
- The Spanish otolaryngology guideline on facial paralysis covers 56 topics related to the facial nerve. [PMID 31097197]
- SARS-CoV-2 infection poses a significantly greater risk of Bell palsy than SARS-CoV-2 vaccination, with no difference between vaccines. [PMID 37103913]
- Acupuncture is beneficial in some peripheral neuropathies but better-designed sham-controlled studies are needed. [PMID 28112552]

**Uncertainties**
- Corticosteroid timing and antiviral value are not summarised in the retrieved excerpts.

**MCQ angles**
- Vaccine versus infection risk.
- Guideline quality.

**Case hooks**
- Sudden unilateral facial weakness with forehead involvement.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Steroid dose and timing, antivirals, eye protection, prognosis and recovery rates are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 28112552: Acupuncture for the Treatment of Peripheral Neuropathy: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis (meta-analysis, 2017)
- PMID 31097197: Facial paralysis: Clinical practice guideline of the Spanish Society of Otolaryngology (guideline, 2020)
- PMID 32743989: Acute Peripheral Facial Palsy: Recent Guidelines and a Systematic Review of the Literature (guideline, 2020)

## Cluster `bempedoic` (topics 240) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Bempedoic acid reduces major adverse cardiovascular events, particularly non-fatal MI and revascularisation, with increased gout risk, but no effect on stroke or all-cause mortality. Guidelines place it among add-on lipid-lowering options with PCSK9 inhibitors.

**Teaching points**
- Bempedoic acid reduced MACE (OR 0.84) and non-fatal MI versus placebo, but not stroke or all-cause mortality. [PMID 37463824]
- A systematic review of bempedoic acid found improved cholesterol control translating to reduced MACE and revascularisation, with satisfactory safety except for increased gout. [PMID 38017541]
- The AACE 2025 guideline offers treatment options for adults with dyslipidaemia and ASCVD needing additional lipid-lowering drugs, with shared decision-making. [PMID 39919851]
- Recommendations on optimal use of lipid-lowering therapy in established ASCVD give practical advice accounting for patient-centred care and drug availability. [PMID 39497020]
- In predominantly primary prevention participants, each 1 mmol/L LDL-C reduction was associated with a 30% relative reduction in 4-point MACE. [PMID 41775617]
- A consensus document summarises novel hypercholesterolaemia agents including PCSK9 inhibitors and bempedoic acid in atherosclerotic disease. [PMID 39998741]
- The NLA update on familial hypercholesterolaemia gives strategies to improve identification and care. [PMID 41741298]

**Uncertainties**
- Long-term outcomes beyond CLEAR Outcomes.

**MCQ angles**
- Bempedoic acid in statin intolerance.
- Gout as adverse effect.

**Case hooks**
- Statin-intolerant patient post MI with LDL above target.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** CLEAR Outcomes numbers, mechanism (ACL inhibition), tendon rupture risk and LDL targets are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 37463824: Cardiovascular events in patients treated with bempedoic acid vs. placebo: systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2023)
- PMID 38017541: Safety and efficacy of bempedoic acid: a systematic review and meta-analysis of randomised controlled trials (meta-analysis, 2023)
- PMID 39497020: 2024 Recommendations on the Optimal Use of Lipid-Lowering Therapy in Established Atherosclerotic Cardiovascular Disease and Following Acute Coronary Syndromes: A Position Paper of the International Lipid Expert Panel (ILEP) (guideline, 2024)

## Cluster `bppv` (topics 241) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** BPPV is diagnosed by positional testing and treated with repositioning manoeuvres such as the Epley, which improves symptoms of posterior canal BPPV; vestibular suppressants should be avoided. Recurrence and residual dizziness have identifiable risk factors.

**Teaching points**
- The Epley manoeuvre improves symptoms of posterior canal BPPV, and emergency clinicians should be familiar with performing it. [PMID 37186435]
- Primary care shows gaps in guideline adherence, with a need to increase diagnostic and treatment manoeuvres and decrease vestibular-suppressant medications. [PMID 36884006]
- The AAO-HNS clinical practice guideline update aims to increase appropriate therapeutic repositioning manoeuvres. [PMID 28248602]
- The Bárány Society consensus defines diagnostic criteria for BPPV including established and emerging syndromes. [PMID 29056234]
- Risk factors for BPPV recurrence were identified, supporting counselling about follow-up after diagnosis. [PMID 32839838, 32776833]
- Residual dizziness after successful repositioning has multiple risk factors, with psychological and physical contributors. [PMID 35218384]
- Otoneurology practice guidelines include tests and manoeuvres supported by evidence, differential diagnosis and management. [PMID 28826856]

**Uncertainties**
- Recurrence prevention strategies.

**MCQ angles**
- Epley for posterior canal BPPV.
- Avoid vestibular suppressants.

**Case hooks**
- Brief spinning on turning in bed with a positive Dix-Hallpike.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Dix-Hallpike technique details, horizontal canal manoeuvres and red-flag central causes are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 28248602: Clinical Practice Guideline: Benign Paroxysmal Positional Vertigo (Update) Executive Summary (guideline, 2017)
- PMID 28826856: Practice Guidelines for the Diagnosis and Management of Benign Paroxysmal Positional Vertigo Otoneurology Committee of Spanish Otorhinolaryngology and Head and Neck Surgery Consensus Document (guideline, 2018)
- PMID 29056234: Benign paroxysmal positional vertigo: Diagnostic criteria Consensus document of the Committee for the Classification of Vestibular Disorders of the Bárány Society (guideline, 2017)
- PMID 32776833: Risk Factors for the Recurrence of Benign Paroxysmal Positional Vertigo: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis (meta-analysis, 2022)

## Cluster `benign_pleural` (topics 242) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** The joint ERS/EACTS/ESTS guidelines give 24 recommendations for spontaneous pneumothorax, with evidence gaps in prolonged air leak and recurrence prevention. For tension pneumothorax, needle length and decompression site are debated, and prehospital ultrasound may help.

**Teaching points**
- The joint ERS/EACTS/ESTS guidelines make 24 evidence-based recommendations for spontaneous pneumothorax and highlight gaps in managing persistent air leak and preventing recurrence. [PMID 38804185, 38806203]
- A meta-analysis of optimal needle length and decompression site for tension pneumothorax urges individualised decisions given heterogeneity and variable measurement techniques. [PMID 40383767]
- Some evidence suggests ultrasound should be considered to identify pneumothorax in the prehospital setting. [PMID 38308661]
- Deep learning shows promising sensitivity and specificity for detecting pneumothorax on chest radiographs. [PMID 38189265]
- Canadian imaging referral guidelines cover thoracic indications including pneumothorax and pleural effusion. [PMID 38099468]
- ACR criteria address radiologic management of pulmonary nodules and masses. [PMID 41721805]

**Uncertainties**
- Recurrence prevention strategies.

**MCQ angles**
- Pneumothorax management recommendations.
- Tension pneumothorax decompression.

**Case hooks**
- Tall young smoker with sudden pleuritic pain and breathlessness.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Conservative versus drainage selection, pleurodesis, pleural plaque and benign effusion causes are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Mentor intro.** AUA 2026 and EAU guidelines frame BPH management as shared decision-making balancing efficacy, durability and adverse events of medications and procedures, with minimally invasive options such as prostatic urethral lift and prostatic artery embolisation.

**Teaching points**
- AUA 2026 guidance states procedural management of LUTS/BPH involves a balance of efficacy, durability and procedure-specific adverse effects, with shared decision-making essential. [PMID 42095468]
- AUA evaluation guidance includes history, examination, appropriate tests and counselling for initial management of LUTS/BPH. [PMID 42095481]
- AUA medical guidance takes into account efficacy, durability and adverse events, and unique actions of medication in populations such as those on active surveillance for prostate cancer. [PMID 42095477]
- Practical guidance on benign prostatic obstruction describes factors to consider when choosing treatment through shared decisions. [PMID 40664548]
- The 2026 EAU guidelines on non-neurogenic male LUTS give international recommendations for diagnosis and management. [PMID 41997777]
- A Spanish consensus supports prostatic urethral lift as an effective minimally invasive treatment for LUTS/BPH. [PMID 39321994]
- SIR 2026 guidance covers prostatic artery embolisation. [PMID 42223385]

**Uncertainties**
- Terminology differences across guidelines.

- Shared decision-making in BPH.
- Minimally invasive options.

- 70-year-old with nocturia and weak stream wanting to avoid sexual side effects.

## Cluster `benzo_deprescribing` (topics 244) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Deprescribing benzodiazepines and Z-drugs is feasible with structured support and may be done by pharmacists, though evidence quality is low. Withdrawal rates vary by intervention type, psychotherapy and tapering support help, and inpatient withdrawal needs an individualised taper.

**Teaching points**
- Deprescribing benzodiazepines and Z-drugs is feasible depending on the process and support employed, with very low to low quality evidence of effectiveness (intervention versus routine care RR 3.26). [PMID 35815384]
- Withdrawal of benzodiazepines and hypnotics is feasible in older people, though withdrawal rates vary by intervention type and sustainability is unclear. [PMID 28456823]
- Pharmacists may contribute to deprescribing benzodiazepines in older outpatients. [PMID 37713028]
- A review of interventions to improve tapering success in older adults notes psychotherapy costs as an issue and calls for research on pharmaceutical adjuvants. [PMID 29336611]
- A narrative review proposes a systematic, individualised taper for inpatient benzodiazepine withdrawal, especially in complex patients with co-occurring disorders and protracted symptoms. [PMID 42294745]
- After catatonia has been fully treated and maintenance lorazepam established, lorazepam should be discontinued in line with benzodiazepine discontinuation guidelines to minimise catatonia re-emergence. [PMID 40544922]

**Uncertainties**
- Optimal taper schedule is not in the retrieved excerpts.

**MCQ angles**
- Feasibility of deprescribing in older people.
- Role of pharmacist-led interventions.

**Case hooks**
- Older patient on nightly zopiclone for years wanting to stop.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Taper rates (for example 5-10% per 1-2 weeks), switching to diazepam, CBT-I and withdrawal symptoms are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 28456823: A systematic review of interventions to deprescribe benzodiazepines and other hypnotics among older people (systematic review, 2017)
- PMID 29336611: Interventions to improve benzodiazepine tapering success in the elderly: a systematic review (systematic review, 2019)
- PMID 35815384: Feasibility and effectiveness of deprescribing benzodiazepines and Z-drugs: systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2023)
- PMID 37713028: Pharmacists' contribution to benzodiazepine deprescribing in older outpatients: a systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2023)

- PMID 42294745: Inpatient Management of Benzodiazepine Withdrawal: A Narrative Review of a Systematic, Individualized Taper Design (review, 2026)

## Cluster `beryllium` (topics 245) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** Retrieved evidence is limited to reviews: chronic beryllium disease closely mimics sarcoidosis, so occupational exposure history is essential in granulomatous lung disease. Beryllium disease involves BAL CD4+ T cell trafficking, and occupational interstitial lung disease reviews stress surveillance and training.

**Teaching points**
- Chronic beryllium disease and drug-induced granulomatosis are conditions that often closely mimic pulmonary sarcoidosis. [PMID 37250639]
- Granuloma alone is a nonspecific histopathological finding, so a multidisciplinary approach is important for a confident diagnosis of granulomatous lung disease. [PMID 28794143]
- Adaptive immunity in sarcoidosis and chronic beryllium disease involves BAL CD4+ T cells trafficking to and expanding in the lung in response to common antigens. [PMID 32256501]
- Occupational interstitial lung disease review stresses surveillance, prevention, and training health professionals about exposures. [PMID 37535448]
- Pathology and mineralogy of pneumoconioses can be confirmed with special stains and electron microscopy where available. [PMID 36972614]

**Uncertainties**
- Beryllium-specific evidence is thin in retrieved sources.

**MCQ angles**
- Beryllium mimicking sarcoidosis.
- Granuloma nonspecific.

**Case hooks**
- Aerospace worker with granulomatous lung disease.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Beryllium lymphocyte proliferation test, sensitisation versus disease, steroid treatment and exposure control are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts; a dedicated chronic beryllium disease source is needed.

**Sources**
- PMID 28794143: Differential diagnosis of granulomatous lung disease: clues and pitfalls: Number 4 in the Series "Pathology for the clinician" Edited by Peter Dorfmüller and Alberto Cavazza (review, 2017)
- PMID 32256501: Adaptive Immunity in Pulmonary Sarcoidosis and Chronic Beryllium Disease (review, 2020)
- PMID 36972614: Pathology and Mineralogy of the Pneumoconioses (review, 2023)
- PMID 37250639: Differential diagnosis of pulmonary sarcoidosis: a review (review, 2023)
- PMID 37535448: Occupational interstitial lung diseases (review, 2023)

## Cluster `pe_test` (topics 246) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** In a haemodynamically stable patient the first step is a pre-test probability assessment, then D-dimer in non-high probability, then imaging. Age-adjusted D-dimer and the YEARS algorithm reduce imaging while staying safe.

**Teaching points**
- Original and simplified Wells rules combined with age-adjusted D-dimer have similar performance for ruling out PE, and the simplified Wells rule is preferred for ease of use. [PMID 28106338]
- Age-adjusted D-dimer cutoffs combined with low clinical probability rule out PE with fewer false positives than conventional D-dimer, across 47,720 patients with non-high clinical probability. [PMID 34814782]
- The YEARS algorithm had pooled sensitivity of 96% and specificity of 50%, with a risk ratio of 0.78 for advanced imaging use, suggesting it is effective and safe. [PMID 39634805]
- D-dimer interpretation depends on assay performance characteristics, and international guidelines differ in how they allow age-adjusted and pretest-adjusted thresholds. [PMID 38135252]
- In pregnancy, introducing the pregnancy-adapted YEARS guideline increased investigation for PE, and over 5 years there were no known missed cases among women assessed with D-dimer alone. [PMID 42283136]
- In cancer patients, D-dimer with clinical decision rules had failure rates similar to standard imaging, with the highest yield (26%) from revised Geneva with age-adjusted D-dimer and the YEARS algorithm. [PMID 41354153]
- In COVID-19, D-dimer of at least 1000 micrograms/L may help select patients for CT pulmonary angiography. [PMID 33966132]
- Double low-dose CT pulmonary angiography is feasible, with reduced radiation and contrast dose without compromising diagnostic image quality. [PMID 32008552]

**Uncertainties**
- Optimal D-dimer thresholds in special populations.

**MCQ angles**
- Pre-test probability before D-dimer.
- Age-adjusted D-dimer cutoff.

**Case hooks**
- Stable patient with pleuritic pain and low Wells score.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** PERC, the age-adjusted formula (age x 10 micrograms/L above 50), Wells and Geneva score components, V/Q scanning and CTPA pathways are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 28106338: The original and simplified Wells rules and age-adjusted D-dimer testing to rule out pulmonary embolism: an individual patient data meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2017)
- PMID 32008552: A Systematic Review of Double Low-dose CT Pulmonary Angiography in Pulmonary Embolism (systematic review, 2019)
- PMID 33966132: Pulmonary embolism in patients with COVID-19 and value of D-dimer assessment: a meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2021)
- PMID 34814782: Age-Adjusted D-Dimer in the Prediction of Pulmonary Embolism: Systematic Review and Meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2021)
- PMID 38135252: D-dimer Levels for the exclusion of pulmonary embolism: making sense of international guideline recommendations (guideline, 2024)
- PMID 39634805: Diagnostic Test Accuracy of the YEARS Algorithm for Pulmonary Embolism: A systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2024)
- PMID 41354153: Safety and efficiency of D-dimer testing in combination with clinical decision rules to exclude pulmonary embolism in patients with cancer: individual patient data meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2026)
- PMID 42283136: Suspected Pulmonary Embolism Exclusion With D-Dimers in Emergency Departments: Pregnancy (SPEED-ED Pregnancy). A Retrospective Evaluation of the Pregnancy-Adapted YEARS Guideline (guideline, 2026)

## Cluster `bb_hf` (topics 247) [draft_pending_clinician_review]

**Mentor intro.** In heart failure beta-blocker use is established, but in HFpEF observational data show mortality association without effect on rehospitalisation. Evidence for long-term beta-blockers after uncomplicated MI is weak, and heart rate remains high in many HFrEF patients despite titration.

**Teaching points**
- Beta-blocker therapy in HFpEF was associated with lower mortality in observational studies but did not affect rehospitalisation for heart failure or the composite with mortality, and large randomised trials are needed. [PMID 38184132]
- Prolonged beta-blocker treatment does not change peak VO2 but improves functional status in heart failure patients. [PMID 29206784]
- Higher heart rate was frequent in HFrEF even after aggressive titration and was associated with worse outcomes. [PMID 28728744]
- Oral beta-blockers for a year or more after MI do not reduce mortality in patients without heart failure in the reperfusion era. [PMID 35058412]
- In patients without heart failure or LV systolic dysfunction after ACS in the PCI era, beta-blocker therapy at discharge may still be associated with lower all-cause death. [PMID 36376209]
- The impact of beta-blocker maintenance on decompensated heart failure remains uncertain and needs more randomised trials. [PMID 39289936]
- A consensus statement from India recommends the place of bisoprolol in heart failure management. [PMID 38736057]

**Uncertainties**
- Benefit of beta-blockers in HFpEF.

**MCQ angles**
- Titration to target heart rate and dose.
- Uncertain value in HFpEF.

**Case hooks**
- HFrEF patient with heart rate 90 on a low bisoprolol dose.

**Gaps for the reviewer.** Evidence-based agents (carvedilol, bisoprolol, metoprolol succinate, nebivolol), starting doses, contraindications and the landmark trials are not stated in the retrieved abstract excerpts.

**Sources**
- PMID 28728744: Serial Heart Rates, Guideline-Directed Beta Blocker Use, and Outcomes in Patients With Chronic Heart Failure With Reduced Ejection Fraction (guideline, 2017)
- PMID 29206784: Effect of Beta-blocker Treatment on V˙O2peak in Patients with Heart Failure (meta-analysis, 2018)
- PMID 35058412: Long-Term Beta-Blocker Therapy After Myocardial Infarction Without Heart Failure in the Reperfusion Era-Systemic Review and Meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2022)
- PMID 36376209: Association of beta-blocker therapy at discharge with clinical outcomes in patients without heart failure or left ventricular systolic dysfunction after acute coronary syndrome: An updated systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2022)
- PMID 38184132: Beta-blocker therapy in heart failure with preserved ejection fraction (B-HFpEF): A systematic review and meta-analysis (meta-analysis, 2024)
- PMID 38736057: Role of Bisoprolol in Heart Failure Management: A Consensus Statement from India (guideline, 2023)
